Uniform Scaling Limits in AdamW-Trained Transformers
William Gibson, Christoph Reisinger

Abstract
We study the large-depth limit of transformers trained with AdamW, by modelling the hidden-state dynamics as an interacting particle system (IPS) coupled through the attention mechanism. Under appropriate scaling of the attention heads, we prove that the joint dynamics of the hidden states and backpropagated variables converge in L 2 L^{2} , uniformly over the initial condition, to the solution of a forward–backward system of ODEs at rate 𝒪 ( L − 1 + L − 1 / 3 H − 1 / 2 ) \mathcal{O}(L^{-1}+L^{-1/3}H^{-1/2}) . Here, L L and H H denote the depth and number of heads of the transformer, respectively. The limiting system of ODEs can be identified with a McKean–Vlasov ODE (MVODE) when the attention heads do not incorporate causal masking. By using the flow maps associated with this MVODE and applying concentration of measure techniques, we obtain bounds on the difference between the discrete and continuous models that are uniform over compact sets of initial conditions. As this is achieved without resorting to a covering argument, the constants in our bounds are independent of the number of tokens. Furthermore, under a suitable adaptation to AdamW, the bounds become independent of the token embedding dimension.



1
Introduction

The empirical scaling laws for Large Language Models
kaplan2020scalinglawsneurallanguage
drive an industry’s pursuit to increase the number of parameters and training data samples. Therefore, a theoretical understanding of how model design choices affect generalizability, robustness, and related properties is crucial for guiding best practices as models continue to scale. Furthermore, infinite depth and width limits of neural networks offer a more tractable setting for analysis, as demonstrated by the growing literature on the convergence of gradient descent to global optimality
barboni2025understandingtraininginfinitelydeep
;
chizat2018globalconvergencegradientdescent
;
isobe2024convergenceresultcontinuousmodel
. Transferring results from these limiting models to those used in practice requires convergence bounds to hold uniformly over the model’s input. Accordingly, this paper studies the uniform convergence of the unmasked transformer
NIPS2017_3f5ee243
as the depth
L
, the number of heads
H
tend to infinity. The main result is given in Theorem
7
, which demonstrates that the uniform convergence is independent of the number of tokens. This is surprising, as one expects uniform convergence bounds to scale with the effective dimension of the class over which the supremum is taken. Bounds of this form arise from covering arguments, such as Dudley’s Entropy Integral Theorem
Wainwright_2019
, and would therefore introduce dependency on the number of tokens.

1.1
Setup

The transformer neural network
NIPS2017_3f5ee243
is a ResNet formed from alternating multi-head attention (
1
) and multi-layer perceptron (MLP) layers. Each attention head consists of Query, Key, Value and Output matrices
Q_{r}^{h},K_{r}^{h},V_{r}^{h},O_{r}^{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, respectively, where
d
is the token embedding dimension,
k
is the head dimension, and
r,h
indicate the layer and head number. Given a matrix of token embeddings
\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}
whose
i^{th}
row is denoted
x^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the unmasked multi-head attention layer is defined by

\mathrm{MHA}_{r}(x,\mathcal{X}):=\sum_{h=1}^{H}(O^{h}_{r})^{T}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\exp(\beta\langle Q_{r}^{h}x,K_{r}^{h}x^{i}\rangle)\,V_{r}^{h}x^{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\exp(\beta\langle Q_{r}^{h}x,K_{r}^{h}x^{i}\rangle)},

(1)

where
\beta>0
is the inverse temperature introduced in
geshkovski2025mathematicalperspectivetransformers
, which controls the concentration of the attention matrix to individual tokens.
As seen in
geshkovski2024emergenceclustersselfattentiondynamics
;
furuya2024transformersuniversalincontextlearners
;
geshkovski2025mathematicalperspectivetransformers
;
rigollet2025meanfielddynamicstransformers
;
sander2022sinkformerstransformersdoublystochastic
, the attention mechanism is well-defined on compactly supported measures by replacing the summation over the tokens
x^{i}
in equation (
1
) with an integral against the empirical measure of tokens. We denote the corresponding function on measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by

\gamma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad\gamma(z,\mu)=\frac{\int\exp(\left\langle z,y\right\rangle)\,y\,d\mu(y)}{\int\exp(\left\langle z,y\right\rangle)\,d\mu(y)},

(2)

where the token embedding
x^{i}
and parameters
\beta K^{T}Q
have been absorbed into one variable

z=\beta K^{T}Qx
so that Lipschitz continuity with respect to parameters and token embeddings can be studied simultaneously. We write
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
for the parameters in a layer and head, split as
\theta=(\theta_{Q},\theta_{K},\theta_{V},\theta_{O})
with each
\theta_{\cdot}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
. Multi-head attention averages
\gamma
over the heads. Therefore, with parameters
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d})
, it is given by

\Gamma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d})\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad\Gamma(x,\mu,\nu)=\int\theta_{O}^{T}\theta_{V}\,\gamma\left(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}\,x,\mu\right)d\nu(\theta).

(3)

The embedding of the
i^{th}
token propagates from layer
r
to
r+1
in the attention-only transformer according to

x^{i}_{r+1}=x_{r}^{i}+\frac{1}{L}\Gamma\left(x^{i}_{r},m^{N}_{r},\underline{\nu}^{H}_{r}\right),

(4)

where
m^{N}_{r},\underline{\nu}^{H}_{r}
are the empirical measures

m^{N}_{r}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x^{i}_{r}},\qquad\underline{\nu}^{H}_{r}:=\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\delta_{(Q^{h}_{r},K^{h}_{r},V^{h}_{r},O^{h}_{r})}.

(5)

For simplicity, we restrict our analysis to the attention-only transformer without any normalisation layers, having depth-scaled the residual to ensure that the hidden states and backpropagated variables do not explode. In fact, incorporating a post-normalisation layer
xiong2020layernormalizationtransformerarchitecture
improves the bounds given in Theorem
7
. We use Grönwall’s Lemma to obtain exponential bounds on the norm of the hidden states. This exponential growth does not occur when normalisation layers are incorporated. The transformer given by (
4
) uses attention heads without causal masking. Therefore, our work is relevant to Vision Transformers
dosovitskiy2021imageworth16x16words
, Diffusion Transformers
peebles2023scalable
, and BERT-style language models
devlin2019bert
.

Note that the residual in the transformer given by (
4
) has been explicitly scaled by
(LH)^{-1}
, where the
H^{-1}
scaling is implicit to the integration against the empirical measure
\underline{\nu}^{L}
. Alternatively, transformers with
L^{-1/2}
-scaled residual have been studied in
agazzi2026stochastic
;
fedorov2026clustering
;
koubbi2026homogenized
and results in the hidden states converging to the solution of a system of Itô Stochastic Differential Equations (SDEs). However, these papers focus on the token clustering phenomena and do not consider the training dynamics. A complication that would arise when adapting the methodology in the present paper to the SDE scaling regime under training is that in the limit, the adjoint variable (see Section
3.1
) is expected to satisfy a backward SDE, whose solution consists of a pair of adapted processes. As in the classical setting of forward-backward stochastic differential equations (see
Ma2007
), the second process is required to ensure that the adjoint process is adapted to the same filtration as the forward process. A priori, it is unclear whether the adjoint variable of the transformer is adapted to the filtration generated by the parameter initialisation.
Extending our analysis to this regime is left for future research.

1.2
Related Work

The large depth limit for ResNets whose residual has been scaled by
L^{-1}
towards neural ODEs has previously been studied in
avelin2020neuralodesdeeplimit
;
marion2024implicit
. These works consider parameters that are either shared across layers
avelin2020neuralodesdeeplimit
or dependently initialised across layers
marion2024implicit
.
This does not reflect common practice where parameters are initialised
i.i.d.
from a (sub-)Gaussian distribution. However, there is no meaningful continuous-time analogue to
i.i.d.
parameter initialisation as we will elaborate in Section
3.2
.
Instead, we consider the simultaneous infinite-depth and infinite-width limit, which is well-posed due to the continuity of the distribution of parameters across layers. This simultaneous large depth and width limit was established in
ding2
, where the limiting hidden state dynamics are governed by a neural mean ODE with a velocity field defined by the expectation over the parameter distribution. They obtained an error bound between the hidden states of the mean ODE and the discrete model that was of order
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1}+H^{-1/2})
. However, they still required parameter dependence across the layers at initialisation. This infinite depth-and-width limit was extended to the transformer and to include
i.i.d
parameter initialisation by
gao2024global
, yielding a convergence rate
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1}+\sqrt{\log(L+1)/H})
.
In contrast,
chizat2025hiddenwidthdeepresnets
, and subsequently
chaintron2026resnets
, views the ResNet with
i.i.d.
parameter initialisation as a stochastic approximation, in the sense of
doi:10.1137/S0363012993253534
, to the mean ODE. In this regime, the authors of
chizat2025hiddenwidthdeepresnets
demonstrated that large-depth and width convergence occurs with high probability at an improved rate
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1}+(LH)^{-1/2})
. Thus, ResNets with
i.i.d.
initialised parameters converge to the corresponding mean ODE without the requirement for the width to tend to infinity.

The aforementioned works consider ResNets trained using gradient descent or gradient flows. However, large neural networks, especially transformers
zhao2025deconstructingmakesgoodoptimizer
, fail to train using SGD without careful hyperparameter tuning. Accordingly, the AdamW optimiser
loshchilov2018decoupled
dominates the training of state-of-the-art Large Language Models,
brown2020languagemodelsfewshotlearners
;
grattafiori2024llama3herdmodels
;
deepseekai2025deepseekv3technicalreport
. Also, a common assumption in the mean-field Neural ODE literature, used in
ding2021globalconvergencegradientdescent
;
gao2024global
;
isobe2024convergenceresultcontinuousmodel
for example, is that the gradient of the residual with respect to the parameters grows (sub-)linearly with the parameters. This is similar to the quadratic parameter growth assumption of
barboni2025understandingtraininginfinitelydeep
. However, in the softmax self-attention proposed by
NIPS2017_3f5ee243
, this gradient grows cubically, due to the products between the pairs of matrices
Q,K
and
O,V
. This cubic growth dominates
\ell^{2}
regularisation, causing theoretical guarantees for the Lipschitz constants to grow exponentially with training. Hence, a naive extension of
chizat2018globalconvergencegradientdescent
, for ResNets trained with SGD, would produce convergence bounds that grow triply exponentially in the number of training steps. Instead, under weak assumptions, we show in Lemma
26
that the decoupled weight-decay of AdamW forces the support of the parameter measures
\underline{\nu}_{r}^{H}
to lie in a compact set that is common to all training steps. This provides us with Lipschitz constants that are global in training, see Remark
27
.

1.3
Our Contributions

•

The main result is given in Theorem
7
, where we study the unmasked attention-only transformer given by (
4
) with parameters initialised
i.i.d.
from a distribution with compact support, eg. Xavier Uniform
pmlr-v9-glorot10a
. We demonstrate that the transformer converges in
L^{2}
, uniformly over the initial conditions, to an interacting particle system of mean ODEs at rate
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1}+L^{-1/3}H^{-1/2})
. This extends the results of
chizat2025hiddenwidthdeepresnets
;
chaintron2026resnets
1
1

1

In
chaintron2026resnets
, the dependence on the cardinality of the training data set
n
in Theorem 2.5 is stated in Remark 4.
, who demonstrate uniform convergence for the classical ResNet over a finite training data set of cardinality
n
, with bounds that grow with
\sqrt{\log(n)}
. In contrast, we consider training data sampled
i.i.d.
from an arbitrary distribution
\Xi
supported on
(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}
and establish uniform convergence over
(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}
with bounds that are independent of the number of tokens
N
. Accordingly, our results hold for initial conditions that were not included in the training data.

•

We extend the large-depth convergence results of
avelin2020neuralodesdeeplimit
;
ding2021globalconvergencegradientdescent
;
gao2024global
;
marion2024implicit
to transformers trained using AdamW
loshchilov2018decoupled
. This is crucial for the transformer as the decoupled weight-decay of AdamW can be used to show local Lipchitz continuity bounds for
\gamma
and its derivatives that are global in training step, see Lemma
26
and Remark
27
.

•

Using the Blockwise AdamW optimiser, introduced in
xie2024adam
and outlined in Section
B
, our bounds become independent of the token embedding dimension
d
as well.

•

We explicitly compute Lions’ derivative on the measure component of
\gamma
, given in Lemma
1
. As a result, we find that
\gamma
is locally Lipschitz continuous with respect to the
p
-Wasserstein distance (see Appendix
A
) for every
p\in[1,\infty]
, where the Lipschitz constant is non-exponential when
p=\infty
. Furthermore, we provide explicit bounds for the local Lipschitz continuity of its derivatives
\nabla_{x}\Gamma
and
\partial_{\mu}\Gamma
as well, given in Lemma
17
.

We refer the reader to Appendix
A.1
for notation and conventions.

2
Mean-Field Attention and its Derivatives

The generalised attention
\gamma
defined on compactly supported measures was introduced in (
2
).
For Lions’ derivative (see Appendix
A
) of
\gamma
to be well-defined, the Fréchet derivative of its lifting
\hat{\gamma}
must belong to
L^{2}
; latter requires the measure to have finite exponential moments to be well-defined. To circumvent this, fix some radius
R>0
, which will then be chosen according to Lemma
26
, and let
P_{R}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the projection map

P_{R}(x)=\frac{x}{1+\frac{1}{8(R\vee 1)^{2}}(\|x\|-R)_{+}^{2}},\qquad(r)_{+}=\mathrm{max}\{r,0\}.

(6)

A direct computation yields that
\|P_{R}(x)\|\leq\mathrm{min}\{2(R\vee 1),\|x\|\}
and
\|DP_{R}(x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, to recover a well-defined
L^{2}
lifting, we define

\gamma^{R}(z,\mu):=\gamma(z,P_{R}{}_{\#}\mu),

(7)

for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Lemma 1
.

Denoting Lions’ derivative by
\partial_{\mu}
,
\gamma^{R}
is Lions differentiable at any
y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with derivative given by

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\gamma^{R}(z,\mu)(y)=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp(\langle z,P_{R}(y)\rangle)}{\gamma^{R}_{2}(z,\mu)}\left[(P_{R}(y)-\gamma^{R}(z,\mu))z^{T}+I\right]DP_{R}(y),

where
DP_{R}
is the Fréchet derivative of
P_{R}
and
\gamma_{2}^{R}
is the normalisation constant

\gamma_{2}^{R}(z,\mu)=\int\exp(\langle z,P_{R}(y)\rangle)\,d\mu(y).

(8)

Throughout the paper, we shall use the
p
-Wasserstein distance as our metric on the space of Borel probability measures
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
p\in[1,\infty]
, which is detailed in Appendix
A
. Using Lions’ derivative of
\gamma^{R}
, the Lipschitz continuity of
\gamma^{R}
in its measure argument is established as follows.

Corollary 2
.

Take any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
. Then, for any
\mu,\tilde{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\bar{B}(R))
, there exists
\Lambda_{\mu,p}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

\|\gamma^{R}(z,\mu)-\gamma^{R}(z,\tilde{\mu})\|\leq\Lambda_{\mu,p}(\|z\|)W_{p}(\mu,\tilde{\mu}),

where
\Lambda_{\mu,p}
is given by

\Lambda_{\mu,p}(\tilde{R})=(1+2R\tilde{R})\exp(2R\tilde{R}p^{-1})
.

Remark 3
.

The Lipschitz continuity of
\gamma
with respect to the measure has been studied previously in
geshkovski2024emergenceclustersselfattentiondynamics
;
how-smooth-is-attention
for
W_{2}
.
Deviating from their approach, we use calculus on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in the present work, which allows to deduce strict bounds. The Lipschitz continuity with respect to
W_{p}
is studied in Appendix B8.4 of
castin2025unifiedperspectivedynamicsdeep
, where the Lipschitz constants derived are independent of
p
. Thus, they observe exponential behaviour.

Remark 4
.

The
z
argument of
\gamma
is taken to be
\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}x
in the definition of
\Gamma
(
3
) and the parameters
\theta
are integrated with respect to
\nu
. Thus, for
p<\infty
,
\Gamma
is Lipschitz continuous in its measure argument with respect to the
p
-Wasserstein distance only if
\nu
has exponential moments.

In Lemma
26
, we demonstrate that the hidden states and adjoint variables (see Section
3.1
) in our infinitely deep and wide transformer can be respectively bounded by
R_{X},R_{a}
uniformly in training. Therefore, by taking
R\geq R_{X}\vee R_{a}
, the projection map
P_{R}
is the identity map over the domain in which all variables in our analysis are defined. Accordingly, the superscript
R
and projection map
P_{R}
are dropped for notational convenience.

3
Uniform Scaling Limits in Transformers

As seen in Section
2
, for
\Gamma(x,m,\nu)
defined in (
3
) to be locally Lipschitz continuous with respect to the
2
-Wasserstein distance, it is necessary that
\nu
possess finite exponential moments. A sufficient and strictly stronger condition is that
\nu
has compact support. Thus, we assume that the parameters of the transformer are suitably initialised to guarantee that they remain uniformly bounded throughout training, see Lemma
26
.

Assumption 1
.

Parameter initialisation

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d})
and suppose that there exists
\lambda>0
such that the support of
\pi
is contained in
\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}:\|\mathcal{R}(v)\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{-1}\}
, where
\mathcal{R}
is the map used in Algorithm
1
. The parameters of the transformer are initialised according to

\theta^{h}_{r}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\pi,\qquad\text{for}\;0\leq r<L,\;1\leq h\leq H.

Further assume that the mean-field parameters of the continuous-time model, defined in (
17
), satisfy
\nu_{t,0}=\pi
at initialisation.

This assumption holds for common transformer initialisation strategies, such as Xavier Uniform
pmlr-v9-glorot10a
or sampling parameters from a truncated normal distribution.

3.1
The Adjoint Variable

For the gradients of the loss function to be well-defined and so that we can bound the difference between the gradients of two models, we require the following assumption on the loss function
\ell:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Assumption 2
.

Assume that
\ell:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is differentiable in the sense of Lions (see Appendix
A
), and that its Lions derivative satisfies the following Lipschitz condition: for every
R>0
, there exists
\Lambda_{\ell}=\Lambda_{\ell}(R)
, such that for all
y_{1},y_{2}\in\bar{B}(R)
, and
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R))
,

\|\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mu_{1})(y_{1})-\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mu_{2})(y_{2})\|\leq\Lambda_{\ell}(R)\left(\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|+W_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\right).

Then as a result of the continuity of
\partial_{\mu}\ell
, there also exists a constant
K=K(R)
such that
\|\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mu)(y)\|\leq K(R)
for every
y\in\bar{B}(R)
,
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R))
.

Remark 5
.

Let
(x^{i}_{0})_{i=1}^{N}
be the input token embeddings, with associated targets
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
that lie in some space
\mathcal{S}
. Then, the supervised learning problem can be embedded into our framework by augmenting the state space of the IPS to
(x,c)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S}
and using
(x^{i}_{0},c_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
as the initial condition. We define the augmented IPS dynamics so that the velocity field is independent of
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
and acts only on the
\mathbb{R}^{d}
component. Accordingly, the supervised learning objective can be written as
\ell(\widetilde{m}^{N}_{L})
, where
\widetilde{m}^{N}_{L}
is the empirical measure on the terminal distribution of the augmented IPS. The above construction corresponds to taking
c\in\mathcal{S}
to be the noise injected onto an image in a (single step) Diffusion Model
2
2

2

We do not consider the Adaptive Layer Norm that is used in DiTs
peebles2023scalable
, which depends on the diffusion step. This modification to the architecture can be ignored in the single diffusion step setting
SSDMs
.
, while
c\in S
corresponds to the token that has been masked in BERT-style LLMs. Analogous constructions hold for Vision Transformers.

Let
m^{N}_{r}
,
x^{i}_{r}
be defined according to (
4
). The key step in determining the gradients of the loss function is to find the derivative of the loss with respect to
x^{i}_{r}
, which we call the adjoint variable. By the chain rule, the adjoint variable satisfies a backward recursion, whose terminal condition is given by the derivative of
\ell(m^{N}_{L})
with respect to one of its atoms
x^{i}_{L}
. Proposition 5.35 in
carmona2018probabilistic
states that this terminal condition coincides with Lions’ derivative of
\ell
evaluated at
(\mu^{N}_{L},x^{i}_{L})
multiplied by
N^{-1}
. We can rescale the adjoint variable by
N
so that the norm of the terminal condition is independent of
N
. Correspondingly, summations over tokens are replaced by integrals against the empirical measure of tokens. Therefore, a direct computation yields that the rescaled adjoint variable satisfies

\displaystyle\underline{a}^{i}_{r}

\displaystyle=\underline{a}^{i}_{r+1}+\frac{1}{L}\mathcal{K}(x^{i}_{r},\underline{\rho}^{N}_{r},\underline{\nu}^{H}_{r},\underline{a}^{i}_{r+1}),\qquad\underline{\rho}^{N}_{r}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x^{i}_{r}}\delta_{\underline{a}^{i}_{r+1}},

(9)

\displaystyle\underline{a}^{i}_{L}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\ell(m^{N}_{L})(x^{i}_{L}),

(10)

where, for ease of notation, we have introduced
\mathcal{K}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(x,\mu,\nu,a)

\displaystyle=a^{T}\Gamma(x,\mu,\nu),

(11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(x,\rho,\nu,a)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{x}\mathcal{H}(x,\rho|_{x},\nu,a)+\int\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}(y,\rho|_{x},\nu,p)(x)d\rho(y,p).

(12)

Here,
\rho|_{x}
denotes the
x
-marginal of
\rho
. Then, the gradient of the loss function with respect to the parameters in the
r^{th}
layer and the
h^{th}
head is computed via

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta^{h}_{r}}\ell(m^{N}_{L})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{NLH}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(x^{i}_{r},m^{N}_{r},\underline{\nu}^{H}_{r},\underline{a}^{i}_{r+1})(\theta^{h}_{r}).

(13)

Proposition 5.35 in
carmona2018probabilistic
is invoked to identify the derivative of
\mathcal{H}(x,m_{r}^{N},\underline{\nu}_{r}^{H},a)
, evaluated at the empirical measure
\underline{\nu}_{r}^{H}
, with respect to one of its atoms
\theta^{h}_{r}
as the Lions derivative rescaled by
H^{-1}
. For Lions’ derivative to be well-defined, the Fréchet derivative of its lifting
\hat{\mathcal{H}}
must be in
L^{2}
. However, for a finite-width transformer, the gradient with respect to the
Q
and
K
parameters grows cubically in the parameters. Similarly to the construction of
\gamma^{R}
in Section
2
, we can define an extension to
\mathcal{H}
by pushing forward any
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d})
onto a compactly supported measure. This projection can be chosen to be the identity map over the support of
\nu_{t,\tau},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}
for any
t\in[0,1]
and
\tau\geq 0
, and so it will not affect the dynamics of the hidden states and adjoint variables. Thus, we do not incorporate it explicitly and note that this is a method to extend
\mathcal{H}
to a function whose Lions’ derivative is well-defined.

3.2
Continuous Time Models

Since we are interested in how the discrepancy between the infinitely wide and deep model and the discrete-time model evolves over training, a subscript
\tau
is introduced to denote which training step a variable is taken at. Let
\bm{y}=(y^{1},\ldots,y^{N})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
. Then, the continuous time extension (CTE) to the hidden states
(x^{i}_{r})_{r=0,i=1}^{L-1,N}
, adjoint variables
(\underline{a}^{i}_{r})_{r=0,i=1}^{L-1,N}
and parameters
(\underline{\nu}^{H}_{r})_{r=0}^{L-1}
of the discrete time IPS defined in equations (
4
), (
5
) and (
9
) with
x^{i}_{0}=y^{i}
is constructed as follows

\displaystyle\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{L}x^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{r,\tau}\mathbbm{1}_{Lt\in[r,r+1)},\quad\bar{\mu}^{N,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}},

(14)

\displaystyle\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{L}\underline{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{r+1,\tau}\mathbbm{1}_{Lt\in(r,r+1]},\quad\bar{\rho}^{N,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}}\delta_{\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}},

(15)

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{L-1}\delta_{\theta_{r,\tau}^{h}}\mathbbm{1}_{Lt\in[r,r+1)}.

(16)

Since, there is no measurable stochastic process
\Theta_{t,0}
with
\Theta_{t,0},\Theta_{s,0}
independent and identically distributed for
t,s
arbitrarily close, the large depth limit of (
14
) cannot converge to a finite width model under Assumption
1
. Therefore, we only consider the infinitely wide and deep model and initialise its parameters
\nu_{t,\tau}
according to

\nu_{t,0}=\pi,\qquad\text{for}\;t\in[0,1],

where
\pi
is the distribution from which the parameters of the discrete-time transformer are sampled at initialisation. Then, the proposed continuous time limit of (
14
)-(
16
) is

\displaystyle\frac{dX^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\mu^{N,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\nu_{t,\tau}),\qquad

\displaystyle\mu^{N,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}},

(17)

\displaystyle\frac{da^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}}{dt}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{K}(X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\rho^{N,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\nu_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}),\qquad

\displaystyle\rho^{N,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}}\delta_{a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}},

(18)

with initial and terminal conditions

X^{i,\bm{y}}_{0,\tau}=y^{i},\qquad a^{i,\bm{y}}_{1,\tau}=\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mu^{N,\bm{y}}_{1,\tau})(X^{i,\bm{y}}_{1,\tau}).

(19)

3.3
Parameter Updates

We now introduce the data that our transformer models are trained on.

Assumption 3
.

Let
\Xi\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N})
be the law of the training data, and suppose that there exists a constant
R_{0}>0
such that
\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}

\Xi
-a.s. Assume that, at step
\tau\geq 0
, the training data consists of
i.i.d.
samples
\{\bm{y}_{b,\tau}\}_{b=1}^{B}
drawn from
\Xi
.

We denote by
(X^{i,b}_{t,\tau},a^{i,b}_{t,\tau})_{i=1}^{N}
the solution to (
17
) with initial condition
\bm{y}=\bm{y}_{b,\tau}
. The same superscript notation is used for the empirical measures
\mu^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau}
, and for the corresponding discrete-time variables. To compensate for explicitly scaling the residual by
(LH)^{-1}
in (
4
), the gradients used to update the parameters are rescaled by
LH
, as done in
chizat2018globalconvergencegradientdescent
;
chaintron2026resnets
;
marion2024implicit
. Then, the rescaled gradients of the loss given in equation (
13
) at the
(\tau+1)^{th}
training step can be obtained by evaluating the map

\displaystyle g_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}_{\tau}](\theta)=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\int\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(x,\bar{\mu}^{N,L,b}_{t,\tau},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau},a)(\theta)d\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{t,\tau}(x,a),

(20)

at
\theta=\theta^{h}_{r}
for any
1\leq h\leq H
and
0\leq r<L
.
The gradient map for the continuous-time transformer (
17
) is defined similarly. It differs from (
20
) only in how
\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau}
and
\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{t,\tau}
are calculated.

Let
\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}\in(C([0,1];\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d})))^{T}
. We call
\Phi_{t,\tau}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}]:\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
, defined in equation (
28
), the AdamW flow map associated to the sequence of parameters
\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}
. It is constructed iteratively so that
\Phi_{t,\tau}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}](\theta)
coincides with the result of applying
\tau
AdamW updates to
\theta
, using gradients given by
g_{t,j+1}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}_{j}]
evaluated at
\Phi_{t,j}(\theta)
for
j<\tau
.
Therefore, at every training step
\tau\geq 0
, our mean-field parameters satisfy

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}_{<\tau}]_{\#}\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,0},\qquad\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}_{<\tau}=(\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{0},\ldots,\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{\tau-1}),

(21)

\displaystyle\nu_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\nu}_{<\tau}]_{\#}\pi,\qquad\qquad\,\,\,\bm{\nu}_{<\tau}=(\bm{\nu}_{0},\ldots,\bm{\nu}_{\tau-1}).

(22)

The subscript “
<\tau
”, indicating the length of the sequence of mean-field parameters, will be dropped, as it will be clear from the context. See Appendix
B
for further details and variants of the AdamW algorithm.

3.4
Main Results

Let
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
and
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
be complete probability spaces. We assume that the training data
((\bm{y}_{\tau,b})_{b=1}^{B})_{\tau\geq 0}
are random variables defined on
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
, and the transformer parameters
((\theta^{h}_{r})_{h=1}^{H})_{r=0}^{L-1}
at initialisation are random variables defined on
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
. Therefore, all variables are defined on the product space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, where
\Omega=\Omega^{0}\times\Omega^{1}
and
(\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is the completion of
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{0}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{1})
. The only source of randomness in the continuous-time model (
17
) is the sampling of training data, and so any of its associated variables are defined on
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
. Finally, we write
\mathbb{E}^{1}
to denote the expectation with respect to the measure
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Consider parameters
\hat{\nu}_{t,\tau}
which are initialised identically to the discrete model
\hat{\nu}_{t,0}=\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,0}
but trained using the gradients of the continuous model. That is, for any
t\in[0,1]
,
\tau\in[0:T]
and
r_{t}=\lfloor Lt\rfloor/L
, define

\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}=\Phi_{r_{t},\tau}[\bm{\nu}_{<\tau}]_{\#}\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,0}.

(23)

The discrepancy between
\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}
and
\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}
encapsulates the error between the training algorithm of the continuous and discrete time models. This source of error is bounded in the following Lemma.

Lemma 6
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
-
3
hold and take
\hat{\nu}_{t,\tau}
in (
23
) and let
\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}
be the discrete time model’s parameters (
14
) at the
\tau^{th}
training step. Then, after training the parameters for
T
steps using AdamW, Algorithm
1
with
\mathcal{R}
equal to the identity map,
0<\beta_{1}\leq\beta_{2}<1
, weight decay
\lambda
and step sizes satisfying
\eta_{k}\in(0,1/\lambda)
, there exists a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\Lambda_{\ell},R_{0},\lambda,T,d,k,\varepsilon)
such that for any
0\leq\tau\leq T
,

\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{0\leq r<L}W_{2}^{2}(\hat{\nu}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau})\right]\leq C_{1}\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{L^{\frac{2}{3}}H}\right),\qquad\mathbb{P}^{0}-a.s.

Furthermore, the constant
C_{1}
can be taken independent of
d,k
if the models are trained via

Algorithm
1
with
\mathcal{R}
given by equation (
26
).

Our uniform convergence result for the unmasked attention-only transformer is as follows.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose that we are in the same setting as Lemma
6
and that the same assumptions hold. Then, there exists a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\Lambda_{\ell},R_{0},\lambda,T,d,k,\varepsilon)
, such that for any training step
\tau\in[0:T]

\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}\|\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}-X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}\|^{2}+\|\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}-a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}\|^{2}\right]\leq C_{2}\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{L^{\frac{2}{3}}H}\right).

The above inequality holds
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. Furthermore, the constant
C_{2}
can be taken independent of
d,k
if Algorithm
1
is performed with
\mathcal{R}
given by equation (
26
).

Remark 8
.

The Euclidean norm of the input data to neural networks typically scales as
\sqrt{d}
. Our framework can be adapted to incorporate this by choosing
\beta=\mathcal{O}(d^{-1})
, and rescaling the output by
d^{-1/2}
before applying the loss and rescaling the gradients by
d^{-1}
. Having done this, the bound in Theorem
7
remains valid on the rescaled variables
\widetilde{X}^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}=\sqrt{d}X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
and
\widetilde{a}^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}=d\,a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
, with the Euclidean norms replaced by the RMS norm. The adjoint variable is rescaled by
d
so that
\widetilde{a}^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
has RMS norm of order one, with the additional factor of
\sqrt{d}
arising from rescaling the output before applying the loss. For further details on this regime, see
chaintron2026resnets
and Section 4 in
chizat2025hiddenwidthdeepresnets
; Our rescalings differ due to alternative parameter initialisation assumptions.

3.5
Sketch Proof

Firstly, we show that Adam updates are bounded (see Lemma
43
), and so the weight decay dominates for sufficiently large parameters. Therefore, there exists a compact set
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
, that depends only on
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}

\lambda
, such that
\nu_{t,\tau}
and
\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}
are supported in
\mathfrak{S}
uniformly in
t\in[0,1]
and training step
\tau\geq 0
. Under AdamW updates,
\mathfrak{S}
depends on
d
and
k
. However, this dependence is removed when using Blockwise AdamW, Algorithm
1
, with
\mathcal{R}
given by (
26
). This implies that the estimates of Appendix
D
ensure that the velocity fields
\Gamma
and
\mathcal{K}
in (
17
) have local Lipschitz continuity and exhibit linear growth.

We consider a forward-backwards system of McKean–Vlasov ODEs (MVODEs) (
56
)-(
57
) associated to the IPS (
17
). Given initial condition
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and by writing the solution
(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t})
to the forward-backwards system of MVODEs as a fixed point of an operator, we demonstrate that the Picard iterates associated with this operator preserve measurability with respect to the noise induced by the training data sampling. These Picard iterates converge to
(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t})
due to Theorem 4.21 in
carmona2018probabilistic
Vol. I. By the stability of measurability under pointwise limits, it follows that
(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t})
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable, and that its conditional law
\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t})
given
\mathcal{F}^{0}
(ie. the training data) is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
. The solution
(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t})
conditioned on
\xi=x
satisfies the ODEs (
60
)-(
61
), whose solution we denote
(X^{x,\zeta}_{t},a^{x,\zeta}_{t})
where
\zeta
is the law of
\xi
. By comparing ODEs (
60
)-(
61
) to (
17
), we obtain that the IPS (
17
) has a unique
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable solution
(X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})
, which coincides with
(X^{x,\zeta}_{t},a^{x,\zeta}_{t})
when
x=y_{i}
and
\zeta
is the empirical measure on the atoms
(y_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
. This provides a framework to study the transformer that is independent of the number of tokens.

Next, define

M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}=\int_{0}^{\frac{r}{L}}\Gamma\left(X^{x,\zeta}_{s},(X^{\cdot,\zeta}_{s})_{\#}\zeta,\nu_{r_{s},\tau}\right)-\Gamma(X^{x,\zeta}_{s},(X^{\cdot,\zeta}_{s})_{\#}\zeta,\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{s,\tau})ds,

for
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
and
x\in\bar{B}(R_{0})
. A more general version of
M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}
is introduced in Appendix
H
to study the forward and backward equations at the same time. In Lemma
38
, we show that
M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}
is a martingale with respect to the natural filtration generated by the initialised parameters. Accordingly, the supremum over
(x,\zeta)
on
\|M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}\|
, which we denote
M^{*}_{r,\tau}
, is a sub-martingale. Hence, Doob’s
L^{2}
inequality
revuz2013continuous
can be applied to bound the
L^{2}
norm on the maximum value of
M^{*}_{r,\tau}
over the layers
r\in[0:L]
by the
L^{2}
norm on
M^{*}_{L,\tau}
.

Notice that
\gamma(z,\mu)
and its derivatives, given in equations (
2
), (
31
) and (
36
) respectively, can be expressed as ratios of functions that depend linearly on either the measure
\mu
or the product measure
\mu\otimes\mu
. By exploiting the independence of the parameters at initialisation, concentration of measure techniques can be applied to bound the
L^{1}
norm on
M^{*}_{L,\tau}
above by an expression that depends linearly in
\zeta
or
\zeta\otimes\zeta
. This argument is formalised in Lemma
33
. Linear functionals on probability measures
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
have extreme points at Dirac masses, which reduces the space over which the supremum is taken from the infinite-dimensional
\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
to
\bar{B}(R_{0})\times\bar{B}(R_{0})
. Thus, the uniform bounds lose their dependence on the number of tokens.

In Lemma
36
, we split the layers into
\lceil L/m\rceil
blocks, and approximate
X^{x,\zeta}_{t}
within each block by a Taylor expansion in
t
. The
(x,\zeta)
dependence in the bound on the
L^{1}
norm of
M^{*}_{L,\tau}
from Lemma
33
only involves terms that are multi-linear in
X^{x,\zeta}_{t}
. Furthermore, for each
l\in[0:\lceil L/m\rceil-1]
, the Taylor approximation on layers
r\in[lm:(l+1)m-1]
only involves derivatives of
X^{x,\zeta}_{t}
evaluated at
r=lm
. Therefore, we can factor out the dependency of
\partial^{j}_{t}X^{x,\zeta}_{t}
from each block of
m
layers. By bounding these derivatives uniformly in
(x,\zeta)
, we obtain a bound on the
L^{1}
norm of
M^{*}_{L,\tau}
without using a covering argument over the domain of the supremum. Having split the layers into blocks and factored out the
X_{t}^{x,\zeta}
dependence, we observe averaging over
mH
parameters in each block. Since the parameters are initialised independently and have been explicitly scaled by
(LH)^{-1}
, the
L^{2}
norm on this average scales as
\sqrt{mL^{-2}H^{-1}}
. By bounding the error between
X^{x,\zeta}_{t}
and its Taylor approximation in standard fashion, we optimise over
m
to obtain our bound on the
L^{1}
norm of
M^{*}_{L,\tau}
. Finally, noting that
\Gamma
satisfies a bounded differences property whenever the measure
\nu
on the parameters is empirical, McDiarmid’s inequality is used in Lemma
38
to control the difference between the
L^{1}
and
L^{2}
norms.

In Lemma
40
, the error
\|X^{i,\bm{y}}_{(r+1)/L,\tau}-\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{(r+1)/L,\tau}\|
is bounded by three terms. The first is
M^{x,\zeta}_{r+1,\tau}
for
x,\zeta
as above. The second arises from evaluating the parameters
\nu_{r_{s}}
in
M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}
at the discrete time
r_{s}
, and is controlled using the Lipschitz continuity of
t\mapsto\nu_{t,\tau}
established in Lemma
29
. The third term is bounded using the Lipschitz continuity of
\Gamma
, yielding the expression given in Lemma
6
together with terms involving
\|X^{i,\bm{y}}_{l/L,\tau}-\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{l/L,\tau}\|
for
l\leq r
, which are handled by Grönwall’s Lemma. By construction, the parameters
\bm{\hat{\nu}}_{0}^{N,L}
and
\bm{\bar{\nu}}_{0}^{N,L}
agree at initialisation. Hence, using the coupling induced by pushing forward
\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,0}
under
(\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\nu}],\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}])
, we find that

W_{2}^{2}(\hat{\nu}_{t,\tau},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau})\leq\int\|\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)-\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}](\theta)\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\,d\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,0}(\theta).

By Lemma
28
, the difference between
\theta_{t,\tau}:=\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)
and
\vartheta_{t,\tau}:=\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}](\theta)
is bounded by the differences in the gradients
g_{t,j+1}[\nu_{j}](\theta_{t,j})
and
g_{t,j+1}[\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{j}](\vartheta_{t,j})
, for
j<\tau
. In turn, by Lemma
30
, the squared Frobenius norm of the difference between the gradient maps
g_{t,j+1}[\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{j}](\theta_{t,j})
and
g_{t,j+1}[\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{j}](\vartheta_{t,j})
is bounded above by
\|\theta_{t,j}-\vartheta_{t,j}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}+\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{j}
, where
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{j}
is defined by

\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{j}:=\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}\|X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,j}-\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,j}\|^{2}+\|a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,j}-\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,j}\|^{2}

Therefore, the squared Frobenius norm between
\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)
and
\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}](\theta)
can be
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. bounded uniformly in
\theta\in\mathfrak{S}
by a linear combination of
\mathscr{L}_{j}
for
j<\tau
. Then, taking the supremum over the initial conditions, particle index and time in the three errors described above and then taking the expectation under
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we obtain that
\mathbb{E}^{1}[\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{\tau}]
is bounded by
C(L^{-2}+L^{-2/3}H^{-1})
, for some
C>0
, plus a linear combination of
\mathbb{E}^{1}[\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{j}]
for
j<\tau
. Hence, in Appendix
J
, Lemma
6
and Theorem
7
are concluded by a Grönwall type argument over the layers and training steps.

4
Conclusion and Future Work

We demonstrated that the dynamics of the hidden state and adjoint variables of AdamW-trained transformers converge in
L^{2}
to a forward–backward system of ODEs. The convergence holds uniformly over the initial conditions, with bounds independent of the number of tokens. If a Blockwise AdamW optimiser, introduced in
xie2024adam
, is used instead, the bounds can be made independent of the token embedding dimension. We have had to sacrifice the order
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1}+(LH)^{-1/2})
rate of convergence seen in
chizat2025hiddenwidthdeepresnets
to
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1}+L^{-1/3}H^{-1/2})
to produce these uniform bounds. Future research will be done to close this gap. Furthermore, our bounds hold up to finite training time. We expect that, similarly to
marion2024implicit
, they can be extended globally in training by exploiting the adjoint variable tending to zero as the parameters approach a local minimum of the loss.

5
Acknowledgements

The authors would also acknowledge support from His Majesty’s Government in the development of this research.

Appendix A
Background Material

A.1
Notation & Conventions

We shall write
r_{t}
for the time discretisation
r_{t}=\lfloor Lt\rfloor/L
. The symbols
\vee,\wedge
denote
a\wedge b=\mathrm{min}\{a,b\}
and
a\vee b=\mathrm{max}\{a,b\}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. For
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we write
[n:m]=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:n\leq i\leq m\}
.

For any radius
R>0
, let us write
\bar{B}_{d}(R)
for the subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of elements with Euclidean norm less than or equal to
R
. The subscript
d
will be dropped when it is the token embedding dimension. Let
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the space of Borel probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite
p
-moment,
p\in[1,\infty)
, and write
\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the space of compactly supported measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Also, for any compact subset
K
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, denote the space of Borel probability measures with support contained in
K
by
\mathcal{P}(K)
.

On a measurable space
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
and for any separable metric space
E
, the map
Z:\widetilde{\Omega}\mapsto E
is said to be
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable if it is
(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{B}(E))
-measurable, where
\mathcal{B}(E)
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
E
. For any Borel measurable
T:E_{1}\mapsto E_{2}
, where
E_{1},E_{2}
are separable metric spaces, we denote the pushforward measure of
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(E_{1})
under
T
by
T_{\#}\mu
so that
T_{\#}\mu(D)=\mu(T^{-1}(D))
for every
D\in\mathcal{B}(E_{2})
.

Motivated by bounding each block of
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
separately, define, for any
R>0
, the set

\mathfrak{S}(R)=\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}:\|\mathcal{R}_{1}(\theta)\|_{\infty}\leq R\},

(24)

where
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is given by equation (
26
).

A.2
Optimal Transport

Given
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the
p
-Wasserstein distance is given by

W_{p}(\mu,\nu)=\left(\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\int\|x-y\|^{p}d\pi(x,y)\right)^{1/p}.

Where
\Pi(\mu,\nu)
denotes the subset of
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
with marginals
\mu,\nu
.
The limit as
p\to\infty
for
W_{p}
is well-defined for measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where the limiting value coincides with

W_{\infty}(\mu,\nu)=\underset{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}{inf}\|x-y\|_{L^{\infty}(\pi)}.

See
[
51
]
for further details on the Wasserstein distance and Optimal Transport. Further details on
W_{\infty}
are given in
[
47
]
.

A.3
Lions’ Derivative

Let
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})
be an atomless Polish probability space and take
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
H:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that there exists a square integrable random variable
Y
on
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})
, and a lifting

\hat{H}:L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\hat{H}(Y)=H(\nu)
is Fréchet differentiable at
Y
. Then, by the Reisz representation theorem, there exists a unique function
\partial_{\mu}H(\nu):\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
independent of the choice of the random variable
Y
and lifting
\hat{H}
such that for any
Z\in L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R}^{d})

\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\langle D\hat{H}(Y),Z\rangle]=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\langle\partial_{\mu}H(\nu)(Y),Z\rangle],

where
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}
is the expectation with respect to the measure
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
.
The Lions derivative of
H
at
\nu
is given by
\partial_{\mu}H(\nu)
. See Chapter 5.2 in
[
8
]
Vol. I for further details.
With the Lions derivative machinery, the strategy for finding Lipschitz constant using the Mean Value Theorem can be extended to measures
[
28
]
.

Lemma 9
.

Suppose
f:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function such that its Lions derivative
\partial_{\mu}f:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
exists. Then there exists
t\in(0,1)
such that

f(\tilde{\mu})-f(\mu)=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\langle\partial_{\mu}f\left(\mathcal{L}(Z+t(\tilde{Z}-Z)),Z+t(\tilde{Z}-Z)\right),\tilde{Z}-Z\right\rangle\right],

for any
\mu,\tilde{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and random variables
Z,\tilde{Z}
, defined on an atomless Polish probability space
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})
, such that
\mathcal{L}(Z)=\mu,\mathcal{L}(\tilde{Z})=\tilde{\mu}
. Here,
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}
is the expectation with respect to the measure
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
.

The proof is given in Lemma 5 of
[
28
]
.

Appendix B
AdamW & Variants

The training of Large Language Models without careful hyperparameter tuning is reliant on adaptive step size methods
[
56
]
. Consequently, the AdamW
[
31
]
optimisation algorithm dominates the training of Large Language Models,
[
29
,
23
,
7
]
. The AdamW optimisation algorithm decouples the weight decay on the parameters from the Adam updates. However, since the weight decay is not incorporated into the loss function, the effective optimisation objective is not, a priori, clear. Theorem 1.1 in
[
53
]
states that if AdamW with weight decay
\lambda>0
converges, then it converges to a KKT point of the constrained optimisation problem (
25
)

\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\theta)\quad\text{subject to}\quad\|\theta\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{-1},

(25)

where
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is any smooth function bounded below.
The
z
argument of
\gamma
is taken to be
\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}x
in the definition of
\Gamma
in (
3
). Therefore, the Lipschitz constants for attention scale with the operator norm on
\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}
. However, from equation (
25
), we see that AdamW will control each component of
\theta
separately, and so we can only hope to bound
\|\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
uniformly in training step using the infinity norm on the parameters. This introduces dimensional dependence into our bounds. Instead, Algorithm
1
generalises AdamW
[
31
]
to regularise with alternative norms on blocks of parameters.

Algorithm 1

Blockwise AdamW Optimisers
[
54
]

Blockwise AdamW optimisation algorithm, where the variance accumulator is performed on
\mathcal{R}(g)
. Here, each gradient
g
lies in a finite-dimensional vector space
\mathscr{V}
and
\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{V}\mapsto\mathscr{V}
. AdamW corresponds to
\mathcal{R}
being the identity map.

1:

\theta_{0}\in\mathscr{V}
,
(\eta_{j})_{j=1}^{T}
,
\lambda
,
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in(0,1)
,
\beta_{2}\geq\beta_{1}
,
\varepsilon>0

2:

m_{0},v_{0}\leftarrow\bm{0}\in\mathscr{V}

3:

for

j=1
to
T

do

4:

g_{j}\leftarrow\nabla_{\theta}Loss(\theta_{j-1})

5:

m_{j}\leftarrow\beta_{1}m_{j-1}+(1-\beta_{1})g_{j}

v_{j}\leftarrow\beta_{2}v_{j-1}+(1-\beta_{2})\mathcal{R}(g_{j})\odot\mathcal{R}(g_{j})

6:

\hat{m}_{j}\leftarrow m_{j}(1-\beta_{1}^{j})^{-1}

\hat{v}_{j}\leftarrow v_{j}(1-\beta_{2}^{j})^{-1}

7:

\theta_{j}\leftarrow(1-\eta_{j}\lambda)\theta_{j-1}-\eta_{j}\frac{\hat{m}_{j}}{\sqrt{\hat{v}_{j}}+\varepsilon}

8:

end

for

9:

return

\theta_{T}

Take
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
and consider adapting the variance accumulator
\hat{v}
of AdamW to be calculated on
\mathcal{R}(g)
rather than
g
per layer and head. To incentivise small Frobenius norm on each
Q,K,V,O
matrix, consider

\mathcal{R}_{1}(g^{(1)},g^{(2)},g^{(3)},g^{(4)})=\left(\|g^{(1)}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\bm{1},\|g^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\bm{1},\|g^{(3)}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\bm{1},\|g^{(4)}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\bm{1}\right),

(26)

with each
g^{(j)}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
,
j=1,\ldots,4
. The brackets refer to column-wise concatenation of matrices, and
\bm{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
is the matrix where every entry is one. This is a specific example of the Blockwise AdamW algorithm introduced in
[
54
]
. Furthermore, when
\mathcal{R}
is given by (
26
), the resulting optimisation algorithm is similar to the Muon algorithm with decoupled weight decay proposed in
[
30
]
. Muon is applied to blocks of weight matrices. Instead of normalising the momentum accumulator by the Frobenius norm on the variance accumulators, Muon approximately orthogonalises the matrix of momentum accumulators.

To facilitate analysis, let us now define the maps and quantities associated to Algorithm
1
.
Given a sequence of gradients
G=(g_{j})_{k=1}^{T}
,
g_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
, the momentum and variance accumulators
\hat{m}^{\mathcal{R}}_{T},\hat{v}^{\mathcal{R}}_{T}:(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d})^{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
are respectively defined by

\hat{m}^{\mathcal{R}}_{T}(G)=\frac{1-\beta_{1}}{1-\beta_{1}^{T}}\sum_{j=1}^{T}\beta_{1}^{T-j}g_{j},\qquad\hat{v}^{\mathcal{R}}_{T}(G)=\frac{1-\beta_{2}}{1-\beta_{2}^{T}}\sum_{j=1}^{T}\beta_{2}^{T-j}\mathcal{R}(g_{j})\odot\mathcal{R}(g_{j}).

(27)

Here,
\odot
refers to the componentwise (Hadamard) product. Notice that the effect of weight decay applied at step
i
on step
\tau
is quantified by
\alpha_{i,\tau}=\prod_{j=i}^{\tau}(1-\eta_{j}\lambda)
, where we use the convention
\alpha_{\tau+1,\tau}=1
.

We inductively define the AdamW flow map
\Phi_{t,\tau}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}]:\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
associated to the sequence of gradients
\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}\in(C([0,1],\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d})))^{T}
. Firstly, for every
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
, define

\Phi_{t,0}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}](\theta)=\theta.

Now, suppose that
\Phi_{t,j}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}]
is well-defined for every
j<\tau
. Then, we define
\Phi_{t,\tau}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}](\theta)
according to

\displaystyle\Phi_{t,\tau}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}](\theta)=\alpha_{1,\tau}\theta-\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}\eta_{i}\alpha_{i+1,\tau}\frac{\hat{m}^{\mathcal{R}}_{i}(G_{t}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}](\theta))}{\sqrt{\hat{v}^{\mathcal{R}}_{i}(G_{t}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}](\theta))}+\varepsilon},

(28)

where division is performed componentwise.
Here,
G_{t}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}](\theta)\in(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d})^{T}
is the sequence of gradients, whose
j
-th element is given by

\displaystyle\left(G_{t}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}](\theta)\right)_{j}=g_{t,j}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}_{j-1}](\Phi_{t,j-1}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}](\theta)),

(29)

where
g_{t,j}[\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}_{j-1}]
is the gradient map defined by (
20
) with hidden states and adjoint variables computed according to the mean-field parameters
\widetilde{\bm{\nu}}_{j-1}
.

Appendix C
Derivatives of
\gamma

Notice that
\gamma^{R}
, as defined in (
7
), is an expectation under a new measure
\mathcal{A}_{z,\mu}
that is define through the Radon-Nikodym derivative

\frac{d\mathcal{A}_{z,\mu}}{d\mu}(y)=\frac{\exp(\langle z,P_{R}(y)\rangle)}{\gamma^{R}_{2}(z,\mu)},

(30)

where
P_{R}
and
\gamma^{R}_{2}
are defined in (
6
) and (
32
), respectively.

Lemma 10
.

Take
R_{1}>0
and
\gamma^{R}
defined in (
7
). Then,
\gamma^{R}
is differentiable in
z
at any
z\in\bar{B}(R_{1})
at fixed
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1}))
. Furthermore, for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1}))
and
z,\tilde{z}\in\bar{B}(R_{1})
, we have

\|\gamma^{R}(z,\mu)-\gamma^{R}(\tilde{z},\mu)\|\leq 2R_{1}^{2}\|z-\tilde{z}\|.

Proof.

Write
\gamma^{R}_{1}=\gamma^{R}_{2}\gamma^{R}
for the unnormalized counterpart to
\gamma^{R}
.
Differentiation and integration in
\gamma^{R}_{1}
,
\gamma^{R}_{2}
can be interchanged as a result of the Dominated Convergence Theorem, where we dominate using the compact support of
(P_{R})_{\#}\mu
. The respective derivatives are given by

\displaystyle D_{z}\gamma^{R}_{1}(z,\mu)

\displaystyle=\int\exp(\langle z,y\rangle)\,yy^{T}\,d(P_{R})_{\#}\mu(y),

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}\gamma^{R}_{2}(z,\mu)

\displaystyle=\int exp(\langle z,y\rangle)\,y\,d(P_{R})_{\#}\mu(y).

As a result of the quotient rule,
\gamma^{R}
in (
7
) is Gâteaux differentiable with respect to
z
, since
\gamma^{R}_{2}
is bounded away from zero by
exp(-2R_{1}(R\vee 1))
as
\|P_{R}(y)\|\leq 2(R\vee 1)
for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, whose derivative is given by

\displaystyle D_{z}\gamma^{R}(z,\mu)=\iint y_{1}(y_{1}-y_{2})^{T}\,d\mathcal{A}_{z,\mu}(y_{1})d\mathcal{A}_{z,\mu}(y_{2}).

(31)

The Radon-Nikodym derivative that defines
\mathcal{A}
is continuous and bounded for
z
ranging over compact sets, and so
\gamma^{R}
is Fréchet differentiable.
Therefore, by the Mean Value Theorem, it suffices to bound the operator norm of the total derivative
D_{z}\gamma^{R}
to find the Lipschitz constant for
\gamma^{R}
in
z
, which we do according to

\displaystyle\|\partial_{z}\gamma^{R}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\iint\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|\|y_{1}\|\;d\mathcal{A}_{z,\mu}(y_{1})d\mathcal{A}_{z,\mu}(y_{2})\leq 2R_{1}^{2}.

The final inequality uses the fact that
supp\;\mathcal{A}
is contained in
\bar{B}(R_{1})
since that of
\mu
is also.
∎

Lemma 11
.

Denoting Lions’ derivative by
\partial_{\mu}
,
\gamma^{R}
is Lions differentiable at any
y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with derivative given by

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\gamma^{R}(z,\mu)(y)=

\displaystyle\frac{\exp(\langle z,P_{R}(y)\rangle)}{\gamma^{R}_{2}(z,\mu)}\left[(P_{R}(y)-\gamma^{R}(z,\mu))z^{T}+I\right]DP_{R}(y),

where
DP_{R}
is the Fréchet derivative of
P_{R}
and
\gamma_{2}^{R}
is the normalisation constant

\gamma_{2}^{R}(z,\mu)=\int\exp(\langle z,P_{R}(y)\rangle)\,d\mu(y).

(32)

Proof.

Let
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})
be an atomless Polish probability space where there exists a random variable
Y\in L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\mathcal{L}(Y)=\mu
. We lift
\gamma^{R}_{1}
, as defined in the proof of Lemma
10
, and
\gamma_{2}^{R}
to
L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
by

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{R}(z,Y)

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\exp(z^{T}P_{R}(Y))P_{R}(Y)],

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{2}^{R}(z,Y)

\displaystyle:=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\exp(z^{T}P_{R}(Y))].

Take
H\in L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\eta>0
. Consider the perturbation of
\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{R}
at
Y
in the direction of
H
, given by

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{R}(z,Y+\eta H)-\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{R}(z,Y)}{\eta}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(z^{T}P_{R}(Y+\eta H))\frac{P_{R}(Y+\eta H)-P_{R}(Y)}{\eta}\right]

\displaystyle+\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(z^{T}P_{R}(Y))\frac{\exp(\langle z,P_{R}(Y+\eta H)-P_{R}(Y)\rangle)-1}{\eta}P_{R}(Y)\right].

Letting
\eta\to 0
and applying the Dominated Convergence Theorem to interchange the order of limit and expectation, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{R}(z,Y+\eta H)-\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{R}(z,Y)}{\eta}\to\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(z^{T}P_{R}(Y))\left\{DP_{R}(Y)+P_{R}(Y)z^{T}DP_{R}(Y)\right\}H\right].

(33)

Therefore, we deduce that the Gâteaux derivative
D\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{R}(z,Y)
is given by the right-hand side of the above expression. The Gâteaux derivative coincides with the Fréchet derivative when
D\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{R}(z,Y)
, viewed as a linear map from
L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega})
to
L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega})
, is continuous in
Y
. Clearly,
D\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{R}(z,Y)
given on the right hand side of equation (
33
) is continuous in
Y
, and so the Lions derivative of
\gamma_{1}^{R}
is given by

\partial_{\mu}\gamma_{1}^{R}(z,\mu)(y)=\exp(\langle z,P_{R}(y)\rangle)\left\{P_{R}(y)z^{T}+I\right\}DP_{R}(y).

In identical fashion, we find

\partial_{\mu}\gamma_{2}^{R}(z,\mu)(y)=\exp(\langle z,P_{R}(y)\rangle)DP_{R}(y)^{T}z.

Since
\gamma_{2}
is bounded away from zero, as seen in the previous proof of
D_{z}\gamma^{R}
, we can apply the quotient rule on
\gamma^{R}
to find

\partial_{\mu}\gamma^{R}(z,\mu)(y)=\frac{\exp(\langle z,P(y)\rangle)}{\gamma^{R}_{2}(z,\mu)}\left\{(P_{R}(y)-\gamma^{R}(z,\mu))z^{T}+I\right\}DP_{R}(y).

∎

Corollary 12
.

Take any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
p\in[1,\infty]
. Then, for any
\mu,\tilde{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\bar{B}(R))
, there exists
\Lambda_{\mu,p}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

\|\gamma^{R}(z,\mu)-\gamma^{R}(z,\tilde{\mu})\|\leq\Lambda_{\mu,p}(\|z\|)W_{p}(\mu,\tilde{\mu}),

where
\Lambda_{\mu,p}
is given by

\Lambda_{\mu,p}(\tilde{R})=(1+2R\tilde{R})\exp(2R\tilde{R}p^{-1})
.

Proof.

The assumptions in Lemma
9
are satisfied by
\gamma^{R}
as a result of Lemma
1
. Hence, for every
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, there exists
t\in(0,1)
and random variables
Y,\tilde{Y}
on some atomless Polish probability space
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})
with laws
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
respectively such that

\displaystyle u^{T}\left(\gamma^{R}(z,\mu)-\gamma^{R}(z,\tilde{\mu})\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[u^{T}\partial_{\mu}\gamma^{R}(z,\mathcal{L}(Y_{t}))(Y_{t})(Y-\tilde{Y})]

(34)

\displaystyle\leq\|\|\partial_{\mu}\gamma^{R}(z,\mathcal{L}(Y_{t}))(Y_{t})\|_{op}\|_{L^{q}(\widetilde{\Omega})}\|Y-\tilde{Y}\|_{L^{p}(\widetilde{\Omega})},

where
Y_{t}=\tilde{Y}+t(Y-\tilde{Y})
and we have used
\|u\|\leq 1
. Since
Y,\tilde{Y}
can be any random variables with laws
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
taking values in a Polish space, we can choose the coupling between
Y,\tilde{Y}
that minimises
\|Y-\tilde{Y}\|_{L^{p}(\widetilde{\Omega})}
. Under this coupling
\|Y-\tilde{Y}\|_{L^{p}(\widetilde{\Omega})}
attains the
p
-Wasserstein distance between
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
. Thus, it suffices to prove that
\|\partial_{\mu}\gamma^{R}\|_{op}
is bounded in
L^{q}(\widetilde{\Omega})
to prove our claim. Since the supports of
\mu,\tilde{\mu}
are contained in
\bar{B}(R)
, it follows that
Y_{t}\in\bar{B}(R)

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. Therefore, we have that
P_{R}(Y_{t})=Y_{t}
and
DP_{R}(Y_{t})=I
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely. Furthermore,
\gamma^{R}(x,\mathcal{L}(Y_{t}))
is bounded
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. by
R
, which yields that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\mu}\gamma^{R}(z,\mathcal{L}(Y_{t}))(Y_{t})\|^{q}_{op}\leq\frac{\exp(\langle z,Y_{t}\rangle)}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\exp(\langle z,Y_{t}\rangle)]}(1+2R\|z\|)^{q}\cdot\left\|\frac{\exp(\langle z,Y_{t}\rangle)}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\exp(\langle z,Y_{t}\rangle)]}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\widetilde{\Omega})}^{q-1}\cdot\|DP_{R}(Y_{t})\|^{q}_{\mathrm{op}}.

holds
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely. By taking the expectation with resepect to
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
and applying
\|Y_{t}\|\leq R

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s., we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\|\partial_{\mu}\gamma\|_{op}\|_{L^{q}(\widetilde{\Omega})}\leq(1+2R\|z\|)exp\left[2(1-q^{-1})R\|z\|\right]

The result concludes by applying the bound on
\|\partial_{\mu}\gamma\|_{\mathrm{op}}
derived above to (
34
), then taking the supremum over
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and noting that
1-q^{-1}=p^{-1}
.
∎

Appendix D
Bounds & Lipschitz continuity of
\Gamma,\mathcal{K}

Let
R_{1}>0
. Throughout this section, we write
\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z,\mu)(y)
for the Lions derivative of
\gamma^{R}
at
(\mu,y)\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1}))\times\bar{B}(R_{1})
with
R>R_{1}
so that the map
P_{R}
in the definition of
\gamma^{R}
acts trivially.

Lemma 13
.

Given
\gamma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined in (
2
) and radius
R_{1}>0
, for any

y\in\bar{B}(R_{1}),\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1}))
and
z,\Delta z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|D_{zz}\gamma(z,\mu)\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle\leq 8R_{1}^{3},

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{d}\partial_{z_{k}}\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z,\mu)(y)(\Delta z)_{k}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq 2R_{1}(2+3R_{1}\|z\|)\frac{\exp(z^{T}y)}{\gamma_{2}(z,\mu)}\|\Delta z\|.

Remark 14
.

We contract the third order tensor
\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}\gamma
in its
z
-derivative component against a test vector
\Delta z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to make the operator norm on a third order tensor explicit.

Proof.

Recall the expression for
D_{z}\gamma
given in equation (
31
) and expand the definition of measure
\mathcal{A}
to give

D_{z}\gamma(z,\mu)=\frac{\iint\exp(\langle z,y_{1}+y_{2}\rangle)\,y_{1}(y_{1}-y_{2})^{T}\,d\mu(y_{1})d\mu(y_{2})}{(\gamma_{2}(z,\mu))^{2}}.

(35)

Integration and differentiation can be interchanged by the Dominated Convergence Theorem. Thus, the quotient rule gives

(D_{zz}\gamma(z,\mu))_{i,j,k}=\iiint(y_{1})_{i}(y_{1}-y_{2})_{j}\left[y_{1}+y_{2}-2y_{3}\right]_{k}\,d\mathcal{A}_{z,\mu}(y_{1})d\mathcal{A}_{z,\mu}(y_{2})d\mathcal{A}_{z,\mu}(y_{3}),

after applying the identity
\partial_{z}\gamma_{2}(z,\mu)=\gamma(z,\mu)\gamma_{2}(z,\mu)

to the term that results from taking the derivative to the denominator of
D_{z}\gamma
. The bound on the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of
D_{zz}\gamma
holds since the support of the probability measure
\mathcal{A}_{z,\mu}
is contained in
\bar{B}(R_{1})
.

Recall from Lemma
1
that, for any
y\in\bar{B}(R_{1})
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1}))
,
\partial_{\mu}\gamma
is given by

\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z,\mu)(y)=\frac{\exp(z^{T}y)}{\gamma_{2}(z,\mu)}\left[\{y-\gamma(z,\mu)\}z^{T}+I\right].

(36)

By the quotient and product rule for differentiation, we get

\displaystyle\partial_{z_{k}}(\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z,\mu)(y))_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(z^{T}y)}{\gamma_{2}(z,\mu)}\left\{(y-\gamma(z,\mu))_{k}\left[(y-\gamma(z,\mu))_{i}z_{j}+\delta_{ij}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle-\frac{\exp(z^{T}y)}{\gamma_{2}(z,\mu)}\left\{(D_{z}\gamma(z,\mu))_{i,k}z_{j}+\delta_{jk}(\gamma(z,\mu)-y)_{i}\right\},

where
\delta_{ij}
takes the value one if
i
equals
j
and is otherwise zero. Consequently, contract the third-order tensor
\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}\gamma
against
\Delta z
to give the matrix

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d}\partial_{z_{k}}\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z,\mu)(y)(\Delta z)_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(z^{T}y)}{\gamma_{2}(z,\mu)}\left((y-\gamma(z,\mu))z^{T}+I\right)(\Delta z^{T}(y-\gamma(z,\mu)))

\displaystyle-\frac{\exp(z^{T}y)}{\gamma_{2}(z,\mu)}\left(D_{z}\gamma(z,\mu)\Delta z\,z^{T}+(\gamma(z,\mu)-y)(\Delta z)^{T}\right).

The claim on the operator norm of
\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}\gamma
holds since
y,\gamma(z,\mu)
are bounded by
R_{1}
, and
\|D_{z}\gamma\|_{\mathrm{op}}
is bounded above by
2R_{1}^{2}
according to Lemma
10
.
∎

Corollary 15
.

Given
R_{1},R_{z}>0
, there exists a constant
\Lambda_{D\gamma,p}=\Lambda_{D\gamma,p}(R_{1},R_{z})
such that for any
y_{i}\in\bar{B}(R_{1}),z_{i}\in\bar{B}(R_{z})
and
\mathfrak{m}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1}))
for
i=1,2
, we have that

\displaystyle\|D_{z}\gamma(z_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1})-D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{D\gamma,p}\left(\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|+W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right),

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1})(y_{2})-\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})(y_{2})\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{D\gamma,p}\left(\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|+\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|+W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right).

Proof.

Let
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})
be an atomless Polish probability space. Then, for any
u,v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
t\in(0,1)
and
Y_{i}\in L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})
with
\mathcal{L}(Y_{i})=\mathfrak{m}_{i}
for
i=1,2
, the Mean Value Theorem given in Lemma
9
implies that

u^{T}\left(D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})-D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right)v=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[u_{i}\,v^{T}\partial_{z_{i}}\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{2},\mathcal{L}(Y_{t}))(Y_{t})(Y_{1}-Y_{2})\right],

where
Y_{t}=Y_{2}+t(Y_{1}-Y_{2})
. Therefore, Hölder’s inequality implies that for any
p,q\in[1,\infty]
such that
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
, we have

\displaystyle u^{T}\left(D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})-D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right)v=\left\|\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{d}u_{i}\partial_{z_{i}}\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{2},\mathcal{L}(Y_{t}))(Y_{t})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\right\|_{L^{q}(\widetilde{\Omega})}\|Y_{1}-Y_{2}\|_{L^{p}(\widetilde{\Omega})}.

Hence, by Lemma
13
and since
\|u\|=1
,

\displaystyle u^{T}\left(D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})-D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right)v=2R_{1}(2+3R_{1}R_{z})\left\|\frac{\exp(z^{T}Y_{t})}{\gamma_{2}\left(z,\mathcal{L}(Y_{t})\right)}\right\|_{L^{q}(\widetilde{\Omega})}\|Y_{2}-Y_{1}\|_{L^{p}(\widetilde{\Omega})}.

Evaluating the above expression at the coupling between
Y_{1},Y_{2}
that attains the
p
-Wasserstein distance, we obtain

u^{T}\left(D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})-D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right)v\leq 3R_{1}(1+2R_{1}R_{z})\exp(2R_{z}R_{1}p^{-1})W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2}),

where we have used
\|Y_{t}\|\leq R_{1}

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. and
1-q^{-1}=p^{-1}
to produce the bound

\left\|\frac{\exp(z^{T}Y_{t})}{\gamma_{2}(z,\mathcal{L}(Y_{t}))}\right\|_{L^{q}(\widetilde{\Omega})}=\left(\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\exp(z^{T}Y_{t})}{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[\exp(z^{T}Y_{t})\right]}\cdot\frac{\exp((q-1)z^{T}Y_{t})}{(\gamma_{2}(z,\mathcal{L}(Y_{t})))^{q-1}}\right]\right)^{1/q}\leq\exp\left(2R_{1}R_{z}p^{-1}\right).

The preceding bound on the difference between
D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})
and
D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})
holds for every
u,v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and thus it holds for the supremum over
u,v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. This supremum attains the operator norm, which implies that

\|D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})-D_{z}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 3R_{1}(1+2R_{1}R_{z})\exp(2R_{z}R_{1}p^{-1})W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2}).

(37)

Automatically from Lemma
13
,
D_{z}\gamma
is
8R_{1}^{3}
-Lipschitz continuous in
z
on
\bar{B}(R_{1})\times\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1}))
. Therefore,
D_{z}\gamma
is Lipschitz continuous on
\bar{B}(R_{1})\times\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1}))
with Lipschitz constant that is the maximum of
8R_{1}^{3}
and the constant in equation (
37
).

Now, consider finding the local Lipschitz constant of
\partial_{\mu}\gamma
. By the Mean Value Theorem on
\mathbb{R}
, for every
u,v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, there exists
t\in(0,1)
with
z_{t}=z_{1}+t(z_{2}-z_{1})
such that

u^{T}\left(\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1})(y_{1})-\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})(y_{1})\right)v\leq\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{d}(z_{1}-z_{2})_{i}\partial_{z_{i}}\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{t},\mathfrak{m}_{1})(y_{1})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Hence, applying Lemma
13
and then taking the supremum over
u,v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, we obtain

\|\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1})(y_{1})-\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})(y_{1})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2R_{1}(2+3R_{1}R_{z})\exp(2R_{z}R_{1})\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|,

(38)

since for any
z_{1},z_{2}\in\bar{B}(R_{z})
so too is
z_{t}\in\bar{B}(R_{z})
. For the Lipschitz continuity of
\partial_{\mu}\gamma
in measure and
y
, we use the expression for
\partial_{\mu}\gamma
given in (
36
) and add and subtract suitable terms to get

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})(y_{1})-\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})(y_{2})\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|z_{2}\|}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})}\left\|\exp(z_{2}^{T}y_{1})\,y_{1}-\exp(z_{2}^{T}y_{2})\,y_{2}\right\|

\displaystyle+\frac{|\exp(z_{2}^{T}y_{1})-\exp(z_{2}^{T}y_{2})|}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})}

\displaystyle+\left|\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})}\right|\exp(z_{2}^{T}y_{2})\left(1+2R_{1}R_{z}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\exp(z_{2}^{T}y_{2})}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})}\|\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})-\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\|\|z_{2}\|,

(39)

Since
y_{1},y_{2}\in\bar{B}(R_{1})
,
z_{2}\in\bar{B}(R_{z})
and
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1}))
, the first term in the expression above is bounded via

\frac{\|z_{2}\|}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})}\left\|\exp(z_{2}^{T}y_{1})\,y_{1}-\exp(z_{2}^{T}y_{2})\,y_{2}\right\|\leq R_{z}\exp(2R_{1}R_{z})\left(1+R_{1}R_{z}\right)\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|.

Similarly, the second term is bounded by

\frac{|\exp(z_{2}^{T}y_{1})-\exp(z_{2}^{T}y_{2})|}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})}\leq\exp(2R_{1}R_{z})R_{z}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|.

For the third term, take any coupling
\bm{m}\in\Pi(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})
. By applying Hölder’s inequality for any
p,q\in[1,\infty]
with
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\int\exp(z_{2}^{T}y_{1})|1-\exp(z_{2}^{T}(y_{2}-y_{1}))|\,d\bm{m}(y_{1},y_{2})}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left(\int\exp(qz_{2}^{T}y_{1})d\mathfrak{m}_{1}\right)^{1/q}}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})}\frac{\exp(2R_{z}R_{1})R_{z}}{\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})}\left(\int\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|^{p}d\bm{m}(y_{1},y_{2})\right)^{1/p}.

Therefore, by taking the coupling
\bm{m}\in\Pi(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})
that attains
W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})
yields

\left|\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})}\right|\leq R_{z}\exp((3+2p^{-1})R_{z}R_{1})W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2}),

(40)

Finally, by applying
\Lambda_{\mu,p}
-Lipschitz continuous of
\gamma
with respect to its measure argument, Corollary
2
, to the final term of (
39
) and the preceding estimates to bound the other terms of (
39
), we deduce that there exists a constant
\Lambda_{\gamma\mu,p}^{\prime}=\Lambda_{\gamma\mu,p}^{\prime}(R_{1},R_{z})
such that

\|\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{1})(y_{1})-\partial_{\mu}\gamma(z_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})(y_{2})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\Lambda_{\gamma\mu,p}^{\prime}\left(\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|+W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right).

(41)

Hence, combining bounds (
38
) and (
41
) yields that
\partial_{\mu}\gamma
is jointly Lipschitz continuous on

\bar{B}(R_{z})\times\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1}))\times\bar{B}(R_{1})
.
∎

Lemma 16
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}(R)
be defined by
24
. Then, for
x,y\in\bar{B}(R_{1})
,
\theta\in\mathfrak{S}(R_{2})
,
a\in\bar{B}(R_{3})
,
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1})),\mathfrak{r}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1})\times\bar{B}(R_{3}))
and
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{S}(R_{2}))
, we have that

\displaystyle\|\Gamma(x,\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n})\|

\displaystyle\leq R_{1}R_{2}^{2},\qquad\|\mathcal{K}(x,\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{n},a)\|\leq R_{3}\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}.

where
\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}
is independent of
R_{3}
and defined as

\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}:=R_{2}^{2}\left(2\beta R_{1}^{2}R_{2}^{2}+(1+2\beta R_{2}^{2}R_{1}^{2})\exp(2\beta R_{1}^{2}R_{2}^{2})\right).

Proof.

The integral triangle inequality gives

\displaystyle\|\Gamma(x,\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n})\|

\displaystyle\leq\iint\frac{\exp(\beta\langle\theta_{Q}x,\theta_{K}y\rangle)}{\gamma_{2}(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}x,\mathfrak{m})}\|\theta_{O}^{T}\theta_{V}y\|d\mathfrak{m}(y)d\mathfrak{n}(\theta)\leq R_{2}^{2}R_{1}.

Recall that
\mathcal{K}
, defined in equation (
12
), can be decomposed into two term. For the first term
\nabla_{x}\mathcal{H}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x}\mathcal{H}(x,\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},a)\|

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|a\|\int\|\theta_{O}^{T}\theta_{V}D_{x}\gamma(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}x,\mathfrak{m})\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}\|_{\mathrm{op}}d\mathfrak{n}(\theta)\leq 2\beta R_{2}^{4}R_{1}^{2}R_{3},

(42)

where we have used the bound on the operator norm of
D_{z}\gamma
given in Lemma
10
. Then, for the second term of
\mathcal{K}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\int\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}(y,\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},p)(x)d\mathfrak{r}(y,p)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\iint\|\theta_{O}^{T}\theta_{V}\partial_{\mu}\gamma(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}y,\mathfrak{m})(x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|p\|d\mathfrak{n}(\theta)d\mathfrak{r}(y,p).

Recall the expression for
\partial_{\mu}\gamma
given in Lemma
1
, which is bounded via

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\mu}\gamma(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}y,\mathfrak{m})(x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\exp(\beta\langle\theta_{Q}y,\theta_{K}x\rangle)}{\gamma_{2}(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}y,\mathfrak{m})}\{\|x-\gamma(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}y,\mathfrak{m})\|\|\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}y\|+1\}

(43)

\displaystyle\leq(1+2\beta R_{2}^{2}R_{1}^{2})\exp(2\beta R_{1}^{2}R_{2}^{2})

By combining the two preceding estimates, we get

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}(y,\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},p)(x)d\mathfrak{r}(y,p)\bigg\|

\displaystyle\leq R_{3}R_{2}^{2}(1+2\beta R_{2}^{2}R_{1}^{2})\exp(2\beta R_{1}^{2}R_{2}^{2})

(44)

The result concludes by noting that
\mathcal{K}
is bounded above by the sum of the terms on the left-hand sides of equations (
42
) and (
44
), and so
\mathcal{K}
is bounded above by the sum of the right-hand sides of these equations.
∎

Lemma 17
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}(R)
be defined by
24
and, for
i=1,2
, take
x_{i}\in\bar{B}(R_{1})
,
\mathfrak{m}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1}))
and
\mathfrak{n}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{S}(R_{2}))
. Then, for every
p\geq 1
, there exists a constant
\Lambda_{p}=\Lambda_{p}(R_{1},R_{2})
such that

\displaystyle\|\Gamma(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1})

\displaystyle-\Gamma(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\|\leq\Lambda_{p}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})+W_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\right),

(45)

\displaystyle\|D_{x}\Gamma(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1})

\displaystyle-D_{x}\Gamma(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\|_{\mathrm{F}}

(46)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{p}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})+W_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\right),

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\mu}\Gamma(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1})(y_{1})

\displaystyle-\partial_{\mu}\Gamma(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2})(y_{2})\|_{\mathrm{F}}

(47)

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{p}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|+W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})+W_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\right).

Proof.

Applying the
2R_{1}^{2}
and
\Lambda_{\mu,p}
-Lipchitz continuity of
\gamma
with respect to the
z
and
m
variables, respectively, from Lemma
10
and Corollary
2
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\Gamma(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1})

\displaystyle-\Gamma(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{1})\|

(48)

\displaystyle\leq\int\|\theta_{O}^{T}\theta_{V}(\gamma(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1})-\gamma(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2}))\|d\mathfrak{n}_{1}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq R_{2}^{2}\left(2\beta R_{2}^{2}R_{1}^{2}\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+\Lambda_{\mu,p}(\beta R_{1}R_{2}^{2})W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right).

Take any
\theta,\vartheta\in\mathfrak{S}(R_{2})
and write
A_{1}=\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q},A_{2}=\beta\vartheta_{K}^{T}\vartheta_{Q}
,
U_{1}=\theta_{O}^{T}\theta_{V},U_{2}=\vartheta_{O}^{T}\vartheta_{V}
. Then, the
2R^{2}_{1}
-Lipschitz continuity of
\gamma
in
z
and boundedness of
\gamma
implies that

\displaystyle\left\|U_{1}\gamma(A_{1}x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})-U_{2}\gamma(A_{2}x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq R_{1}\|U_{1}-U_{2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}+2R^{3}_{1}R_{2}^{2}\|A_{1}-A_{2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

(49)

\displaystyle\leq 2R_{1}R_{2}(1\vee 2\beta R_{1}^{2}R_{2}^{2})\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

The final inequality results from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, which gives

\|U_{1}-U_{2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq R_{2}(\|\theta_{O}-\vartheta_{U}\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\|\theta_{V}-\vartheta_{V}\|_{\mathrm{F}})
, similalry for
\|A_{1}-A_{2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
. Then, applying
v_{1}+\ldots+v_{4}\leq 2\sqrt{v_{1}^{2}+\ldots+v_{4}^{2}}
to write the bound in terms of
\vartheta,\theta
rather than its blocks. By integrating equation (
49
) with respect to a
\bm{\nu}\in\Pi(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})
that attains the
1
-Wasserstein distance between
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}
, we get

\displaystyle\|\Gamma(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{1})-\Gamma(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\Gamma}^{\prime}W_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}),

(50)

where
\Lambda_{\Gamma}^{\prime}=2R_{1}R_{2}(1\vee 2\beta R_{1}^{2}R_{2}^{2})
.
Therefore, claim (
45
) is deduced by adding the estimates given in equations (
48
) and (
50
). In Corollary
15
, we deduced that
D_{z}\gamma
is
\Lambda_{D\gamma,p}
-locally Lipchitz continuous with respect to both variables. Therefore, by applying this estimate with
R_{z}=\beta R_{1}R_{2}^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|D_{x}\Gamma

\displaystyle(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1})-D_{x}\Gamma(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{1})\|_{\mathrm{F}}

(51)
