\displaystyle\leq\beta\int\left\|\theta_{O}^{T}\theta_{V}\left(D_{z}\gamma(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1})-(D_{z}\gamma(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right)\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}d\mathfrak{n}_{1}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq\beta R_{2}^{4}\left(\beta R_{2}^{2}\Lambda_{D\gamma,p}\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+\Lambda_{D\gamma,p}W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right).

Recall
U_{1},U_{2},A_{1},A_{2}
defined in the argument for the Lipschitz continuity of
\Gamma
. After applying the
8R_{1}^{3}
local Lipschitz continuity of
D_{z}\gamma
in
z
, Lemma
13
, and the fact that
D_{z}\gamma
is bounded by
2R_{1}^{2}
, see Lemma
10
, we get

\displaystyle\|U_{1}D_{z}\gamma(A_{1}x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})A_{1}

\displaystyle-U_{2}D_{z}\gamma(A_{2}x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})A_{2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

(52)

\displaystyle\leq 2R^{2}_{1}R_{2}^{2}\left(\beta\|U_{1}-U_{2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\|A_{1}-A_{2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\right)+8\beta R_{1}^{4}R_{2}^{4}\|A_{1}-A_{2}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\beta R^{2}_{1}R_{2}^{3}\left(\|\theta_{O}-\vartheta_{U}\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\|\theta_{V}-\vartheta_{V}\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\|\theta_{Q}-\vartheta_{Q}\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\|\theta_{K}-\vartheta_{K}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\right)

\displaystyle+8\beta^{2}R_{1}^{4}R_{2}^{5}\left(\|\theta_{Q}-\vartheta_{Q}\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\|\theta_{K}-\vartheta_{K}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\right).

The final inequality results from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and expanding the definition of
U_{i},A_{i}
. Take any
\bm{\nu}\in\Pi(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})
that attains the
1
-Wasserstein distance
W_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})
. Since the sum of the Fröbenius norms on the blocks of parameters corresponding to the
Q,K,V,O
matrices is bounded above by twice the Fröbenius norm between
\theta,\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
, integrating the expression given on the left-hand side of equation (
52
) with respect to
\bm{\nu}
gives

\|D_{x}\Gamma(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{1})-D_{x}\Gamma(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq 4R_{1}^{2}R_{2}^{3}\beta(1+4\beta R_{1}^{2}R_{2}^{2})W_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}).

Hence, Claim (
46
) is deduced by combining the above equation with (
51
).

The final claim on the Lipschitz continuity of
\partial_{\mu}\Gamma
follows identically, by replacing
D_{z}\gamma
by
\partial_{\mu}\gamma
in the above estimates and using the second result of Corollary
15
.
∎

Lemma 18
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}(R)
be defined by
24
and, for
i=1,2
, suppose
x_{i}\in\bar{B}(R_{1}),a_{i}\in\bar{B}(R_{3}),

\mathfrak{m}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1})),\mathfrak{n}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{S}(R_{2}))
and
\theta,\vartheta\in\mathfrak{S}(R_{2})
. Then, for every
p\geq 1
, there exists a constant
\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,p}=\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,p}(\beta,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
such that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1},a_{1})(\theta)

\displaystyle-\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2},a_{2})(\vartheta)\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,p}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+\|a_{1}-a_{2}\|+W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})+\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\mathrm{F}}\right).

Proof.

Denote the first variation (Definition 5.43 in
[
8
]
Vol. I) of
\mathcal{H}
in
\nu
by
\delta\mathcal{H}/\delta\nu
, which is given by

\frac{\delta\mathcal{H}}{\delta\nu}(x,\mu,\nu,a)(\theta)=a^{T}\theta_{O}^{T}\theta_{V}\gamma(\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}x,\mu).

Lions’ derivative
\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}
coincides with the gradient of the first variation of
\mathcal{H}
, see Proposition 5.48 in
[
8
]
Volume I. Therefore, writing
A_{1}=\beta\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}
and
A_{2}=\beta\vartheta_{K}^{T}\vartheta_{Q}
, the difference between the gradients corresponding to the
O
parameters is bounded by

\displaystyle\bigg\|\nabla_{\theta_{O}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{H}}{\delta\nu}(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1}

\displaystyle,a_{1})(\theta)-\nabla_{\theta_{O}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{H}}{\delta\nu}\mathcal{H}(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2},a_{2})(\vartheta)\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\theta_{V}\gamma(A_{1}x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1}))a_{1}^{T}-(\vartheta_{V}\gamma(A_{2}x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2}))a_{2}^{T}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq R_{1}R_{2}\|a_{1}-a_{2}\|+R_{1}R_{3}\|\theta_{V}-\vartheta_{V}\|_{\mathrm{F}}+R_{2}R_{3}\Lambda_{\mu,p}(\beta R_{1}R_{2}^{2})W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})

\displaystyle+2\beta R_{3}R_{1}^{2}\|\theta_{K}^{T}\theta_{Q}x_{1}-\vartheta_{K}^{T}\vartheta_{Q}x_{2}\|,

having applied the Lipschitz estimates of
\gamma
deduced in Appendix
C
.
Therefore, there exists a constant
\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,p}^{\prime}
that depends only on
\beta,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}
such that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\nabla_{\theta_{O}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{H}}{\delta\nu}(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1},a_{1})(\theta)

\displaystyle-\nabla_{\theta_{O}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{H}}{\delta\nu}\mathcal{H}(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2},a_{2})(\vartheta)\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,p}^{\prime}\left(\|a_{1}-a_{2}\|+\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})+\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\mathrm{F}}\right).

Here, we have used the fact that the sum of
\|\theta_{V}-\vartheta_{V}\|_{\mathrm{F}}
and that on the
Q,K
parameters is bounded above by twice
\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\mathrm{F}}
. The Lipschitz continuity of the gradient with respect to the
V
parameters can be deduced and bounded identically. Moreover, the difference between the gradients corresponding to the
K
parameters is bounded by

\displaystyle\bigg\|\nabla_{\theta_{K}}

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\mathcal{H}}{\delta\nu}(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1},a_{1})(\theta)-\nabla_{\theta_{K}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{H}}{\delta\nu}\mathcal{H}(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2},a_{2})(\vartheta)\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|\theta_{Q}x_{1}\,(\theta_{O}a_{1})^{T}\theta_{V}D_{z}\gamma(A_{1}x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1})-\vartheta_{Q}x_{2}(\vartheta_{O}a_{2})^{T}\vartheta_{V}D_{z}\gamma(A_{2}x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

Therefore, by using the boundedness of
D_{z}\gamma
given in Lemma
10
and the Lipschitz continuity of
D_{z}\gamma
shown in Corollary
15
, there exists a constant
\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,p}^{\prime\prime}
that depends only on
\beta,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}
such that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\nabla_{\theta_{K}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{H}}{\delta\nu}(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1},a_{1})(\theta)

\displaystyle-\nabla_{\theta_{K}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{H}}{\delta\nu}\mathcal{H}(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2},a_{2})(\vartheta)\bigg\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,p}^{\prime\prime}\left(\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+\|a_{1}-a_{2}\|+W_{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{2})\right).

The Lipschitz continuity of the gradient with respect to the
Q
parameters can be deduced and bounded identically. Since the squared Fröbenius norm on
\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
is bounded above by the squared Fröbenius norm on each
\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
block corresponding to the
Q,K,V,O
parameters separately,
\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}
is locally Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant
\sqrt{2}\sqrt{(\lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,p}^{\prime})^{2}+(\lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,p}^{\prime\prime})^{2}}
that depends only on
\beta,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}
.
∎

Corollary 19
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}(R)
be defined by
24
and, for
i=1,2
, suppose
x_{i}\in\bar{B}(R_{1}),a_{i}\in\bar{B}(R_{3}),\mathfrak{n}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{S}(R_{2}))
and
\rho_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{1})\times\bar{B}(R_{3}))
. Then, for every
p\geq 1
, there exists a constant

\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}=\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}(\beta,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
such that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{K}(x_{1},\rho_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1},a_{1})-\mathcal{K}(x_{2},\rho_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2},a_{2})\|\leq\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+\|a_{1}-a_{2}\|+W_{p}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+W_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\right).

Proof.

Since
a_{1},a_{2}
are bounded by
R_{3}
, and
D_{x}\Gamma
is bounded by
2\beta R_{1}^{4}R_{1}^{2}
, immediate from equation (
42
), and locally Lipschitz continuous, see Lemma
17
, there exists a constant
\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}^{\prime}=\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}^{\prime}(\beta,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x}\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle(x_{1},\rho_{1}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{1},a_{1})-\nabla_{x}\mathcal{H}(x_{2},\rho_{2}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{2},a_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|a_{1}-a_{2}\|\|D_{x}\Gamma(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1})\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\|a_{2}\|\|D_{x}\Gamma(x_{1},\rho_{1}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{1})-D_{x}\Gamma(x_{2},\rho_{2}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}^{\prime}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+\|a_{1}-a_{2}\|+W_{p}(\rho_{1}|_{x},\rho_{2}|_{x})+W_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\right).

Notice that the discrepancies between the second terms in
\mathcal{K}
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle(y,\rho_{1}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{1},p)(x_{1})d\rho_{1}(y,p)-\int\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}(y,\rho_{2}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{2},p)(x_{2})d\rho_{2}(y,p)\bigg\|

\displaystyle\leq\int\underbrace{\|p\|\left\|\partial_{\mu}\Gamma(y,\rho_{1}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{1})(x_{1})-\partial_{\mu}\Gamma(y,\rho_{2}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{2})(x_{2})\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}}_{(\dagger_{1})}d\rho_{1}(y,p)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\left\|\int\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}(y,\rho_{2}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{2},p)(x_{2})d(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2})(y,p))\right\|}_{(\dagger_{2})}.

For the first term
(\dagger_{1})
: by the local Lipschitz continuity of
\partial_{\mu}\Gamma
, Lemma
17
, and the assumed support of
\rho_{1}
in
\bar{B}(R_{1})\times\bar{B}(R_{3})
, there exists a constant
\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}^{\prime\prime}=\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}^{\prime\prime}(\beta,R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
such that
\rho_{1}
-a.s. we have

(\dagger_{1})\leq\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}^{\prime\prime}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+W_{p}(\rho_{1}|_{x},\rho_{2}|_{x})+W_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\right).

For the second term
(\dagger_{2})
:
By combining the Lipschitz continuity of
\partial_{\mu}\Gamma
, Lemma
17
, and the boundedness of
\partial_{\mu}\gamma
, equation (
43
),
the map
(y,p)\mapsto\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}(y,\rho_{2}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{2},p)(x_{2})
is Lipchitz on
\bar{B}(R_{1})\times\bar{B}(R_{3})
. Therefore, the Kantorovich-Rubinstein Theorem, see
[
51
]
, implies that there exists a constant
\Lambda_{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that

(\dagger_{2})\leq\Lambda_{\kappa}^{\prime\prime\prime}W_{1}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}).

Recall the definition of
\mathcal{K}
in (
12
), which decomposes into the sum of a term involving
\nabla_{x}\mathcal{H}
and a term involving
\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}
. Its Lipschitz constant can be estimated by controlling each term separately to give

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{K}(x_{1},\rho_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1},a_{1})

\displaystyle-\mathcal{K}(x_{2},\rho_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2},a_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla_{x}\mathcal{H}(x_{1},\mathfrak{m}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1},a_{1})-\nabla_{x}\mathcal{H}(x_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2},a_{2})\|

\displaystyle+\left\|\int\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}(y,\rho_{1}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{1},p)(x_{1})d\rho_{1}(y,p)-\int\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}(y,\rho_{2}|_{x},\mathfrak{n}_{2},p)(x_{2})d\rho_{2}(y,p)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}^{\prime}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+\|a_{1}-a_{2}\|+W_{p}(\rho_{1}|_{x},\rho_{2}|_{x})+W_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\right)

\displaystyle+\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}^{\prime\prime}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+W_{p}(\rho_{1}|_{x},\rho_{2}|_{x})+W_{1}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\right)+\Lambda_{\kappa}^{\prime\prime\prime}W_{1}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}).

Then, the desired result holds with
\Lambda_{\mathcal{K}}=(\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}^{\prime}+\Lambda_{\mathcal{K},p}^{\prime\prime}+\Lambda_{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime\prime})
, since the
p
-Wasserstein distance is increasing in
p
by Jensen’s inequality and
W_{p}(\rho_{1}|_{x},\rho_{2}|_{x})\leq W_{p}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
as the projection map onto the
x
marginal in
1
-Lipschitz.
∎

Appendix E
The Global Well-Posedness of the Continuous-Time Model

Let
R_{2}>0
and
\bm{\nu}\in C([0,1],\mathfrak{S}(R_{2}))
, and consider the McKean–Vlasov ODE, with initial condition
\xi\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\|\xi\|\leq R_{0}

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.s., associated to the IPS (
17
) given by

\displaystyle\frac{dY^{\xi}_{t}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(Y^{\xi}_{t},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t}),\nu_{t}),\qquad

\displaystyle Y^{\xi}_{0}=\xi,

(53)

\displaystyle\frac{dp^{\xi}_{t}}{dt}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{K}(Y^{\xi}_{t},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t}),\nu_{t},p^{\xi}_{t}),\qquad

\displaystyle p^{\xi}_{1}=\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{1}))(Y^{\xi}_{1}),

(54)

where
\mathcal{L}^{1}(Z):=\mathbb{P}^{1}\circ Z^{-1}
denotes the law of a random variable
Z
defined on
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
. We search for solutions
(\bm{Y}^{\xi},\bm{p}^{\xi})
taking values in

\mathscr{C}^{1,2,2d}:=L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};C([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{2d})),

equipped with the norm

\|\bm{Z}\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{1,2,d}}=\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|Z_{t}\|^{2}\right],\qquad\bm{Z}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2,d}.

Here, the superscript one on
\mathscr{C}^{1,2,d}
indicates that the random variables are defined on
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
. When this superscript is zero or omitted,
\mathscr{C}^{0,2,2d}
and
\mathscr{C}^{2,2d}
denote the corresponding spaces defined on
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
and
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
instead.

Since the velocity fields
\Gamma,\mathcal{K}
are only locally Lipschitz continuous in
x
and
\rho
, we truncate the velocity fields to restore global Lipschitz continuity. With this in mind, let us introduce

\Gamma^{R}(x,\mu,\nu)=\Gamma(P_{R}(x),P_{R}{}_{\#}\mu,\nu),\qquad\mathcal{K}^{R}(x,\rho,\nu,a)=\mathcal{K}(P_{R}(x),P_{R,R}{}_{\#}\rho,\nu,P_{R}(a)),

where
P_{R}
is defined in equation (
6
) and

P_{R,R}((x,a))=(P_{R}(x),P_{R}(a)).

Recall that
P_{R}
is
2
-Lipschitz continuous and is bounded by the minimum of
2(R\vee 1)
and
\|x\|
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Hence, with
x_{i},a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\rho_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{S}(R_{2}))
for
i=1,2
, the following Lipschitz bounds hold:

\displaystyle\|\Gamma^{R}(x_{1},\mu_{1},\nu)

\displaystyle-\Gamma^{R}(x_{2},\mu_{2},\nu)\|\leq 2\Lambda^{R}_{\Gamma}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+W_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\right),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{K}^{R}(x_{1},\rho_{1},\nu,a_{1})

\displaystyle-\mathcal{K}^{R}(x_{2},\rho_{2},\nu,a_{2})\|\leq 2\Lambda^{R}_{\Gamma}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+\|a_{1}-a_{2}\|+W_{2}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\right),

where
\Lambda^{R}_{\Gamma},\Lambda^{R}_{\mathcal{K}}
are the constants from Lemma
17
and Corollary
19
evaluated at
p=2
,
R_{2}
as specified and
R_{1}=R_{3}=2(R\vee 1)
. We will see that the solution to equations (
53
)-(
54
) is
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.s bounded, and so the truncation maps
P_{R}
have no effect, if
R
is taken sufficiently large.

Lemma 20
.

Let
R_{2}>0
and
\bm{\nu}\in C([0,1],\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{S}(R_{2})))
. Then, for every
\xi\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\|\xi\|\leq R_{0}

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.s., there exists a solution
(\bm{Y}^{\xi},\bm{p}^{\xi})\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2,2d}
unique up to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.s. equality, to the forward-backward system of McKean–Vlasov ODEs (
53
)-(
54
). Furthermore, there exist constants
R_{X}=R_{X}(R_{0},R_{2})
and
R_{a}=R_{a}(\beta,R_{0},R_{2},K)
such that
Y^{\xi}_{t}\in\bar{B}(R_{X})
and

p^{\xi}_{t}\in\bar{B}(R_{a})
for every
t\in[0,1]
,
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-almost surely.

Proof.

Step One: Existence and Uniqueness of
\bm{Y}^{\xi}

The mapping
(x,\mu,t)\mapsto\Gamma^{R}(x,\mu,\nu_{t})
satisfies Assumption
(MKV SDE)
in Section 4.2.1 of
[
8
]
Vol. I, with identically zero diffusion coefficient
\Sigma
. Therefore, Theorem 4.21 in
[
8
]
Vol. I applies and proves that there exists a unique solution
\bm{Y}^{\xi,R}=(Y^{\xi,R}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2,d}
to (
53
), when
\Gamma
is replaced by
\Gamma^{R}
. Then, applying Lemma
16
to bound
\Gamma
and the fact that
\|\xi\|\leq R_{0}

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.s., we obtain that

\displaystyle\|Y^{\xi,R}_{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\xi\|+\int_{0}^{t}\|\Gamma(P_{R}(Y^{\xi,R}_{s}),P_{R}{}_{\#}\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi,R}_{s}),\nu_{s})\|ds

\displaystyle\leq R_{0}+R_{2}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}W_{\infty}(\delta_{0},P_{R}{}_{\#}\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi,R}_{s}))ds,\qquad\mathbb{P}^{1}-a.s.

The right-hand side is deterministic, and so for every
q\geq 1

\displaystyle W_{q}(\delta_{0},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi,R}_{t}))

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathbb{E}^{1}[\|Y^{\xi,R}_{t}\|^{q}]\right)^{1/q}\leq R_{0}+R_{2}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}W_{\infty}(\delta_{0},P_{R}{}_{\#}\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi,R}_{s}))ds.

Since
\|P_{R}(x)\|\leq\|x\|
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for every
s\in[0,1]
, we have that

W_{\infty}(\delta_{0},P_{R}{}_{\#}\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi,R}_{s})))\leq W_{\infty}(\delta_{0},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi,R}_{s}))).

By substituting this into the above inequality and passing to the limit as
q\to\infty
yields

W_{\infty}(\delta_{0},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi,R}_{t}))\leq R_{0}+R_{2}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}W_{\infty}(\delta_{0},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi,R}_{s}))ds.

By applying Grönwall’s lemma, we get

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}W_{\infty}(\delta_{0},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi,R}_{t}))\leq R_{0}\exp(R_{2}^{2})=:R_{X}(R_{0},R_{2}).

As a result,
Y^{\xi,R}_{t}\in\bar{B}(R_{X})
for every
t\in[0,1]

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.s., which implies that
P_{R}
acts trivially on
\bm{Y}^{\xi,R}
, whenever
R>R_{X}
. Therefore,
\bm{Y}^{\xi,R}
satisfies (
53
)
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.s., whenever
R>R_{X}
.

Step Two: Existence and Uniqueness of
\bm{p}^{\xi}

Let
\bm{Y}^{\xi}
be the unique solution to (
53
). Since
Y^{\xi}_{1}\in\bar{B}(R_{X})

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.s., the terminal condition

p_{1}^{\xi}=\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{1}))(Y^{\xi}_{1})\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

In fact, Assumption
2
yields that
p_{1}^{\xi}\in\bar{B}(K(R_{X}))

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.s. Furthermore, the mapping

\Omega^{1}\times[0,1]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni(\omega^{1},t,\rho,p)\mapsto-\mathcal{K}^{R}(Y^{\xi}_{t}(\omega^{1}),\rho,\nu_{t},p)

is Lipschitz in variables
(\rho,p)
with respect to the
W_{2}
-metric and the Euclidean metric, respectively. Furthermore,
\bm{Y}^{\xi}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2,d}
implies that
Y^{\xi}_{t}
is continuous in
t
for
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.e.
\omega^{1}
and is
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-measurable for fixed
t
. After modifying
\bm{Y}^{\xi}
on a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-null set, if necessary, to make it continuous in
t
for all
\omega^{1}\in\Omega^{1}
,
Y^{\xi}_{t}
is a Carathéodory function, Definition 4.1 in
[
48
]
, thus it is
(\mathcal{F}^{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}([0,1]))
-measurable by Theorem 4.1 in
[
48
]
. Since
\mathcal{K}^{R}
is continuous in its first and third variable, the above mapping is
(\mathcal{F}^{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}([0,1]))
-measurable, for fixed
(\rho,p)\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Accordingly, after interchanging time
t\leftrightarrow 1-t
, the terminal condition and velocity field
\mathcal{K}^{R}
satisfy Assumption
(MKV SDE)
in Section 4.2.1 of
[
8
]
Vol. I, with identically zero diffusion coefficient. Note that this time reversal is only valid when the forward process has no diffusion term. More care is required when diffusion is present; See Theorem 4.23 in
[
8
]
Vol. I. Hence, by Theorem 4.21 together with Remark 4.22 in
[
8
]
Vol. I., with
Y^{\xi}_{t}
in place of their
\zeta_{t}
, we deduce that there exists a unique solution
\bm{p}^{\xi,R}
to (
54
), when
\mathcal{K}
is replaced by
\mathcal{K}^{R}
.

By applying
p_{1}^{\xi}\in\bar{B}(K(R_{X}))
, as discussed at the start of
Step Two
, we have that

\displaystyle\|p_{t}^{\xi,R}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}(Y^{\xi}_{1}))(Y^{\xi}_{1})\|+\int^{1}_{t}\|\mathcal{K}^{R}(Y^{\xi}_{s},\mathcal{L}(Y^{\xi}_{s},p^{\xi,R}_{s}),\nu_{s},p_{s}^{\xi,R})ds

\displaystyle\leq K(R_{X})+\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}\int_{t}^{1}W_{\infty}(\delta_{0},P_{R}{}_{\#}\mathcal{L}^{1}(p^{\xi,R}_{s}))ds,\qquad\mathbb{P}^{1}-a.s.,

where
\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}
in the final inequality is the constant from Lemma
16
. By the same Grönwall argument used in
Step One
, we deduce that

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}W_{\infty}(\delta_{0},p^{\xi,R}_{t})\leq K(R_{X})\exp(\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}})=:R_{a}.

Therefore,
p^{\xi,R}_{t}\in\bar{B}(R_{a})
for all
t\in[0,1]

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.s., which implies that
P_{R}
acts trivially on
\bm{p}^{\xi,R}
whenever
R>R_{a}
. As a result, the pair
(\bm{Y}^{\xi,R},\bm{p}^{\xi,R})
satisfies (
53
)-(
54
)
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-a.s. when
R
is taken greater than the maximum of
R_{X}
and
R_{a}
.
∎

Lemma
20
establishes the existence and uniqueness for the McKean–Vlasov ODEs associated with the transformer in the case where the mean-field parameters
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}
are deterministic. However,
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}
depends on the sampling of training data, and so we model
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}
as a random curve of measures that takes values in
C([0,1];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}))
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
and
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
be a measurable space. Proposition 5.7 in
[
8
]
Vol. I states that
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is generated by the evaluation maps
(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ni\mu\mapsto\mu(D))
for all
D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Therefore, a map
M:\widetilde{\Omega}\to\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable if

\{\tilde{\omega}\in\tilde{\Omega}\,\colon\,M(\tilde{\omega})(D)\in B\}=M^{-1}(\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\colon\mu(D)\in B\})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},

for every
D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
. This occurs if the following criterion is satisfied.

Criterion 1
.

A map
M:\widetilde{\Omega}\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is
(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
-measurable if
\widetilde{\Omega}\ni\tilde{\omega}\mapsto M(\tilde{\omega})(D)
is
(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}))
-measurable for every
D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=C([0,1],\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and write
e_{t}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the evaluation map
e_{t}\bm{\mathfrak{m}}=\mathfrak{m}_{t}
. We equip
\mathcal{C}
with the metric

\mathcal{D}(\bm{\mu}^{1},\bm{\mu}^{2})=\sup_{t\in[0,1]}W_{2}(e_{t}\bm{\mu}^{1},e_{t}\bm{\mu}^{2}),

(55)

which makes
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})

a separable metric space, Corollary 4.2.18 in
[
17
]
. Accordingly, there exists a countably dense subset
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
such that any open set in
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
can be expressed as a countable union of sets of the form

\displaystyle\left\{\bm{\mathfrak{\mu}}\in\mathcal{C}\,|\mathcal{D}(\bm{\mathfrak{m}},\bm{\mathfrak{\mu}})<q\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{t\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]}\left\{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}\,|\,W_{2}(e_{t}\bm{\mathfrak{m}},e_{t}\bm{\mathfrak{\mu}})<q\right\},

for
q\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,\infty)=:\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, see Appendix M3 in
[
41
]
for further details. The equality uses the continuity of the map
t\mapsto\mathfrak{m}_{t}
to replace the supremum over
[0,1]
by the supremum over any dense subset. Therefore, any open set of
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
can be expressed as a countable union and intersection of sets of the form

\left\{\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}\,|\,W_{2}(e_{t}\bm{\mathfrak{m}},e_{t}\bm{\mathfrak{\mu}})<q\right\},

for
t\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
,
q\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
. This implies that

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C})\subseteq\sigma\left(\{\bm{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}\colon W_{2}(e_{t}\bm{\mathfrak{m}},e_{t}\bm{\mu})<q\}:t\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1],q\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0},\bm{\mu}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\right).

Take any
\widetilde{\bm{\mathfrak{m}}}:\widetilde{\Omega}\to\mathcal{C}
. The above demonstrates that
\widetilde{\bm{\mathfrak{m}}}
is
(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C}))
-measurable if

\left\{\tilde{\omega}\in\widetilde{\Omega}:W_{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{t}(\tilde{\omega}),e_{t}\bm{\mu})<q\right\}=\widetilde{\bm{\mathfrak{m}}}^{-1}(\{\bm{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathcal{C}:W_{2}(e_{t}\bm{\mathfrak{m}},e_{t}\bm{\mu})<q\})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},

for every
t\in[0,1]
,
q\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
and
\bm{\mu}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
. This occurs whenever
\tilde{\omega}\mapsto\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}_{t}}(\tilde{\omega})
is
(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
.
By combining this with Criterion
1
for measurability with respect to
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we deduce the following.

Criterion 2
.

\widetilde{\bm{\mathfrak{m}}}:\widetilde{\Omega}\mapsto\mathcal{C}
is
(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C}))
-measurable if the map

\widetilde{\Omega}\ni\tilde{\omega}\mapsto\tilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{t}(\tilde{\omega})(D)

is
(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}))
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
and
D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Let us generalise Lemma
20
to include random mean-field parameters
\bm{\nu}
. We do this by working on the product probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, defined in Section
3.4
, so that the randomness due to
\bm{\nu}
and initial condition
\xi
are defined on separate spaces
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0})
and
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1})
, respectively, which are then canonically embedded into
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
. As discussed in Section 2.1.3 in
[
8
]
Vol. II, the section
X(\omega^{0},\cdot)
of a random variable
X
defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, where
\mathcal{F}
is the completion of
\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1}
, is only a random variable on
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1})
for
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.e.
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
. Accordingly, the law of
X(\omega^{0},\cdot)
is only well-defined
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s.. However, we choose the initial condition
\xi
and
\bm{\nu}
to be independent so that all variables that follow can be defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
, which makes their section a random variable defined on
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1})
for all
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
. For a random variable that is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable, it is well-defined to denote the conditional law of
X
on
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
by
\mathcal{L}^{1}(X)(\omega^{0})=\mathbb{P}^{1}\circ X(\omega^{0},\cdot)^{-1}
, Section 2.1.3 in
[
8
]
Vol. II.

Lemma 21
.

Let
R_{2}>0
and
\bm{\nu}=(\nu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be a stochastic process on the probability space
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
such that:

•

The map
(\omega^{0},t)\mapsto\nu_{t}(\omega^{0})
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{B}([0,1]),\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}))
-measurable.

•

There exists a subset
\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}\subseteq\Omega^{0}
with
\mathbb{P}^{0}(\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}})=1
so that
\bm{\nu}(\omega^{0})\in C([0,1],\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{S}(R_{2})))
for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
, where
\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{S}(R_{2}))
is endowed by the topology induced by the
W_{2}
metric.

Then, for any initial condition
\xi\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\|\xi\|\leq R_{0}

\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s., there exist a solution
(\bm{Y}^{\xi},\bm{p}^{\xi})\in\mathscr{C}^{2,2d}
, unique up to
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. equality, to the forward-backward system of McKean–Vlasov ODEs

\displaystyle\frac{dY^{\xi}_{t}}{dt}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(Y^{\xi}_{t},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t}),\nu_{t}),\qquad

\displaystyle Y^{\xi}_{0}=\xi,

(56)

\displaystyle\frac{dp^{\xi}_{t}}{dt}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{K}(Y^{\xi}_{t},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t}),\nu_{t},p^{\xi}_{t}),\qquad

\displaystyle p^{\xi}_{1}=\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{1}))(Y^{\xi}_{1}).

(57)

Furthermore, the constants
R_{X},R_{a}
, defined in Lemma
20
, are such that
Y_{t}^{\xi}\in\bar{B}(R_{X})
and
p^{\xi}_{t}\in\bar{B}(R_{a})
for every
t\in[0,1]

\mathbb{P}
-almost surely.

Remark 22
.

We require that
\xi
and
(\nu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
are independent. This occurs in practice since we assume that the training data at step
\tau
is sampled independently of the past training data. However,
we take
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
, despite having introduced
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
in Section
3.4
as the source of randomness from parameter initialisation. This is done so that an additional probability space does not have to be introduced, whilst keeping
\xi
and
\bm{\nu}
independent.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we shall take
R>R_{X}\vee R_{a}
, where
R_{X},R_{a}
are the constants defined in Lemma
20
. This ensures that the solution
(\bm{Y}^{\xi},\bm{p}^{\xi})
to (
56
)-(
57
) coincides
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. with the corresponding solution
(\bm{Y}^{\xi,R},\bm{p}^{\xi,R})
when
\Gamma,\mathcal{K}
are replaced by
\Gamma^{R},\mathcal{K}^{R}
.

Step One: Existence of
(\bm{Y}^{\xi},\bm{p}^{\xi})

Given
\xi\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1};\bar{B}(R_{0}))
that is canonically embedded as a random variable on

(\Omega,\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
, the assumptions of Lemma
20
are satisfied by
\xi
and the mean-field parameters
(\nu_{t}(\omega^{0}))_{t\in[0,1]}
whenever
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
. Thus, there exists
(\bm{Y}^{\xi},\bm{p}^{\xi})(\omega^{0},\cdot)\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2,2d}
such that (
56
)-(
57
) is satisfied for
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.e.
\omega^{0}
. Since we cannot guarantee the existence of the solution to (
56
)-(
57
) on
(\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}})^{c}
, define

(\bm{Y}^{\xi},\bm{p}^{\xi})(\omega^{0},\cdot)=\left((\xi,\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi))(\xi))\right)_{t\in[0,1]},\qquad\omega^{0}\notin\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}.

This guarantees that
(\bm{Y}^{\xi},\bm{p}^{\xi})(\omega^{0},\cdot)\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2,2d}
for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
, as Assumption
2
implies that for
\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi))(\xi)\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1})
.

The uniqueness and boundedness claims are immediate consequences of Lemma
20
.

Step Two: Measurability of
(\bm{Y}^{\xi},\bm{p}^{\xi})

Fix
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{C}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and consider the ODE

\dot{Y}^{x,\bm{\mu},R}_{t}=\Gamma^{R}(Y^{x,\bm{\mu},R}_{t},\mu_{t},\nu_{t}),\qquad Y_{0}^{x,\bm{\mu},R}=x,

(58)

which has a unique solution
\bm{Y}^{x,\bm{\mu},R}=(Y_{t}^{x,\bm{\mu},R})_{t\in[0,1]}
, by Lemma 8.1.4 in
[
33
]
whenever

\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
. To remain consistent with the convention for
\bm{Y}^{\xi}
on
(\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}})^{c}
, we set
\bm{Y}^{x,\bm{\mu},R}=x
for
\omega^{0}\notin\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
. Now, let us define the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{R}:\Omega^{0}\times L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1};\bar{B}(R_{0}))\times\mathcal{C}\mapsto\mathcal{C},\qquad\mathcal{T}^{R}(\omega^{0},\xi,\bm{\mathfrak{m}})\colon=(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi,\bm{\mathfrak{m}},R}_{t})(\omega^{0}))_{t\in[0,1]},

where
Y^{\xi,\bm{\mathfrak{m}},R}_{t}
refers to
Y^{x,\bm{\mathfrak{m}},R}_{t}
evaluated at
x=\xi
. Notice that
\mathcal{T}^{R}(\omega^{0},\xi,\bm{\mathfrak{m}})=\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi)
for

\omega^{0}\notin\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
. As shown in the proof of Theorem 4.21 in
[
8
]
Vol. I,
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\bm{Y}^{\xi}_{t})(\omega^{0})
coincides with
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}_{t}^{\infty}(\omega^{0})
for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
and
t\in[0,1]
, where
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{\infty}
is obtained as the limit, with respect to metric
\mathcal{D}
, of the Picard iterates

\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{n+1}=\mathcal{T}^{R}(\omega^{0},\xi,\bm{\mathfrak{m}^{n}}),\qquad\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}=(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi))_{t\in[0,1]}.

On
(\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}})^{c}
,
\mathcal{T}^{R}_{\omega^{0},\xi}
is a constant map and
\bm{Y}^{\xi}(\omega^{0})=\xi
. Therefore,
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}_{t}^{n}(\omega^{0})
converges to
\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t})(\omega^{0})
for every
t\in[0,1]
and
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
.

Let us show by induction that
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}
belongs to

\mathfrak{M}=\{\bm{\mathfrak{m}}:\Omega^{0}\mapsto\mathcal{C}\,|\,\bm{\mathfrak{m}}\text{ is }(\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{C}))\text{-measurable}\},

for every
n\geq 0
. The base case
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
is deterministic, and hence
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}\in\mathfrak{M}
. Since measurability is stable under pointwise limits, if
\mathcal{T}^{R}
preserves measurability, then
\mathfrak{m}^{\infty}\in\mathfrak{M}
by induction. Let us use the short hand notation
Y^{x,n,R}_{t}
to denote
Y^{x,\bm{\mu},R}_{t}
evaluated at
\bm{\mu}=\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}
, and suppose that
Y^{\xi,n,R}_{t}
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable. Then, for any
D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the function

\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\mathbbm{1}_{D}(Y^{\xi,n,R}_{t}(\omega))

is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable. Therefore, applying Lemma 1.28 in
[
39
]
, yields that

\Omega^{0}\ni\omega^{0}\mapsto\mathfrak{m}^{n+1}_{t}(D)=\mathbb{P}^{1}\left[Y^{\xi,n,R}_{t}(\omega^{0},\cdot)\in D\right]

is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for every
D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
t\in[0,1]
. By Criterion
2
, this is sufficient to conclude that
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{n+1}\in\mathfrak{M}
. Therefore, to complete the inductive step, we are left to show that
Y^{\xi,n,R}_{t}
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable given that
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}\in\mathfrak{M}
. Consider the
\sigma
-algebra defined by

\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}:=\{\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}\cap A:A\in\mathcal{F}^{0}\}.

Since
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
is a complete probability space and
(\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}})^{c}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{0}
null set,
(\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}})^{c}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
. This implies that
A\cap\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
for every
A\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
, hence
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
is a sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathcal{F}^{0}
. Let
Z
be any random variable defined on
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
that takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and write
Z_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
for its restriction to
\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
. Then, for every
S\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

Z_{\mathrm{w.d.}}^{-1}(S)=\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}\cap\{\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}\colon Z(\omega^{0})\in S\}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}.

Thus, the restriction of any random variable defined on
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0})
to
\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
that takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
-measurable. In particular, if
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}\in\mathfrak{M}
, the velocity field

\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]\ni(\omega^{0},x,t)\mapsto\Gamma^{R}(x,\mathfrak{m}^{n}_{t}(\omega^{0}),\nu_{t}(\omega^{0}))

is continuous in
(x,t)
for fixed
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
, and
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
-measurable for fixed
(x,t)
. Hence, Lemma 2.2 in
[
24
]
applies and yields that
Y^{x,n,R}_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
-measurable for each
t\in[0,1]
.

Notice that for any
S\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(Y^{x,n,R}_{t})^{-1}(S)=\underbrace{\{\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}\colon Y^{x,n,R}_{t}(\omega^{0})\in S\}}_{=:S_{1}}\cup\underbrace{\{\omega^{0}\notin\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}\colon Y^{x,n,R}_{t}(\omega^{0})=x\in S\}}_{=:S_{2}}.

The set
S_{1}
is an element of
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{0}
, since the restriction of
Y^{x,n,R}_{t}
to
\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
-measurable.
The second set
B_{2}\subseteq(\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}})^{c}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-null set. Therefore,
B_{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
as
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
is complete. Hence,
B_{1}\cup B_{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{0}
, which implies that
\Omega^{0}\ni\omega^{0}\mapsto Y^{x,n,R}_{t}(\omega^{0})
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable. Furthermore, Lemma 8.1.4 in
[
33
]
also yields that the mapping
x\mapsto\bm{Y}^{x,n,R}(\omega^{0})
is continuous, with respect to the supremum norm on
C([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for each fixed
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
. Therefore,

\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Omega^{0}\ni(x,\omega^{0})\mapsto Y^{x,n,R}_{t}(\omega^{0})

is a Carathéodory function, Definition 4.1 in
[
48
]
, and is thus jointly measurable in
(\omega^{0},x)
by Theorem 4.1 in
[
48
]
. Note that the composition of measurable functions is measurable when the
\sigma
-algebra on the co-domain of the first map agrees with the
\sigma
-algebra on the domain of the second map. Accordingly,
Y^{\xi,n,R}_{t}
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
, having canonically embedded the random variable
Y^{\xi,n,R}_{t}
defined on
(\Omega^{0},\mathcal{F}^{0})
into
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
. This completes the inductive step.

Let
\bm{Y}^{x,\xi}
denote
\bm{Y}^{x,\bm{\mu},R}
evaluated at
\bm{\mu}=\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{\infty}
. The preceding analysis shows that
Y^{x,\xi}_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable and
Y^{x,\xi}_{t}|_{x=\xi}
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
. Furthermore, notice that
\bm{Y}^{x,\xi}|_{x=\xi}
satisfies (
56
)
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., and thus provides a version of the solution to (
56
) that is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
.

Step Three: Measurability of
\bm{p}^{\xi,R}

By Assumption
2
,
\partial_{\mu}\ell
is jointly continuous in each argument. By combining this assumption with the analysis of
Step Two
, we find that the terminal condition
\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{1}))(Y^{x,\xi}_{1})
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable. Furthermore, as seen in
Step Two
of the proof of Lemma
20
, the terminal condition is bounded
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. by
K(R_{X})
for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
. Since we use the convention that
Y_{1}^{x,\xi}=x
on
(\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}})^{c}
and
R_{0}\leq R_{X}
, it follows that
\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{1}))(Y^{x,\xi}_{1})\in\bar{B}(K(R_{X}))
for all
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
.

Let
\bm{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{2,d}
such that
q_{t}
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
. Then,
(Y_{t}^{\xi},q_{t})
is

(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
, which implies that
\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},q_{t})(\omega^{0})
is well-defined for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
. Consider, for

\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
, the velocity field given by

(t,p,\rho)\mapsto-\mathcal{K}^{R}(Y^{x,\xi}_{t}(\omega^{0}),\rho,\nu_{t}(\omega^{0}),p),

which is Lipschitz in variables
(p,\rho)
and continuous in
t
for fixed
(p,\rho)
. Therefore, the above velocity field satisfies the assumptions of Lemma 8.1.4 in
[
33
]
. Thus, by applying this Lemma for
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
,
the ODE

\dot{p}^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}_{t}=-\mathcal{K}^{R}(Y^{x,\xi}_{t},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},q_{t}),\nu_{t},p^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}_{t}),\qquad p^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}_{1}=\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{1}))(Y^{x,\xi}_{1}).

(59)

has a unique solution in
C([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1})
. For
\omega\notin\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
, we define
p^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}_{t}=\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi))(x)
so that
t\mapsto p_{t}^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}
is continuous in
t
for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
. Furthermore, Lemma 8.1.4 in
[
33
]
states that
p^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}_{t}
is continuous in its terminal value. In
Step Two
, we deduced that
x\mapsto Y^{x,\xi}_{1}
is continuous for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
. By Assumption
2
, the terminal condition for
p_{1}^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}
is a continuous function of
Y^{x,\xi}_{1}
, and thus it is continuous in
x
. Accordingly, the map
x\mapsto p^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}_{t}
is continuous, which implies that
p_{t}^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}(\omega^{0})|_{x=\xi}
is
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
,
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
and
\xi\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1};\bar{B}(R_{0}))
. Then, the continuity of
t\mapsto p_{t}^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}(\omega^{0})|_{x=\xi}
seen earlier yields that
\bm{p}^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}(\omega^{0})|_{x=\xi}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,2,2d}
for any
\xi\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1};\bar{B}(R_{0}))
and
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
.

Consider the operator,

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{R}:\Omega^{0}\times L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1};\bar{B}(R_{0}))\times\mathscr{C}^{1,2,d}\to\mathscr{C}^{1,2,d},\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{R}(\omega^{0},\xi,\bm{q})\mapsto\bm{p}^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}(\omega^{0})|_{x=\xi}.

Theorem 4.23 in
[
8
]
Vol. I shows that the Picard iterates defined by

\bm{p}^{\xi,n+1,R}=\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{R}(\cdot,\xi,\bm{p}^{\xi,n,R}),\qquad\bm{p}^{\xi,0,R}=\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi))(\xi).

converge, with respect to the metric induced by
\|_{\cdot}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1,2,d}}
, to
\bm{p}^{\xi}
for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
. Since
\bm{p}^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}
is independent of
\bm{q}
for
\omega^{0}\notin\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
, this convergence holds for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
. Note that Theorem 4.23 in
[
8
]
Vol. I concerns backward McKean–Vlasov SDEs, where the solution is a pair of adapted processes. A second process for the diffusion term in the BSDE is required to ensure that the first process is adapted to the forward filtration. In our setting, we can take the filtration to be independent of time
t
and apply Remark 4.22 in
[
8
]
Vol. I, with
Y^{\xi}_{t}
in place of
\zeta_{t}
, to show that the solution to the backward equation for
\bm{p}^{x,\xi,\bm{q},R}
has identically zero diffusion coefficient. Thus, the convergence of the Picard iterates
\bm{p}^{\xi,n,R}
follows from Theorem 4.23 in
[
8
]
Vol. I, by applying their argument with all diffusion terms set to zero.

Since
Y^{\xi}_{t}
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable and
\xi
is
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-measurable, the map

\Omega\ni(\omega^{0},\omega^{1})\mapsto\mathbbm{1}_{D_{1}}(Y^{\xi}_{t}(\omega^{0},\omega^{1}))\mathbbm{1}_{D_{2}}(\xi(\omega^{1}))

is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable for every
D_{1},D_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By Lemma 1.28 in
[
39
]
, the map

\Omega^{0}\ni\omega^{0}\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi,0,R}_{t})(\omega^{0})(D_{1}\times D_{2})=\int\mathbbm{1}_{D_{1}}(Y^{\xi}_{t}(\omega^{0},\omega^{1}))\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{D_{2}}(\xi(\omega^{1}))d\mathbb{P}^{1}(\omega^{1})

is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable. Hence, since
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})=\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, Criterion
2
applies to guarantee that
\bm{\mathfrak{r}}^{0}\in\mathfrak{M}
.
By following the same argument as
Step Two
, if
p^{\xi,n,R}_{t}
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
, then
\bm{\mathfrak{r}}^{n}:=(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi,n,R}_{t}))_{t\in[0,1]}
is well-defined and belongs to
\mathfrak{M}
. Then, with
\bm{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}
and
\bm{Y}^{\xi,n,R}
replaced by
\bm{\mathfrak{r}}^{n}
and
\bm{p}^{\xi,n,R}
, respectively, in the argument of
Step Two
, we deduce that
p_{t}^{\xi,n+1,R}
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
whenever
\bm{\mathfrak{r}}^{n}\in\mathfrak{M}
. Thus, we have by induction that
p^{\xi,n}_{t}
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{1})
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1],n\geq 0
.

By taking the coupling
\mathcal{L}^{1}((Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi,n,R}_{t}),(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t}))
, we obtain

W^{2}_{2}(\mathfrak{r}^{n}_{t},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t}))\leq\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\|p^{\xi,n,R}_{t}-p^{\xi}_{t}\|^{2}\right]\leq\|\bm{p}^{\xi,n,R}-\bm{p}^{\xi}\|^{2}_{\mathscr{C}^{1,2,d}}

holds for every
t\in[0,1]
and
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
. Hence
\bm{\mathfrak{r}}^{n}
converges to
(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t}))_{t\in[0,1]}
with respect to
\mathcal{D}
. The preceding analysis shows that
\bm{\mathfrak{r}}^{n}\in\mathfrak{M}
for every
n\geq 0
. Therefore,
(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t}))_{t\in[0,1]}\in\mathfrak{M}
by the stability of measurability under pointwise limits.
∎

Corollary 23
.

Let
R_{2}>0
and
\bm{\nu}
satisfy the same conditions as in Lemma
21
and denote the solution to (
56
)-(
57
) by
(\bm{Y}^{\xi},\bm{p}^{\xi})\in\mathscr{C}^{2,2d}
. Then, there exists a solution
(\bm{Y}^{x,\xi},\bm{p}^{x,\xi})\in\mathscr{C}^{0,2,2d}
, unique up to
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. equality, to the following system of ODEs

\displaystyle\dot{Y}^{x,\xi}_{t}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(Y^{x,\xi}_{t},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t}),\nu_{t}),\qquad

\displaystyle Y_{0}^{x,\xi}=x,

(60)

\displaystyle\dot{p}^{x,\xi}_{t}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{K}(Y^{x,\xi}_{t},\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t}),\nu_{t},p^{x,\xi}_{t}),\qquad

\displaystyle p_{1}^{x,\xi}=\partial_{\mu}\ell(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{1}))(Y^{x,\xi}_{1}).

(61)

Furthermore, there exists a constant
\Lambda_{F}=\Lambda_{F}(R_{0},R_{2},\beta)
such that

\|Y^{x_{1},\xi_{1}}_{t}-Y^{x_{2},\xi_{2}}_{t}\|+\|p^{x_{1},\xi_{1}}_{t}-p^{x_{2},\xi_{2}}_{t}\|\leq\Lambda_{F}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+W_{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2}))\right),

\mathbb{P}^{0}
-almost surely.

Remark 24
.

The Lipschitz estimate of
(\bm{Y}^{x,\xi},\bm{p}^{x,\xi})
in
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1})
of Corollary
23
shows that
(\bm{Y}^{x,\xi},\bm{p}^{x,\xi})
depends on
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1})
only through its law. Therefore, with
\zeta=\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi)\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
, the paths

\bm{X}^{x,\zeta},\bm{a}^{x,\zeta}\in C([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})
given by
(\bm{X}^{x,\zeta},\bm{a}^{x,\zeta})=(\bm{Y}^{x,\xi},\bm{p}^{x,\xi})
are well-defined such that
(X_{t}^{x,\zeta},a_{t}^{x,\zeta})
are
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. Lipschitz in
(t,x,\zeta)
with respect to the Euclidean and
W_{2}
-metric, respectively. The Lipschitz continuity in
t
results immediately from the boundedness of
(X^{x,\zeta}_{t},a^{x,\zeta}_{t})
and the Linear growth of
\mathcal{K}
and
\Gamma
, Lemma
16
. Moreover,
(X_{t}^{x,\zeta},a_{t}^{x,\zeta})
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for any
t\in[0,1],x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
.

Proof.

Notice that
\bm{Y}^{x,\xi},\bm{p}^{x,\xi}
were introduced earlier in (
58
) and (
59
) with
\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{q}
evaluated at
\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t})
and
\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi}_{t},p^{\xi}_{t})
, respectively. The existence of
(\bm{Y}^{\xi},\bm{p}^{\xi})\in\mathscr{C}^{2,2d}
was established in Lemma
21
. As a result, the existence, uniqueness and measurability of
\bm{Y}^{x,\xi},\bm{p}^{x,\xi}
follows directly from
Steps Two
and
Three
of the proof of Lemma
21
. Therefore, only the Lipschitz continuity with respect to the initial condition requires further study. Lemma 3.1 in
[
43
]
, with identically zero diffusion terms, establishes that there exists a constant
\Lambda_{Y}
depending only on the Lipschitz constant
\Lambda_{\Gamma}^{R}
of
\gamma^{R}
such that

\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\|Y^{x_{1},\xi_{1}}_{t}-Y^{x_{2},\xi_{2}}_{t}\|\leq\Lambda_{Y}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|+W_{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2}))\right),\qquad\mathbb{P}^{0}-a.s.

Let us argue similarly to Lemma 3.1 in
[
43
]
to prove that the same holds for the adjoint variable. Suppose
\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{2}^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{i}^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{i})
for
i=1,2
.
By the uniqueness of the solution to (
56
), the law of
(Y^{\xi_{i}^{\prime},\xi_{i}}_{s},p^{\xi_{i}^{\prime},\xi_{i}}_{s})
conditioned on
\mathcal{F}^{0}
coincides with
\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{i}}_{s},p^{\xi_{i}}_{s})

\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. for
i=1,2
and
s\in[0,1]
. Then, the Lipschitz continuity of
\bm{Y}^{x,\xi}
implies that

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,1]}W^{2}_{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{1}}_{s}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{2}}_{s}))

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}^{1}[\|Y^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}_{s}-Y^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}_{s}\|^{2}]

(62)

\displaystyle\leq 2\Lambda_{Y}^{2}\left(W_{2}^{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2}))+\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\|\xi_{1}^{\prime}-\xi_{2}^{\prime}\|^{2}\right]\right).

The final term becomes the squared
2
-Wasserstein distance between
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1})
and
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2})
after taking the infimum over all couplings between
\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{2}^{\prime}
.
Accordingly, the difference in terminal conditions for
p
can be bounded using Assumption
2
to get

\displaystyle\|p^{x_{1},\xi_{1}}_{1}-p_{1}^{x_{2},\xi_{2}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda^{2}_{\ell}(R_{X})\left(W_{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{1}}_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{2}}_{1}))+\|Y^{x_{1},\xi_{1}}_{1}-Y^{x_{2},\xi_{2}}_{1}\|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\Lambda^{2}_{\ell}(R_{X})\Lambda^{2}_{Y}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|^{2}+3W^{2}_{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2}))\right).

Then, by applying the above estimate, Young’s and Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities and the Lipschitz continuity of
\mathcal{K}^{R}
, we obtain, for
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.}}
, that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\left\|p^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}_{t}-p_{t}^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 24\Lambda_{Y}^{2}\Lambda_{\ell}^{2}(R_{X})\left(\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\|\xi_{1}^{\prime}-\xi_{2}^{\prime}\|^{2}\right]+W_{2}^{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2}))\right)

\displaystyle+6\Lambda^{2}_{\mathcal{K}}(1-t)\int_{t}^{1}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\|Y^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}_{s}-Y^{\xi_{2},\xi_{2}^{\prime}}_{s}\|^{2}+\|p^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}_{s}-p^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}_{s}\|^{2}\right]ds

\displaystyle+6\Lambda^{2}_{\mathcal{K}}(1-t)\int_{t}^{1}W_{2}^{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{1}}_{s},p^{\xi_{1}}_{s}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{2}}_{s},p^{\xi_{2}}_{s}))ds,

(63)

where
\Lambda_{\mathcal{K}}
is the Lipschitz constant of
\mathcal{K}^{R}
with
R=R_{X}\vee R_{a}
.
The final term can be bounded using the Lipschitz continuity of
(x,\xi)\mapsto Y_{t}^{x,\xi}
according to

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{1}}_{s},p^{\xi_{1}}_{s}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{2}}_{s},p^{\xi_{2}}_{s}))\leq\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\|Y_{s}^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}-Y^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}_{s}\|^{2}+\|p^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}_{s}-p^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}_{s}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\Lambda_{Y}^{2}W^{2}_{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2}))+\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[2\Lambda_{Y}^{2}\|\xi_{1}^{\prime}-\xi_{2}^{\prime}\|^{2}+\|p^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}_{s}-p^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}_{s}\|^{2}\right].

Combining this with the preceding bound and applying the Lipschitz continuity of
(x,\xi)\mapsto\bm{Y}^{x,\xi}
implies that there exists some constant
C^{\prime}
depending only on
\Lambda^{R_{X}\vee R_{a}}_{\mathcal{K}},\Lambda_{Y},\Lambda_{\ell}(R_{X})
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\left\|p^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}_{t}-p_{t}^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\left(W^{2}_{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2}))+\mathbb{E}^{1}[\|\xi_{1}^{\prime}-\xi_{2}^{\prime}\|^{2}]+\int_{t}^{1}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\left\|p^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}_{s}-p_{s}^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}\right\|^{2}\right]ds\right).

By applying Grönwall’s lemma, we get

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\left\|p^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}_{t}-p_{t}^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq C^{\prime}e^{C^{\prime}}\left(W_{2}^{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2}))+\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\|\xi_{1}^{\prime}-\xi_{2}^{\prime}\|^{2}\right])\right).

Combining this with the bound on
\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\|Y^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}_{t}-Y^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}\|^{2}\right]
seen in (
62
), we obtain

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}\left(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{1}}_{t},p^{\xi_{2}}_{t}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{2}}_{t},p^{\xi_{2}}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\|Y^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}_{t}-Y^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}_{t}\|^{2}+\|p^{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{1}}_{t}-p^{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\xi_{2}}_{t}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(2\Lambda_{Y}^{2}+C^{\prime}e^{C^{\prime}})\left(W_{2}^{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2}))+\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\|\xi_{1}^{\prime}-\xi_{2}^{\prime}\|^{2}\right]\right).

Again, by taking the infimum over the couplings
\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{2}^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\Omega^{1})
with
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{i}^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{i})
,
i=1,2
, we find that

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}W_{2}\left(\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{1}}_{t},p^{\xi_{2}}_{t}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi_{2}}_{t},p^{\xi_{2}}_{t})\right)\leq(4\Lambda_{Y}^{2}+2C^{\prime}e^{C^{\prime}})^{1/2}W_{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2})),

holds
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. for any
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1};\bar{B}(R_{0}))
. Note that (
E
) holds with
x_{1},x_{2}
in place of
\xi_{1}^{\prime},\xi_{2}^{\prime}
, without requiring the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Therefore, the above estimates yield that there exists some constant
C^{\prime\prime}
depending only on
\Lambda^{R_{X}\vee R_{a}}_{\mathcal{K}},\Lambda_{Y},\Lambda_{\ell}(R_{X})
such that

\left\|p^{x_{1},\xi_{1}}_{t}-p^{x_{2},\xi_{2}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\leq C^{\prime\prime}\left(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|^{2}+W^{2}_{2}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi_{2}))+\int_{t}^{1}\|p^{x_{1},\xi_{1}}_{s}-p^{x_{2},\xi_{2}}\|^{2}ds\right).

The desired result now holds after applying Grönwall’s lemma.
∎

Let
\bm{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})
with
y_{i}\in\bar{B}(R_{0})
for every
1\leq i\leq N
.
Suppose that
\xi^{N}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a random variable that takes the values
y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}
each with probability
N^{-1}
, so that its law is given by

\zeta^{N}:=\mathcal{L}^{1}(\xi^{N})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{y_{i}}.

Accordingly, the law of
(X^{x,\zeta^{N}}_{t},a^{x,\zeta^{N}}_{t})_{x=\xi^{N}}
conditioned on
\mathcal{F}^{0}
is the empirical measure

\mathfrak{r}^{N}_{t}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\left(X^{y_{i},\zeta^{N}}_{t},\,a^{y_{i},\zeta^{N}}_{t}\right)}.

Then, by the uniqueness of the solutions to (
53
)-(
54
) established in Lemma
21
,
(\bm{X}^{x,\zeta^{N}},\bm{a}^{x,\zeta^{N}})|_{x=\xi^{N}}
equals to
(\bm{Y}^{\xi^{N}},\bm{p}^{\xi^{N}})

\mathbb{P}
-almost surely. By Lemma 2.4 in
[
8
]
Vol. II, the conditional laws
\mathfrak{r}^{N}_{t}
and
\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi^{N}}_{t},p^{\xi^{N}}_{t})

coincide
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s., and so we can replace
\mathcal{L}^{1}(Y^{\xi^{N}}_{t},p^{\xi^{N}}_{t})
by
\mathfrak{r}^{N}_{t}
in (
60
)-(
61
). This yields our continuous-time model (
17
)-(
19
), when
\bm{\nu}
in Corollary (
23
) is taken to be the mean-field parameters
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}
. Hence, there exists a solution to (
17
)-(
19
) provided that
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}
satisfies the assumptions of Corollary (
23
).

Furthermore, the uniqueness of the solution to (
17
)-(
19
) follows by a standard Grönwall argument from the local Lipchitz continuity and linear growth of
\Gamma,\mathcal{K}
, Lemmas
17
and
16
, and the local Lipschitz continuity of
\partial_{\mu}\ell
, Assumption
2
. Therefore, we obtain that

\left(X^{i,N}_{t,\tau},a^{i,N}_{t,\tau}\right)=\left(X^{y_{i},\zeta^{N}}_{t},a^{y_{i},\zeta^{N}}_{t}\right),\qquad\mathbb{P}^{0}-a.s.,

(64)

for every
t\in[0,1]
when
\bm{\nu}
in (
56
)-(
54
) is taken as
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}
.

This is made precise and extended as follows.

Lemma 25
.

For training step
\tau\geq 0
, suppose that mean-field parameters
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
21
, for some
R_{2}>0
. Then, for any initial condition
\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}
, there exists a solution
(X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})
to (
17
)-(
19
), unique up to
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. equality, such that
(X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})
are
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
. Furthermore, these solutions satisfy

X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}\in\bar{B}(R_{X})
and
a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}\in\bar{B}(R_{a})

\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s., where
R_{X},R_{a}
are the constants from Lemma
20
. Furthermore, given training data
(\bm{y}_{b,\tau})_{b=1}^{B}
that is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable, the gradient map
g_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}_{\tau}](\theta)
, given by (
20
), is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
and
t\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

Let
(X_{t,\tau}^{x,\zeta},a^{x,\zeta}_{t,\tau})
be the solution to (
60
)-(
61
) with mean-field parameters
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}
. This solution exists by Remark
24
and Corollary
23
, whose conditions are satisfied by
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}
by assumption.
Let
\zeta^{N}
be the empirical measure on
(y^{i})_{i=1}^{N}

Then, (
64
) identifies
X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
with
X_{t}^{x,\zeta},a^{x,\zeta}_{t}
evaluated at
x=y^{i}
and
\zeta=\zeta^{N}
. Therefore, the claim on the boundedness, uniqueness and measurability of
X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
now follows immediately from Corollary
23
.

Let
\zeta^{N}_{b,\tau}
be the empirical measure on the training data
y_{b,\tau}^{1},\ldots,y_{b,\tau}^{N}
. Since
(x,\zeta)\mapsto(X_{t}^{x,\zeta},a^{x,\zeta}_{t})
is jointly continuous, after modifying it on a
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-null set if necessary, and
(y^{i}_{b,\tau})_{i=1}^{N}
and
\zeta^{N}_{b,\tau}
are
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable, it follows by the composition of continuous and measurable maps that
X^{i,b}_{t,\tau},a^{i,b}_{t,\tau}
are
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
.
Similarly, we find that, for any deterministic
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
,
\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(X^{i,b}_{t,\tau},\mu^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\nu_{t,\tau},a^{i,b}_{t,\tau})(\theta)
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable as
\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}
is jointly continuous in each argument, see Lemma
18
, and
X^{i,b}_{t,\tau},\mu^{N,b}_{t,\tau},a^{i,b}_{t,\tau}
and
\nu_{t,\tau}
are
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable. The gradient
g_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}_{\tau}](\theta)
, given in equation (
20
), is the average of
\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(X^{i,b}_{t,\tau},\mu^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\nu_{t,\tau},a^{i,b}_{t,\tau})(\theta)
over particles
1\leq i\leq N
and batches
1\leq b\leq B
. Hence,
g_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}_{\tau}](\theta)
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
and
t\in[0,1]
.

∎

Lemma
43
states that the infinity norm on
\mathcal{R}
applied to the updates of Algorithm
1
are bounded by some constant
B_{\beta}=B_{\beta}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
. As a result, when
\|\mathcal{R}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\|_{\infty}
exceeds
B_{\beta}\lambda^{-1}
, the weight-decay term dominates and forces
\|\mathcal{R}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau+1})\|_{\infty}
to be smaller than
\|\mathcal{R}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\|_{\infty}
. In this way, we can find a compact set with which the support of
\nu_{t,\tau}
is common to all
t\in[0,1],\tau\geq 0
. To compensate for the different choice of
\mathcal{R}
in Algorithm
1
, introduce

c_{\mathcal{R}}=\begin{cases}1&\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{1},\\
\sqrt{dk}&\mathcal{R}=\mathrm{Id},\end{cases}

where
\mathrm{Id}
is the identity map. This constant is chosen so that any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
that satisfies

\|\mathcal{R}(\theta)\|_{\infty}\leq R
is an element of
\mathfrak{S}(c_{\mathcal{R}}R)
for each of our choices of
\mathcal{R}
.

Lemma 26
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
-
3
hold and that the parameters
\nu_{t,\tau},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}
are updated according to Algorithm
1
with
\mathcal{R}
either the identity map or given by equation (
26
). Suppose that Algorithm
1
is performed with weight decay
\lambda>0
, and step sizes satisfying
\eta_{k}\in(0,\lambda^{-1})
. Then, there exists a constant
R_{\theta}>0
such that the supports of
\nu_{t,\tau}
and
\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}
are contained in
\mathfrak{S}(R_{\theta})
for any training step
\tau
,
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. Specifically
R_{\theta}=c_{\mathcal{R}}B_{\beta}\lambda^{-1}
, where
B_{\beta}
is defined in Lemma
43
. Also, the map
(\omega^{0},t)\mapsto\nu_{t,\tau}(\omega^{0})
is continuous for
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.e.
\omega^{0}
, and
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for
t\in[0,1]
. Similarly,
\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
.

Furthermore, there exists a solution to the system of equations (
17
)-(
19
), unique up to
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. equality, given any initial condition
\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}
. For every
t\in[0,1]
and
\tau\geq 0
, these solutions
(X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})
are
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable and
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. satisfy
X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}\in\bar{B}(R_{X})
,
a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}\in\bar{B}(R_{a})
for every
1\leq i\leq N
, where
R_{X},R_{a}
are the constants of Lemma
20
with
R_{2}=R_{\theta}
. Finally, the solutions to the discrete-time IPS (
14
)-(
16
), denoted by
\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
are
\mathcal{F}
-measurable and satisfy the same bounds
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

Proof.

Let us first consider the continuous-time model.

Base Case
: The support of
\nu_{t,0}=\pi
is contained in
\{\theta:\|\mathcal{R}(\theta)\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{-1}\}
by Assumption
1
. The constant
c_{\mathcal{R}}
is chosen so that
\|\mathcal{R}(\theta)\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{-1}
implies
\theta\in\mathfrak{S}(c_{\mathcal{R}}\lambda^{-1})
. As a result,
supp\,\pi\subseteq\mathfrak{S}(c_{\mathcal{R}}\lambda^{-1})\subseteq\mathfrak{S}(R_{\theta})
, where
R_{\theta}=B_{\beta}c_{\mathcal{R}}\lambda^{-1}
, since
B_{\beta}>1
, as seen in Lemma
43
. Also,
\nu_{t,0}=\pi
is deterministic and constant in
t
, and so satisfies the measurability and continuity requirements. Finally,
\Phi_{t,0}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)=\theta
is deterministic and thus measurable.

Inductive Hypothesis
: Suppose for some training step
\tau\geq 0
that
\bm{\nu}_{j}
satisfies the Assumptions of Lemma
21
with
R_{2}=R_{\theta}
and that
\Phi_{t,j}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for every
j\leq\tau,\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
and
t\in[0,1]
.

Lemma
25
applies, with
R_{2}=R_{\theta}
, and guarantees that the desired results for
\bm{X}^{i,\bm{y}}_{\tau},\bm{a}^{i,\bm{y}}_{\tau}
hold. Furthermore, it states that
g_{t,j+1}[\bm{\nu}_{j}](\theta)
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for every
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
and
j\leq\tau
. Since the map
\theta\mapsto g_{t,j+1}[\bm{\nu}_{j}](\theta)
is continuous, by replacing
\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}
by
\bm{\nu}
in Lemma
30
,
g_{t,j+1}[\bm{\nu}_{j}](\vartheta)
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for every
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable
\vartheta
. Specifically,
g_{t,j+1}[\bm{\nu}_{j}](\Phi_{t,j}[\bm{\nu}](\theta))
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for every
j\leq\tau
and
t\in[0,1]
. The variance and momentum accumulators are linear combinations of these gradients and are therefore
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable. The denominators of the Adam updates are bounded away from zero, and so
\hat{m}_{j}/(\sqrt{\hat{v}_{j}}+\varepsilon)
are
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for every
j\leq\tau+1
. As a result,
\Phi_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable. Moreover, Lemma
28
, whose conditions are satisfied according to Lemma
30
, implies that
\theta\mapsto\Phi_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)
is continuous, and thus
(\omega^{0},\theta)\mapsto\Phi_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}](\omega^{0})(\theta)
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}))
-measurable by Theorem 4.1 in
[
48
]
. Then, consider the map given by

\omega^{0}\mapsto\nu_{t,\tau+1}(\omega^{0})(D)=\int\mathbbm{1}_{D}(\Phi_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}](\omega^{0})(\theta))\,d\pi(\theta).

It is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for every
D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d})
by Lemma 1.28 in
[
39
]
. Therefore,
\omega^{0}\mapsto\nu_{t,\tau+1}(\omega^{0})
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
by Criterion
1
.

Lemma
43
in Appendix
K.2
states that the infinity norm on
\mathcal{R}
applied to the Adam updates is bounded by the constant
B_{\beta}
, which only depends on
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
. Furthermore, if
\mathcal{R}
is the identity map or given by equation (
26
), then
\mathcal{R}
is
1
-homogeneous and sub-additive in each component. Applying these properties gives

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}(\Phi_{t,\tau+1}[\nu_{<\tau+1}](\theta))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{1,\tau}\|\mathcal{R}(\theta)\|_{\infty}+\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\eta_{i}\alpha_{i+1,\tau+1}\left\|\mathcal{R}\left(\frac{\hat{m}^{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}{\sqrt{\hat{v}^{\mathcal{R}}_{i}}+\varepsilon}\right)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{1,\tau}\|\mathcal{R}(\theta)\|_{\infty}+B_{\beta}\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\eta_{i}\alpha_{i+1,\tau+1}.

Notice that
(1-\eta_{i}\lambda)\alpha_{i+1,\tau+1}=\alpha_{i,\tau+1}
, which applied to the above bound produces the telescoping sum

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}(\Phi_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}_{<\tau+1}](\theta))\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{1,\tau}\|\mathcal{R}(\theta)\|_{\infty}+\lambda^{-1}B_{\beta}\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\alpha_{i+1,\tau+1}-\alpha_{i,\tau+1}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{1,\tau}\left(\|\mathcal{R}(\theta)\|_{\infty}-\lambda^{-1}B_{\beta}\right)+\lambda^{-1}B_{\beta}.

As a result, if
\theta
belongs to the closed set
S
defined by

S=\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}:\|\mathcal{R}(\theta)\|_{\infty}\leq B_{\beta}\lambda^{-1}\},

then
\Phi_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)\in S
. This implies that
\Phi^{-1}_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}](S^{c})\subseteq S^{c}
. Therefore,
S^{c}
is an open set such that

\nu_{t,\tau+1}(S^{c})=\nu_{t,0}(\Phi^{-1}_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}_{<\tau+1}](S^{c}))\leq\nu_{t,0}(S^{c})=0,

since the support of
\nu_{t,0}
is contained within
S
by Assumption
1
. Hence, it must be that the support of
\nu_{t,\tau+1}
is contained within
S\subseteq\mathfrak{S}(R_{\theta})
. The inductive step is completed by applying Lemma
29
to show that
t\mapsto\nu_{t,\tau+1}
is continuous
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s.

Note that the above analysis bounding
\Phi[\bm{\nu}]
requires only that the sequence of mean-field parameters
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}

is generated according to Algorithm
1
. Accordingly,
supp\,\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}(R_{\theta})
for every
t\in[0,1]
, and
\tau\geq 0
. Furthermore,
(\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})\in\bar{B}(R_{X})\times\bar{B}(R_{a})
for every
t\in[0,1],\tau\geq 0
and
\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}
,
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely, by adapting the Grönwall argument in the proof of Lemma
20
to the discrete-time setting. If the parameters
\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}_{j}
are
\mathcal{F}
-measurable for every
j\leq\tau
, then the
(\bar{X}^{i,L,b}_{t,j},\bar{a}^{i,L,b}_{t,j})
are
\mathcal{F}
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
and
j\leq\tau
. This is because the updates defining
(\bar{X}^{i,L,b}_{t,j},\bar{a}^{i,L,b}_{t,j})
are continuous functions of the
\mathcal{F}
-measurable parameters and the previous states. Accordingly,
g_{j+1}[\bm{\nu}^{N,L}_{j}](\theta)
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable for every
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
and
j\leq\tau
, by the same argument as Lemma
25
. By Lemma
18
, the gradient map
g_{j+1}[\bm{\nu}^{N,L}_{j}](\theta)
is continuous in
\theta
, as it is a linear combination of
\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}
. Therefore, by applying the same argument as above,
\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau+1}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
. Hence, by induction,
\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable for every
t\in[0,1]
and
\tau\geq 0
. The base case holds since the initialised parameters are
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.
∎

Remark 27
.

Lemma
26
establishes that the trajectories of
X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
, and the parameters
\nu_{t,\tau}
remain confined to a compact set that is uniform across all training steps. The same is true for the corresponding discrete-time variables. Consequently, the local Lipschitz constants and bounds obtained in Appendix
D
apply globally on the relevant domain when evaluated at
R_{1}=R_{X},R_{2}=R_{\theta}
and
R_{3}=R_{a}
. For the remainder of the paper, we take all of these constants to be evaluated at these radii and suppress their explicit dependence on
R_{X},R_{\theta},R_{a}
for notational convenience. Similarly, we shall write
\mathfrak{S}
to denote
\mathfrak{S}(R_{\theta})
.

Appendix F
Regularity of the AdamW Flow Map
\Phi[\nu]

Lemma 28
.

Suppose that there exists a constant
D>0
such that for any sequence of mean-field parameters
\bm{\nu}=(\nu_{j})_{j=0}^{T}
and
\bm{\bar{\nu}}=(\bar{\nu}_{j})_{j=0}^{T}
, and
\theta,\vartheta\in\mathfrak{S}
, the gradient map defined in (
20
) satisfies

\|g_{t,\tau+1}[\nu_{\tau}](\theta)-g_{t,\tau+1}[\bar{\nu}_{\tau}](\vartheta)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\leq D\left(A_{t,\tau}+\|\theta-\vartheta\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\right),

for some non-negative
A_{t,\tau}
at every training step
\tau\in[0:T-1]
. Then, there exists a constant
D_{1}=D_{1}(D,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\varepsilon,\lambda,T)
such that for any
\tau\in[1:T]

\|\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)-\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}](\vartheta)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\leq D_{1}\left(\|\theta-\vartheta\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}+\sum_{j=0}^{T-1}\kappa^{0}_{j,T}A_{t,j}\right),

where
\kappa^{0}_{i,T}>0
is given by

\displaystyle\kappa^{0}_{i,T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau=i+1}^{T}\frac{(1-\beta_{1})\eta_{\tau}}{1-\beta_{1}^{\tau}}(\beta_{1}^{\tau-i-1}+2\beta_{2}^{\tau-i-1}).

(65)

Proof.

For ease of notation, write

\theta_{t,T}=\Phi_{t,T}[\bm{\nu}](\theta),\qquad\vartheta_{t,T}=\Phi_{t,T}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}](\vartheta).

By applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality on the definition of the flow map (
28
), we get

\displaystyle\|\theta_{t,T}-\vartheta_{t,T}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\alpha^{2}_{1,T}\|\theta-\vartheta\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle+2\left(\sum_{\tau=1}^{T}\eta_{\tau}\alpha_{\tau+1,T}\right)\left(\sum_{\tau=1}^{T}\eta_{\tau}\alpha_{\tau+1,T}\left\|\frac{\hat{m}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\tau}(G_{t}[\bm{\nu}])}{\sqrt{\hat{v}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\tau}(G_{t}[\bm{\nu}])}+\varepsilon}-\frac{\hat{m}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\tau}(G_{t}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}])}{\sqrt{\hat{v}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\tau}(G_{t}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}])}+\varepsilon}\right\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\right).

Notice that
\lambda\eta_{\tau}\alpha_{\tau+1,T}=\alpha_{\tau+1,T}-\alpha_{\tau,T}
, and so the first term becomes the telescoping sum

\sum_{\tau=1}^{T}\eta_{\tau}\alpha_{\tau+1,T}=\lambda^{-1}\sum_{\tau=1}^{T}\alpha_{\tau+1,T}-\alpha_{\tau,T}\leq\lambda^{-1}.

Lemma
44
establishes the Lipschitz continuity of the Adam updates with respect to the gradient sequence
G_{t}
, which states that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\hat{m}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\tau}(G_{t}[\nu])}{\sqrt{\hat{v}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\tau}(G_{t}[\nu])}+\varepsilon}-\frac{\hat{m}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\tau}(G_{t}[\bar{\nu}])}{\sqrt{\hat{v}^{\mathcal{R}}_{\tau}(G_{t}[\bar{\nu}])}+\varepsilon}\right\|_{F}^{2}\leq\frac{2(1-\beta_{1})}{\varepsilon^{2}(1-\beta_{1}^{\tau})}\sum_{j=1}^{\tau}(\beta_{1}^{\tau-j}+2\beta_{2}^{\tau-j})\|g_{t,j}-\bar{g}_{t,j}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2},

where we use the short hand notation
g_{t,j}=g_{t,j}[\nu_{j-1}](\theta_{t,j-1})
and
\bar{g}_{i,j}=g_{t,j}[\bar{\nu}_{j-1}](\vartheta_{t,j-1})
. Therefore, after interchanging the order of summation, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\theta_{t,T}-\vartheta_{t,T}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\alpha^{2}_{1,T}\|\theta-\vartheta\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}+\sum_{\tau=1}^{T}\frac{4\eta_{\tau}\alpha_{\tau+1,T}(1-\beta_{1})}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}(1-\beta_{1}^{\tau})}\sum_{i=1}^{\tau}(\beta_{1}^{\tau-i}+2\beta_{2}^{\tau-i})\|g_{t,i}-\bar{g}_{t,i}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\alpha^{2}_{1,T}\|\theta-\vartheta\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}+\frac{4}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{T}\left(\sum_{\tau=i}^{T}\frac{\eta_{\tau}\alpha_{\tau+1,T}(1-\beta_{1})}{(1-\beta_{1}^{\tau})}(\beta_{1}^{\tau-i}+2\beta_{2}^{\tau-i})\right)\|g_{t,i}-\bar{g}_{t,i}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}.

Accordingly, define
\kappa^{\lambda}_{i,T}
, for
0\leq i<T
, by

\displaystyle\kappa^{\lambda}_{i,T}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau=i+1}^{T}\frac{(1-\beta_{1})\eta_{\tau}\alpha_{\tau+1,T}}{1-\beta_{1}^{\tau}}(\beta_{1}^{\tau-i-1}+2\beta_{2}^{\tau-i-1}),

(66)

so that

\displaystyle\|\theta_{t,T}-\vartheta_{t,T}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\alpha^{2}_{1,T}\|\theta-\vartheta\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}+\frac{4}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{T}\kappa^{\lambda}_{i-1,T}\|g_{t,i}[\nu_{i-1}](\theta_{t,i-1})-g_{t,i}[\bar{\nu}_{i-1}](\vartheta_{t,i-1})\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}.

(67)

Then, the assumed continuity of
g
implies that

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{t,T}[\nu](\theta)-\Phi_{t,T}[\bar{\nu}](\vartheta)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\alpha_{1,T}\|\theta-\vartheta\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}+\frac{4D}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{T-1}\kappa^{\lambda}_{j,T}\left(A_{t,j}+\|\Phi_{t,j}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)-\Phi_{t,j}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}](\theta)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\right),

where we have applied
\alpha_{1,T}^{2}\leq\alpha_{1,T}
as each
(1-\eta_{j}\lambda)\in(0,1)
. Using the convention
\sum_{j=0}^{-1}\kappa^{\lambda}_{0,0}A_{t,0}=0
, let us define the sequence

c_{T}=\alpha_{1,T}\|\theta-\vartheta\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}+\sum_{j=0}^{T-1}\kappa^{\lambda}_{j,T}A_{t,j},

which is non-negative and
c_{0}=\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
. Since
\kappa^{\lambda}_{t,T}
satisfies

\kappa^{\lambda}_{i,T}=(1-\eta_{T}\lambda)\kappa^{\lambda}_{i,T-1}+\frac{(1-\beta_{1})\eta_{T}}{1-\beta_{1}^{T}}(\beta_{1}^{T-i-1}+2\beta_{2}^{T-i-1}),\qquad\kappa^{\lambda}_{T-1,T}=\frac{3(1-\beta_{1})\eta_{T}}{1-\beta_{1}^{T}},

for
0\leq i\leq T-2
, the sequence
c_{T}
satisfies

c_{T}=(1-\eta_{T}\lambda)c_{T-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{T-1}\frac{1-\beta_{1}}{1-\beta_{1}^{T}}\eta_{T}(\beta_{1}^{T-j-1}+2\beta_{2}^{T-j-1})A_{t,j}.

As a result, the conditions for Lemma
41
hold since
c_{j}\geq(1-\eta_{T}\lambda)c_{j-1}
for
j\geq 1
, and so there exists a constant
D_{1}=D_{1}(\lambda,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},T)
such that

\|\Phi_{t,T}[\nu](\theta)-\Phi_{t,T}[\bar{\nu}](\vartheta)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\leq D_{1}\left(\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{T}\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{1-\beta_{1}}{1-\beta_{1}^{i}}\eta_{i}(\beta_{1}^{i-j-1}+2\beta_{2}^{i-j-1})A_{t,j}\right).

The Lemma concludes by interchanging the order of summation and substituting in the definition of
\kappa^{0}_{j,T}
given in (
65
).
∎

Lemma 29
.

Continuity of
\nu_{t}
in time

Let
\nu_{t,\tau}
be the parameters of (
22
) at training step
\tau
.
Then, there exists a constant

D_{2}=D_{2}(R_{X},R_{a},R_{\theta},\varepsilon,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\lambda,T)
such that for every training step
\tau\in[0:T]
and
s,t\in[0,1]

W_{2}(\nu_{t,\tau},\nu_{s,\tau})\leq D_{2}|t-s|,\qquad\mathbb{P}^{0}-a.s.

Proof.

For any
\theta,\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
, expanding the gradient map of (
20
) by the triangle inequality yields

\displaystyle\|g_{t,\tau+1}

\displaystyle[\bm{\nu}_{\tau}](\theta)-g_{s,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}_{\tau}](\vartheta)\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\int\left\|\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(x,\mu^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\nu_{t,\tau},a)(\theta)-\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(x,\mu^{N,b}_{s,\tau},\nu_{s,\tau},a)(\vartheta)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}d\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau}(x,a)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\left\|\int\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(x,\mu^{N,b}_{s,\tau},\nu_{s,\tau},a)(\vartheta)d(\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau}-\rho^{N,b}_{s,\tau})(x,a)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}},\qquad\mathbb{P}^{0}-a.s.

Lemma
18
states that
\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}
is
\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}
-Lipschitz continuous in
\theta
and
\mu
, with respect to the Frobenius norm and the
W_{\infty}
-metric, respectively. Since
\mathcal{H}
is linear in
\nu
, its derivative
\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}
with respect to
\nu
is independent of
\nu
. Hence, the first term is bounded by

\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}\left(\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\mathrm{F}}+W_{\infty}(\mu^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\mu^{N,b}_{s,\tau})\right).

Note that for any fixed
\mu,\theta,\nu
with the support of
\nu
and
\mu
contained in
\mathfrak{S},\bar{B}(R_{X})
respectively and
\theta\in\mathfrak{S}
, the map
(x,a)\to\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(x,\mu,\nu,a)(\theta)
is
\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}
-Lipschitz continuous on
\bar{B}(R_{X})\times\bar{B}(R_{a})
, Lemma
18
. Therefore, applying the Kantorovich-Rubinstein Theorem
[
51
]
to the second term is bounded above by
\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}W_{1}(\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\rho^{N,b}_{s,\tau})
. Combining the contributions from each term yields that

\displaystyle\|g_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}_{\tau}](\theta)-g_{s,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}_{\tau}](\vartheta)\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\frac{\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}W_{\infty}(\mu^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\mu^{N,b}_{s,\tau})+\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{\mathrm{F}}+W_{1}(\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\rho^{N,b}_{s,\tau}).

Immediately from the boundedness of
\Gamma
and its derivatives, see Remark
27
and Lemma
16
, we get

W_{\infty}(\mu^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\mu^{N,b}_{s,\tau})\leq|t-s|R_{X}R_{\theta}^{2},\qquad W_{1}(\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\rho^{N,b}_{s,\tau})\leq|t-s|(R_{X}R_{\theta}^{2}+R_{a}\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}),

where
\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}
is the constant from Lemma
16
with
R_{1},R_{2}
evaluated at
R_{X},R_{\theta}
, respectively. Therefore, for every
j<T
, the gradient map
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. satisfies the Lipschitz estimate

\|g_{t,j+1}[\bm{\nu}_{j}](\theta)-g_{s,j+1}[\bm{\nu}_{j}](\bar{\theta})\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}\left(\|\theta-\vartheta\|_{F}+(R_{a}\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}+2R_{X}R_{\theta}^{2})||t-s|\right).

(68)

Recall the notation
\Phi_{t,T}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)=\theta_{t,T}
from Lemma
28
. Then, applying the same argument as seen in the proof of Lemma
28
until equation (
67
), with
\theta=\vartheta
, results in

\displaystyle\|\theta_{t,T}-\theta_{s,T}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{T}\kappa^{\lambda}_{j-1,T}\|g_{t,j}[\nu](\theta_{t,j-1})-g_{s,j}[\nu](\theta_{s,j-1})\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}^{2}}{\lambda\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{T-1}\kappa^{\lambda}_{j,T}\left((\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}R_{a}+2R_{X}R_{\theta}^{2})^{2}|t-s|^{2}+\|\theta_{t,j}-\theta_{s,j}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\right),

where
\kappa^{\lambda}_{j,T}
is defined in (
66
).
The final step is deduced by Young’s inequality and the bound given in (
68
). By applying Lemma
42
to bound the summation of
\kappa^{\lambda}_{j,T}
in the first and then applying Lemma
41
, there exists a constant
D_{2}
such that

\|\Phi_{t,T}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)-\Phi_{s,T}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\leq D^{2}_{2}|t-s|^{2}.

Hence, the result holds by integrating over
\theta
with respect to
\pi
.
∎

Lemma 30
.

Let
g:\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}
be the gradient map defined in (
20
). Then, there exists a constant
D=D(\Lambda,R_{a},R_{X},R_{\theta})
such that for any
\tau\geq 0
and
\theta,\vartheta\in\mathfrak{S}

\displaystyle\|g_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}_{\tau}](\vartheta)-g_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}_{\tau}](\theta)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\leq D\left(\|\theta-\vartheta\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}+\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}W^{2}_{\infty}(\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{t,\tau},\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau})\right),

\mathbb{P}
-almost surely.

Proof.

Take
\theta,\vartheta\in\mathfrak{S}
. By expanding the expression for the gradient map
g
defined in (
20
) via Young’s Inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\bar{g}_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}_{\tau}](\vartheta)-g_{t,\tau+1}[\bm{\nu}_{\tau}](\theta)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}

(69)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\underbrace{\left\|\int\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(x,\bar{\mu}^{N,L,b}_{t,\tau},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau},a)(\vartheta)-\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(x,\mu^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\nu_{t,\tau},a)(\theta)d\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{t,\tau}(x,a)\right\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}}_{(a)}
