\displaystyle+\frac{2}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\underbrace{\left\|\int\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}(x,\mu^{N,b}_{t,\tau},\nu_{t,\tau},a)(\theta)d(\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{t,\tau}-\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau})(x,a)\right\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}}_{(b)}.

Notice that
\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}
is independent of
\nu
, since
\mathcal{H}
is linear in
\nu
.
Then, the
\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}
-Lipchitz continuity of
\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}
deduced in Lemma
18
and Remark
27
implies that

(a)\leq 2\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}^{2}\left(W_{\infty}^{2}(\bar{\mu}^{N,L,b}_{t,\tau},\mu^{N,b}_{t,\tau}))+\|\theta-\vartheta\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\right)\leq 2\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}^{2}\left(W_{\infty}^{2}(\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{t,\tau},\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau}))+\|\theta-\vartheta\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}\right).

The second inequality comes from the fact that the projection map onto the
x
-coordinate is
1
-Lipschitz.
Furthermore,
the Kantorovich-Rubinstein Theorem, which applies since
\partial_{\nu}\mathcal{H}
is
\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}
- Lipschitz in
(x,a)
, implies that term
(b)
is bounded according to

(b)\leq\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}^{2}W_{1}^{2}(\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{t,\tau},\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau})\leq\Lambda_{\mathcal{H}\nu,\infty}^{2}W_{\infty}^{2}(\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{t,\tau},\rho^{N,b}_{t,\tau}).

The Lemma results from combining the above two bounds according to (
69
).
∎

Appendix G
Framework & Notation

The assumptions introduced in this section are not required for Theorem
7
, but are stated for intermediate Lemmas. We later justify that these assumptions hold for the transformer.

Our goal is to prove uniform convergence of the transformer to the flow maps defined in Corollary
23
, which take an initial point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and an initial distribution
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as input. Accordingly, we introduce the domain

\mathscr{D}=\bar{B}(R_{0})\times\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0})).

(70)

With
(X^{x,\zeta}_{t},a^{x,\zeta}_{t})
from Remark
24
in mind, consider the map

Z:\Omega_{0}\times[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{2d},

that satisfies

Assumption 4
.

For
t\in[0,1],x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
, the map
\Omega^{0}\ni\omega^{0}\mapsto Z(\omega^{0},t,x,\zeta)
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable. Furthermore, for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
, the map
(t,x,\zeta)\mapsto Z(\omega^{0},t,x,\zeta)
is Lipschitz continuous on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
.

This Assumption requires continuity in
(t,x,\zeta)
for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
, rather than
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-almost surely. However, the version of the solution to (
56
)-(
57
) used in the proof of Lemma
21
is constructed such that it is continuous in
(t,x,\zeta)
for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
.

We extend this map canonically to
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
via

\widetilde{Z}((\omega_{0},\omega_{1}),t,x,\zeta)=Z(\omega_{0},t,x,\zeta).

For notational ease, we drop the dependence on
\omega
and write

Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}:=\widetilde{Z}(\omega,t,x,\zeta),\qquad\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t}=(Z^{\cdot,\zeta}_{t})_{\#}\zeta.

(71)

Assumption 5
.

Suppose that, for some
n\geq 1
, the map
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}\in C^{n}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}})

for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
. Assume that there exist constants
C_{Z},R_{Z}>0
such that its derivatives are bounded uniformly in
(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}
and
t\in(0,1)
according to

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\sup_{(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}}\|\partial_{t}^{j}Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}\|\leq C_{Z}R_{Z}^{j}j!,\qquad 0\leq j\leq n.

We saw in equation (
35
) that
D_{z}\gamma(z,\mu)
could be expressed as the ratio of two functions that are linear in the product measure
\mu\otimes\mu
, which we denote by
\mu^{\otimes 2}
. Notice that the product measure
((Z^{\cdot,\zeta}_{t})_{\#}\zeta)^{\otimes 2}
can be expressed as the pushforward of
\zeta^{\otimes 2}
by the map

\Psi^{\zeta}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{2d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{4d},\qquad\Psi_{t}^{\zeta}(y_{1},y_{2})=\left(Z^{y_{1},\zeta}_{t},Z^{y_{2},\zeta}_{t}\right).

(72)

Similarly to
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
, we shall write
\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{t}
for
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. Notice that
\Gamma
and both terms in
\mathcal{K}
are linear in
\nu
. To reflect this in our framework, we consider functions of the following form. Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{S})\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}
be continuous in each variable and define
F
according to

\displaystyle F:\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\times\mathfrak{S}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad F(x,m,\nu)=\int f(x,m,\theta)d\nu(\theta),

(73)

for any
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{S})
. The functions
\Gamma
and
\nabla_{x}\mathcal{H}
have a very specific structure, which we encapsulate by the following assumptions.

Assumption 6
.

For each
j=1,3
, let

f_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{R}^{4d}\times\mathfrak{S}\mapsto\mathbb{R},\qquad f_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{R}^{4d}\times\mathfrak{S}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{d}

be functions that are jointly continuous in each variable such that for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,
\rho\in\mathcal{P}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
and
\theta\in\mathfrak{S}
, we have

\displaystyle f(z,\rho,\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int\exp(f_{1}(z,w,\theta))f_{2}(z,w,\theta)d\rho^{\otimes 2}(w)}{F_{2}(z,\rho,\theta)},

(74)

\displaystyle F_{2}(z,\rho,\theta)

\displaystyle=\int\exp(f_{3}(z,w,\theta))d\rho^{\otimes 2}(w).

(75)

Let
C_{Z}
be as in Assumption
5
. Further assume that there exists a constant
f_{\mathrm{max}}>0
such that for any
z\in\bar{B}_{2d}(C_{Z}),w\in(\bar{B}_{2d}(C_{Z}))^{2}
,
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
\theta\in\mathfrak{S}
, we have

\|f_{j}(z,w,\theta)\|\leq f_{\mathrm{max}},\qquad j=1,2,3.

Assumption 7
.

Fix
R>0
. Then, there exists constants
\Lambda_{F},B_{F}>0
, that depend only on
R,R_{\theta}
, such that for any
z_{1},z_{2}\in\bar{B}_{2d}(R)
,
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}_{2d}(R))
,
\theta\in\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathfrak{S})
, we have

\displaystyle\|f(z_{1},m_{1},\theta)\|

\displaystyle\leq B_{F},

\displaystyle\|F(z_{1},m_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{1})-F(z_{2},m_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{F}\left(\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|+W_{2}(m_{1},m_{2})+W_{2}(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2})\right).

Appendix H
A Maximal
L^{2}
Inequality Uniform in the Initial Condition

Let
M^{x,\zeta}_{0,\tau}=0
and, for
r\in[0:L]
, define

M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}=\int_{0}^{\frac{l}{L}}F\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{t},\mathfrak{r}_{t}^{\zeta},\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{r_{t},\tau}\right)-F\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{t},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t},\nu_{r_{t},\tau}\right)dt,

(76)

where
\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t}
is given by (
71
). The key idea is that the expected value, under
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, of a test function integrated against the random measure
\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{r/L,\tau}
, defined in (
23
), is the same test function integrated against
\nu_{r/L,\tau}
. Furthermore,
M_{l,\tau}^{x,\zeta}
is adapted to the filtration defined by

\mathcal{G}_{r}=\sigma\left(\mathcal{F}^{0}\cup\sigma\left(\mathcal{\theta}^{h}_{r^{\prime},0}:0\leq r^{\prime}<r,\,1\leq h\leq H\right)\right),\qquad\mathcal{G}_{0}=\mathcal{F}^{0}.

(77)

As a result,
M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}
is a
\mathcal{G}_{r}
-martingale for every
x,\zeta
. Before undertaking any further analysis, we must ensure that the supremum of
M^{x,\zeta}_{t,\tau}
over the initial data
\mathscr{D}
is measurable so that the expectations in the following analysis are well-defined.

Lemma 31
.

For every
r\in[0:L]
and
\tau\geq 0
, the random variable

\sup_{(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}}M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}

is defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
and is
\mathcal{G}_{r}
-measurable.

Proof.

By Assumption
4
and applying Theorem 4.1 in
[
48
]
,
(\omega^{0},x)\mapsto Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}(\omega^{0})
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
-measurable and continuous in
x
for each
\zeta\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0})
and
t\in[0,1]
. As seen in the proof of Lemma,
(\omega^{0},\theta)\mapsto\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\omega^{0})(\theta)
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d}))
-measurable. Then, Criterion
1
can be verified by applying Lemma 1.28 in
[
39
]
to the jointly measurable maps, as in the proof of Lemma
26
. As a result,
\nu_{t,\tau}
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t}
are
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable and
\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}
is
\mathcal{G}_{r}
-measurable, for every
t\in[0,1]
and
0\leq r<L
. By the Lipschitz continuity of
F
, Assumption
7
, the integrand of
M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}
in (
76
) is
\mathcal{G}_{l}
-measurable for every
0\leq l<L
. Furthermore, for each
(\omega^{0},\omega^{1})\in\Omega
, the continuity in
t
of
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}(\omega^{0})
and
\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t}(\omega^{0})
, Assumption
4
, combined with the Lipschitz continuity of
F
, Assumption
7
, implies that the map

t\mapsto F(Z_{t}^{x,\zeta},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t},\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{r_{t},\tau})-F(Z_{t}^{x,\zeta},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t},\nu_{r_{t},\tau})

is continuous on
(r/L,{r+1}/L)
. Therefore, the integrand of
M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}
is a Carathéodory function in
(\omega,t)
, and thus is
(\mathcal{G}_{r}\otimes\mathcal{B}([0,1]))
-measurable by Theorem 4.1 in
[
48
]
. By invoking Lemma 1.28 in
[
39
]
,
M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}
is
\mathcal{G}_{r}-
measurable for every
x\in\bar{B}(R_{0}),\zeta\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
.

The metric spaces
(\bar{B}(R_{0}),\|.\|)
, where
\|.\|
denotes the metric induced by the Euclidean norm, and
(\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0})),W_{2})
are separable. The latter is due to Theorem 6.18 in
[
51
]
. Therefore, let
\tilde{B}(R_{0}),\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
be countable dense subsets of
\bar{B}(R_{0}),\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
, respectively. Since
(x,\zeta)\mapsto Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
is Lipschitz by Assumption
4
, for every
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
, the map
\zeta\mapsto\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}
satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle W_{2}(\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta_{1}}_{t},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta_{2}}_{t})

\displaystyle\leq W_{2}((Z^{\cdot,\zeta_{1}}_{t})_{\#}\zeta_{1},(Z^{\cdot,\zeta_{1}}_{t})_{\#}\zeta_{2})+\left(\int\|Z^{x,\zeta_{1}}_{t}-Z^{x,\zeta_{2}}_{t}\|^{2}d\zeta_{2}(x)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\Lambda_{Z}W_{2}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}),

where
\Lambda_{Z}
is the Lipschitz constant of
(x,\zeta)\mapsto Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
on
\mathscr{D}
. Therefore,

\zeta\mapsto\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t}=(Z^{\cdot,\zeta}_{t})_{\#}\zeta
is continuous.
Combining this with the boundedness and Lipschitz continuity of
F
in each argument, Assumption
7
, the map
(x,\zeta)\mapsto M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}
is continuous, by the Dominated Convergence Theorem. As a result, the supremum

\sup_{x\in\bar{B}(R_{0})}\sup_{\zeta\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))}M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}=\sup_{x\in\tilde{B}(R_{0})}\sup_{\zeta\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}}M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}

can be taken over the countably dense subset instead. The pointwise supremum over a countable number of functions is measurable, which concludes the claim.
∎

The martingale
M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}
is the difference between two models that use parameters
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}
and
\bm{\hat{\nu}}^{N,L}_{\tau}
, respectively. Therefore, we want to show that the supremum of
\|M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}\|
over
\mathscr{D}
tends to zero in expectation. One technique we shall use to do this is Rademacher symmetrisation, Lemma
2.3.6
in
[
50
]
, which requires taking a supremum over a class of mean-zero random variables. We express the Euclidean norm as the supremum over
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
of
u^{T}M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}
, which allows us to exploit the mean-zero property of
u^{T}M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}
. Hence, we are interested in the function
J_{2}(z,w,u,\theta)=u^{T}f_{2}(z,w,\theta)
, where
f_{2}
is a function from the decomposition of
F
in Assumption
6
.

Definition 32
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}
be as in Assumption
6
. Then, for j=1,2,3, define

J_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{R}^{4d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathfrak{S}\mapsto\mathbb{R}

by

J_{1}(z,w,u,\theta)=f_{1}(z,w,\theta),\quad J_{2}(z,w,u,\theta)=\langle u,f_{2}(z,w,\theta)\rangle,\quad J_{3}(z,w,u,\theta)=f_{3}(z,w,\theta).

Although
J_{1},J_{3}
are independent of
u
, we include this dependence on
u
so that each function is defined on the same domain.

Lemma 33
.

Suppose that the
f
in equation (
73
) satisfies Assumptions
6
,
7
. Further suppose that the map
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
satisfies Assumptions
4
and
5
for some
n\geq 1
, and take
\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{t}
defined by equation (
72
).
Then, there exists a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(\Lambda_{F},C_{Z},R_{Z},f_{\mathrm{max}})
such that for any
l\in[1:L]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\Bigg[\sup_{(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}}

\displaystyle\left\|M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}\right\|\Bigg]\leq C_{3}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,w)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{\xi_{r,h,j}}{LH}\;J_{j}(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Psi^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}(w),u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right]+\frac{C_{3}l}{L^{2}},

\mathbb{P}^{0}
-almost surely, where

\mathscr{D}_{2}=\bar{B}(R_{0})\times\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{2}.

Proof.

The
\Lambda_{F}-
Lipschitz continuity of
F
, Assumption
7
, and structure of
F
given in (
73
) implies that

\displaystyle\|M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{LH}\left\|\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\left(f\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Phi_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta_{r,0}^{h})\right)-F\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\nu_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}\right)\right)\right\|

\displaystyle+2\Lambda_{F}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\int_{\frac{r}{L}}^{\frac{r+1}{L}}\left\|Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}-Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}\right\|+W_{2}\left(\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}\right)dt.

Then, the
C_{Z}R_{Z}-
Lipschitz continuity of the flow map
Z^{\zeta}_{t}
in
t
, Assumption
5
, applied to the final term yields

W_{2}\left(\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}\right)\leq\sqrt{\int\left\|Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}-Z_{\frac{r}{L}}^{x,\zeta}\right\|^{2}d\zeta(x)}\leq\frac{C_{Z}R_{Z}}{L}.

The same bound holds on
\left\|Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}-Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}\right\|
.
After applying this time discretisation error, we get

\displaystyle\|M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}u^{T}\left(f\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Phi_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta_{r,0}^{h})\right)-F\left(Z^{\xi,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\nu_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}\right)\right)

(78)

\displaystyle+\frac{4\Lambda_{F}C_{Z}R_{Z}l}{L^{2}}.

Recall the product decomposition of
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
that was outlined in Section
3.4
. As seen in the proof of Lemma
26
, the AdamW flow map
\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for any deterministic
\theta
. Furthermore, the maps
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t},\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{t}
are
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable by Assumption
4
. In fact,
(\omega^{0},x)\mapsto Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}(\omega^{0})
is a Carathéodory function, Definition 4.1 in
[
48
]
, and so is
(\mathcal{F}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
-measurable by Theorem 4.1 in
[
48
]
. Then, for any
D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, Lemma 1.28 in
[
39
]
yeilds that the map

\omega^{0}\mapsto\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t}(\omega^{0})(D)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbbm{1}_{D}(Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}(\omega^{0}))d\zeta(x)

is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable. Therefore, Criterion
2
states that
\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t}
is
(\mathcal{F}^{0},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d}))
-measurable. Accordingly, each of
Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Phi_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)
is independent of
\mathcal{F}^{1}
. Therefore, for any
r\in[0:l]
, training step
\tau\in[0:T]
, and
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.e.
\omega^{0}
, the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
acts only on
\theta^{h}_{r}
. Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[f\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Phi_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta_{r,0}^{h})\right)\right](\omega^{0})

\displaystyle=\int f\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}(\omega^{0}),\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}(\omega^{0}),\Phi_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}[\bm{\nu}(\omega^{0})](\theta_{r,0}^{h}(\omega^{1}))\right)d\mathbb{P}^{1}(\omega^{1})

\displaystyle=\int f\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}(\omega^{0}),\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}(\omega^{0}),\Phi_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}[\bm{\nu}(\omega^{0})](\theta)\right)d\pi(\theta)

\displaystyle=F\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}(\omega^{0}),\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}(\omega^{0}),\nu_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}(\omega^{0})\right),

where
\pi=\mathbb{P}^{1}\circ(\theta^{h}_{r,0})^{-1}
is the law of the parameters
\theta^{h}_{r,0}
at initialisation, Assumption
1
. From now on, we omit the dependence on
\omega^{0}
, and all equalities are understood to hold
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. The above expression implies that the first term on the right-hand side of equation (
78
) is the average over
LH
independent mean-zero random variables, when conditioned on
\mathcal{F}^{0}
. As a result, we may apply the Rademacher symmetrization given in Lemma
2.3.6
in
[
50
]
, that only requires independence of the stochastic processes, and not identical distribution. Therefore, take
i.i.d.
random variables
\epsilon_{0,1},\ldots,\epsilon_{l-1,H}
, defined on
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
, and independent of
((\theta^{h}_{r})_{r=0}^{L-1})_{h=1}^{H}
that take the values
\pm 1
with probability one half. By applying Rademacher symmetrization, Lemma
2.3.6
in
[
50
]
, under
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\sup_{(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}}\|M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}\|\bigg]\leq 2\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u)\in\mathscr{D}_{1}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\epsilon_{r,h}\;u^{T}f\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta_{r,\tau}^{h}\right)\right]+\frac{4\Lambda_{F}C_{Z}R_{Z}l}{L^{2}},

(79)

where the supremum is over the set
\mathscr{D}_{1}=\mathscr{D}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Then, writing
f
in the form given in Assumption
6
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[

\displaystyle\sup_{(x,\zeta,u)\in\mathscr{D}_{1}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\epsilon_{r,h}\;u^{T}f\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta_{r,\tau}^{h}\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u)\in\mathscr{D}_{1}}\int\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\frac{\epsilon_{r,h}}{LH}\frac{\exp\left(f_{1}(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}))\right)J_{2}(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})}{F_{2}(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})}d\zeta^{\otimes 2}(w)\bigg].

Let us introduce the notation
\psi=(x,\zeta,u,w)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}
, where
\mathscr{D}_{2}=\mathscr{D}_{1}\times(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{2}
, and define

J^{\psi}_{j,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})=J_{j}(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}),\qquad j=1,2,3.

(80)

Since an integral with respect to the measure
\zeta^{\otimes 2}
is bounded above by the supremum of the integrand over the support of
\zeta^{\otimes 2}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u)\in\mathscr{D}_{1}}\frac{1}{LH}

\displaystyle\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\epsilon_{r,h}\;u^{T}f\left(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta_{r,\tau}^{h}\right)\bigg]

(81)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,w)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\epsilon_{r,h}\frac{\exp\left(J^{x,\zeta,u,w}_{1,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right)J^{x,\zeta,u,w}_{2,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})}{F_{2}(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})}\right].

Define the map
\mathcal{Q}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle C^{\prime}=\left(f_{\mathrm{max}}\vee 1\right)e^{3f_{\mathrm{max}}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=\frac{1}{C^{\prime}}\begin{cases}\frac{exp(z_{1})z_{2}}{z_{3}}&\text{if }z_{3}>\exp(-f_{\mathrm{max}})\\
\exp(z_{1})z_{2}z_{3}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

which is zero when
z_{2}
is zero and
1
-Lipchitz, with respect to the
1
-norm, on
[-f_{\mathrm{max}},f_{\mathrm{max}}]\times[-f_{\mathrm{max}},f_{\mathrm{max}}]\times[0,\infty)
. Accordingly, the map given by

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=\mathcal{Q}\left((z_{1}\wedge f_{\mathrm{max}})\vee(-f_{\mathrm{max}}),\ (z_{2}\wedge f_{\mathrm{max}})\vee(-f_{\mathrm{max}}),\ z_{3}\vee 0\right)

is 1-Lipschitz continuous with respect to the
1
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, by composition of Lipschitz functions. Notice that
|J_{1}^{\psi}(\theta)|
and
|J_{2}^{\psi}(\theta)|
are bounded by
f_{\mathrm{max}}
for all
\psi\in\mathscr{D}_{2}
and
\theta\in\mathfrak{S}
by Assumption
6
, which applies due to the boundedness of
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
from Assumption
5
. Similarly,

F_{2}(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta)>\exp(-f_{\mathrm{max}})
for all
(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}
and
\theta\in\mathfrak{S}
. Then, the term inside the summation on the right-hand side of (
81
) coincides with
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
with
z_{1},z_{2}
evaluated at
J_{1}^{\psi}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}),J_{2}^{\psi}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})
and
z_{3}
at
F_{2}
. Therefore, the multivariate Talagrand-Ledoux contraction inequality, Theorem 16.3 in
[
49
]
, applies to equation (
81
). We take the reference function
f^{*}
in Theorem 16.3 in
[
49
]
to be identically zero as
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(0)=0
. As a result, there exists a constant
C^{\prime\prime}>0
such that, for any family of
i.i.d.
random variables
\{\xi_{r,h,i}\}
, independent of
((\theta_{r,0}^{h})_{r=0}^{L-1})_{h=1}^{H}
defined on
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1})
with
\mathcal{\xi}_{r,h,i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
for
i\in\{1,2,3\},\,0\leq r<L
and
1\leq h\leq H
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\Bigg[

\displaystyle\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,w)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\epsilon_{r,h}\frac{\exp\left(J^{x,\zeta,u,w}_{1,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right)J^{x,\zeta,u,w}_{2,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})}{F_{2}(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=C^{\prime}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,w)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\frac{\epsilon_{r,h}}{LH}\;\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}\left(J^{x,\zeta,u,y}_{1,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}),J^{x,\zeta,u,y}_{2,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}),F_{2}(Z^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{\psi\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\xi_{r,h,1}J^{\psi}_{1,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})+\xi_{r,h,2}J^{\psi}_{2,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right]

\displaystyle+C^{\prime\prime}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\xi_{r,h,3}F_{2}(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right].

(82)

Although Theorem 16.3 in
[
49
]
is for deterministic
\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}
, we proceed by taking the expectation conditioned on
\theta^{h}_{r}
first, then applying Theorem 16.3 in
[
49
]
and finally taking the expectation over the parameters. A more rigorous version of this argument is given at the end of this proof. Again,
F_{2}
is linear in its measure argument, and so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\sup_{(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\frac{\xi_{r,h,3}}{LH}\,

\displaystyle F_{2}(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\bigg]

(83)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}}\int\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\frac{\xi_{r,h,3}}{LH}\,\exp\left(J_{3,r}^{x,\zeta,u,w}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right)d\zeta^{\otimes 2}(w)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{\psi\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\xi_{r,h,3}\,\exp\left(J_{3,r}^{\psi}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right)\right].

We would like to apply the Ledoux-Talagrand contraction inequality to the above expression. However, this only applies when the noise is Rademacher. Instead, we construct two collections of Gaussian random variables and apply the Sudakov-Fernique Theorem to compare their maximum, Corollary 3.14 in
[
37
]
. Let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{S}^{l\times H}
and define
\mathcal{J}^{\psi},\mathscr{J}^{\psi}:\mathfrak{S}^{l\times H}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\mathcal{J}^{\psi}(\phi)=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\frac{\xi_{r,h,3}}{LH}\,\exp\left(J^{\psi}_{3,r}(\phi_{r,h})\right),\qquad\mathscr{J}^{\psi}(\phi)=\exp(f_{\mathrm{max}})\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\frac{\xi_{r,h,3}}{LH}\,J^{\psi}_{3,r}(\phi_{r,h}).

For any deterministic
\phi\in\mathfrak{S}^{l\times H}
and
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\mathscr{D}_{2}
, by applying the independence and zero mean of
\xi_{r,h,3}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}[|\mathcal{J}^{\psi_{1}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{J}^{\psi_{2}}(\varphi)|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{1}\Bigg[\bigg(\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\frac{\xi_{r,h,3}}{LH}\;\left\{\exp\left(J^{\psi_{1}}_{3,r}(\varphi_{r,h})\right)-\exp\left(J^{\psi_{2}}_{3,r}(\varphi_{r,h})\right)\right\}\bigg)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{1}\Bigg[\frac{1}{L^{2}H^{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\xi^{2}_{r,h,3}\;\left\{\exp\left(J^{\psi_{1}}_{3,r}(\varphi_{r,h})\right)-\exp\left(J^{\psi_{2}}_{3,r}(\varphi_{r,h})\right)\right\}^{2}\Bigg]

As a result of Assumptions
6
and the boundedness of
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
from Assumption
5
,
|J^{\psi}_{3}(\theta)|\leq f_{\mathrm{max}}
for every
\psi\in\mathscr{D}_{2}
and
\theta\in\mathfrak{S}
. Therefore, the function
\exp
is
\exp(f_{\mathrm{max}})
-Lipschitz on the range of
J^{\psi}_{3}(\theta)
. This yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\Bigg[\bigg(\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}

\displaystyle\frac{\xi_{r,h,3}}{LH}\;\left\{\exp\left(J^{\psi_{1}}_{3,r}(\varphi_{r,h})\right)-\exp\left(J^{\psi_{2}}_{3,r}(\varphi_{r,h})\right)\right\}\bigg)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq e^{2f_{\mathrm{max}}}\mathbb{E}^{1}\Bigg[\frac{1}{L^{2}H^{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\xi^{2}_{r,h,3}\;\left\{J^{\psi_{1}}_{3,r}(\varphi_{r,h})-J^{\psi_{2}}_{3,r}(\varphi_{r,h})\right\}^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{1}\Bigg[\bigg(\mathscr{J}^{\psi_{1}}(\varphi)-\mathscr{J}^{\psi_{2}}(\varphi)\bigg)^{2}\Bigg].

Since
\mathscr{D}_{2}
, equipped with the product metric induced by the Euclidean and
W_{2}
metrics, is separable, there exists a countable dense subset
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}_{2}\subseteq\mathscr{D}_{2}
. The separability of
\mathcal{P}(\bar{B}(R_{0}))
is due to Theorem 6.18 in
[
51
]
. Let
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{n}_{2}
be an increasing sequence of subsets of
\mathscr{D}_{2}
with
|\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{n}_{2}|=n
, such that

\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}^{n}_{2}=\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}_{2}.

Then, for
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.e.
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
, and any deterministic
\varphi\in\mathfrak{S}^{l\times H}
, and for
\bm{\psi}=(\psi_{1},\ldots\psi_{n})
an enumeration of
\mathscr{D}^{n}_{2}
, the vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
given by

\underline{\mathcal{J}}^{\bm{\psi}}(\varphi):=(\mathcal{J}^{\psi_{1}}(\varphi),\ldots,\mathcal{J}^{\psi_{n}}(\varphi)),\qquad\underline{\mathscr{J}}^{\bm{\psi}}(\varphi):=(\mathscr{J}^{\psi_{1}}(\varphi),\ldots,\mathscr{J}^{\psi_{n}}(\varphi)),

are mean-zero Gaussians on
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
, since each component is a linear combination of independent Gaussian random variables. The above calculation shows that
\underline{\mathcal{J}}^{\bm{\psi}}(\varphi),\underline{\mathscr{J}}^{\bm{\psi}}(\varphi)
satisfies the conditions for the Corollary 3.14 of
[
37
]
, which is a Corollary to the Sudakov-Fernique Theorem. By applying this Corollary, we obtain

\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{i\in[1:n]}\mathcal{J}^{\psi_{i}}(\varphi)\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{i\in[1:n]}\mathscr{J}^{\psi_{i}}(\varphi)\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{\psi\in\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}_{2}}\mathscr{J}^{\psi}(\varphi)\right].

By applying the Monotone Convergence Theorem to the left-hand side, we get

\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{\psi\in\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}_{2}}\mathcal{J}^{\psi}(\varphi)\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{\psi\in\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}_{2}}\mathscr{J}^{\psi}(\varphi)\right].

Recall that
f_{3}=J_{3}
is jointly continuous in each variable, Assumption
6
, and the maps

(t,x,\zeta)\mapsto Z_{t}^{x,\zeta}
and
(t,w,\zeta)\mapsto\Psi_{t}^{w,\zeta}
are jointly continuous by Assumption
4
for every
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{J}^{\psi}(\varphi)
and
\mathscr{J}^{\psi}(\varphi)
are continuous in
\psi
on
\mathscr{D}
. This implies that the supremum of
\mathcal{J}^{\psi}(\varphi)
and
\mathscr{J}^{\psi}(\varphi)
over the countable dense subset
\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}_{2}
coincides with that over
\mathscr{D}_{2}
. Since
\Theta=\{\theta_{0}^{1},\ldots,\theta^{H}_{L-1}\}
is
\mathcal{G}_{l}
-measurable, while
((\xi_{r,h,3})_{r=0}^{L-1})_{h=1}^{H}
are independent of
\mathcal{G}_{l}
, it follows from Example 4.1.7 in
[
16
]
and the tower property for conditional expectation that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{\psi\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\frac{\xi_{r,h,3}}{LH}\,\exp\left(J^{\psi}_{3,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{\psi\in\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}_{2}}\mathcal{J}^{\psi}(\Theta)\middle|\mathcal{G}_{l}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{\psi\in\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}_{2}}\mathcal{J}^{\psi}(\phi)\right]\middle|_{\phi=\Theta}\right].

By applying the bound on the expected surpemum of
\mathcal{J}^{\psi}
by that on
\mathscr{J}^{\psi}
derived above, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{\psi\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\frac{\xi_{r,h,3}}{LH}\,\exp\left(J^{\psi}_{3,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{\psi\in\widetilde{\mathscr{D}}_{2}}\mathscr{J}^{\psi}(\Theta)\middle|\mathcal{G}_{l}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{\psi\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\frac{e^{f_{\mathrm{max}}}}{LH}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l-1}\xi_{r,h,3}\;J^{\psi}_{3,r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right].

Substituting this bound back into equation (
83
) and then into (
82
) concludes the Lemma.
∎

Example choices for
J_{2}
, which we will make in Appendix
J
, are

J_{2}^{\gamma}(z,w,u,\theta)=\langle\theta_{O}u,\theta_{V}w_{1}\rangle,\qquad J_{2}^{\gamma z}(z,w,u,\theta)=\beta\langle\theta_{Q}u,\theta_{K}(w_{1}-w_{3})\rangle\langle\theta_{O}z_{2},\theta_{V}w_{1}\rangle,

corresponding to
F=\Gamma
and
\nabla_{x}\mathcal{H}
, respectively, where the subscript
j
indexes the
j
-th block of
d
components of
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and
w\in\mathbb{R}^{4d}
. The structures of
J_{1},J_{3}
are similar. Note that, for any
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
w\in\mathbb{R}^{4d}
and
\phi^{1},\ldots,\phi^{H}\in\mathfrak{S}
,

\sum_{h=1}^{H}J_{2}^{\gamma}(z,w,u,\phi^{h})=u^{T}\left(\sum_{h=1}^{H}(\phi_{O}^{h})^{T}\phi^{h}_{V}\right)w_{1}.

Hence, when
(\phi^{h}_{O})^{T}\phi_{V}^{h}
are
i.i.d.
with zero mean, the expected Frobenius norm of the sum scales like
\sqrt{H}
. However, such an interchange between
J_{2}^{\gamma z}
and summation is no longer true. Instead, we informally have that

J_{2}^{\gamma z}(z,w,u,\theta)\;\text{``=''}\;\beta(u\otimes z_{2})^{T}\left(\theta_{Q}^{T}\theta_{K}\otimes\theta_{O}^{T}\theta_{V}\right)((w_{1}-w_{3})\otimes w_{1}).

As a result, a higher-order tensor analog occurs rather than a matrix sum, which we summarise by Assumption
8
. This structure allows us to compute explicit bounds on the suprema rather than rely on Theorems, such as Dudley’s Metric Integral Theorem (5.22 in
[
52
]
), to bound the expected suprema. Therefore, the argument will not require any coverings of the space the suprema is taken over, which makes the bounds independent of the number of tokens and dimension.

Assumption 8
.

For some
\bm{q}\in\{0,1,2\}^{3}
, there exists a tensor-valued map

\widetilde{J}:\mathfrak{S}\mapsto(\mathbb{R}^{2d})^{\otimes q_{1}}\otimes(\mathbb{R}^{4d})^{\otimes q_{2}}\otimes(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes q_{3}}

such that for all
(z,w,u,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{R}^{4d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathfrak{S}
, the map
J
takes the form

\displaystyle J(z,w,u,\theta)=\left\langle\widetilde{J}(\theta),\,z^{\otimes q_{1}}\otimes w^{\otimes q_{2}}\otimes u^{\otimes q_{3}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}},

where
\langle.,.\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}
is the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product. Here, to account for the cases when
J
is independent of
z
or
u
, we use the convention
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes 0}=\mathbb{R}
and for any
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we define
u^{\otimes 0}=1
. Furthermore, there exists
C_{4}=C_{4}(\beta,R_{\theta})
such that for any
\theta\in\mathfrak{S}

\left\|\widetilde{J}(\theta)\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq C_{4}.

Corollary 34
.

Given
l,n\geq 1
and
M,R>0
, take
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},z_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d},w_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{4d}
,
\theta_{s}\in\mathfrak{S}
and
e_{s},c_{s}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\|z_{j}\|,\|w_{j}\|\leq MR^{j}

for
j\in[0:n-1],s\in[1:l]
. Suppose that
J
satisfies Assumption
8
for some
\bm{q}\in\{0,1,2\}^{3}
. Then, with
q^{\prime}=q_{1}+q_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{s=1}^{l}e_{s}J\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}z_{j}c^{j}_{s},\ \sum_{j=0}^{n-1}w_{j}c^{j}_{s},\ u,\theta_{s}\right)\right|\leq M^{q^{\prime}}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q^{\prime}}=0}^{n-1}\left\|\sum_{s=1}^{l}(Rc_{s})^{j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q^{\prime}}}\,e_{s}\widetilde{J}(\theta_{s})\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}},

where the superscript
j
on
c_{s}
denote the
j
-th power of
c_{s}
.

Proof.

For
v_{0},\ldots,v_{n-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
q\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we have the following identity

\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}v_{j}\right)^{\otimes q}=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q}=0}^{n-1}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{q}v_{j_{i}}.

Since
J
satisfies Assumption
8
, and using the above identity, we get

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{s=1}^{l}e_{s}J

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}z_{j}(c_{s})^{j},\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}w_{j}(c_{s})^{j},u,\theta_{s}\bigg)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{s=1}^{l}e_{s}\left\langle\widetilde{J}(\theta_{s}),\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}z_{j}(c_{s})^{j}\right)^{\otimes q_{1}}\otimes\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}w_{j}(c_{s})^{j}\right)^{\otimes q_{2}}\otimes u^{\otimes q_{3}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{s=1}^{l}e_{s}\left\langle\widetilde{J}(\theta_{s}),\left(\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q_{1}}=0}^{n-1}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{q_{1}}z_{j_{i}}(c_{s})^{j_{i}}\right)\otimes\left(\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q_{2}}=0}^{n-1}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{q_{2}}z_{j_{i}}(c_{s})^{j_{i}}\right)\otimes u^{\otimes q_{3}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q^{\prime}}=0}^{n-1}\left|\left\langle\sum_{s=1}^{l}e_{s}c^{j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q^{\prime}}}_{s}\widetilde{J}(\theta_{s}),\bigotimes_{i=1}^{q_{1}}z_{j_{i}}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=q_{1}+1}^{q^{\prime}}w_{j_{i}}\otimes u^{\otimes q_{3}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\right|,

with the convention that
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{0}z_{j_{i}}=1
.
Then, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the fact that the Hilbert-Schmidt norm is multiplicative under tensor products and the assumption that the norms of
w_{j},z_{j}
scale with
R^{j}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{s=1}^{l}e_{s}J\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}z_{j}c^{j}_{s}

\displaystyle,\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}w_{j}c^{j}_{s},u,\theta_{s}\bigg)\bigg|\leq M^{q^{\prime}}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q^{\prime}}=0}^{n-1}\left\|\sum_{s=1}^{l}e_{s}(Rc_{s})^{j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q^{\prime}}}\widetilde{J}(\theta_{s})\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}.

∎

Corollary 35
.

Suppose that Assumption
8
holds for some
\bm{q}\in\{0,1,2\}^{3}
and fix
R>0
. Then, there exists a constant
\Lambda_{J}=\Lambda_{J}(\bm{q},R)
such that for any
z_{1},z_{2}\in\bar{B}_{2d}(R),w_{1},w_{2}\in(\bar{B}_{2d}(R))^{2}
,
u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
e_{1},\ldots,e_{l}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{l}\in\ \mathfrak{S}
, we have

\left|\sum_{s=1}^{l}e_{s}\left(J(z_{1},w_{1},u,\theta_{s})-J(z_{2},w_{2},u,\theta_{s})\right)\right|\leq\Lambda_{J}\left(\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|+\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|\right)\left\|\sum_{s=1}^{l}e_{s}\widetilde{J}(\theta_{s})\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}.

Proof.

Since
J
satisfies
8
, we get that

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{s=1}^{l}

\displaystyle e_{s}\bigg(J(z_{1},w_{1},u,\theta_{s})-J(z_{2},w_{2},u,\theta_{s})\bigg)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left\langle\sum_{s=1}^{m}e_{s}\widetilde{J}(\theta_{s}),\left(\left(z_{1}^{\otimes q_{1}}-z_{2}^{\otimes q_{1}}\right)\otimes w_{1}^{q_{2}}+z_{2}^{\otimes q_{1}}\otimes\left(w_{1}^{q_{2}}-w_{2}^{\otimes q_{2}}\right)\right)\otimes u^{\otimes q_{3}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}\right|.

Therefore, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and using that the Hilbert-Schmidt norm is multiplicative under tensor products, we get

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{s=1}^{l}

\displaystyle e_{s}\bigg(J(z_{1},w_{1},u,\theta_{s})-J(z_{2},w_{2},u,\theta_{s})\bigg)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\left((\sqrt{2}R)^{q_{2}}\left\|z_{1}^{\otimes q_{1}}-z_{2}^{\otimes q_{1}}\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}+R^{q_{1}}\|w_{1}^{\otimes q_{2}}-w_{2}^{\otimes q_{2}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\right)\left\|\sum_{s=1}^{m}e_{s}\widetilde{J}(\theta_{s})\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}.

The Corollary follows from the fact that, for any
v_{1},v_{2}\in\bar{B}(R)
and
q\geq 1
,

\|v_{1}^{\otimes q}-v_{2}^{\otimes q}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq qR^{q-1}\|v_{1}-v_{2}\|.

If
q=0
, we use the convention that
v_{1}^{\otimes 0}=v_{2}^{\otimes 0}=1
, so that
\|v_{1}^{\otimes 0}-v_{2}^{\otimes 0}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=0\leq\|v_{1}-v_{2}\|
.
∎

Lemma 36
.

Suppose that the flow map
Z_{t}^{x,\zeta}
, with
\Psi_{t}^{w,\zeta}
defined in (
72
), satisfies Assumptions
4
and
5
for some integer
n\geq 1
and that
J
satisfies Assumption
8
for some
\bm{q}\in\{0,1,2\}^{3}
.
Let
\xi_{r,h}
be
i.i.d.
samples from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, independent of
((\theta^{h}_{r,0})_{r=0}^{L-1})_{h=1}^{H}
defined on
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
, for
r\in[0:l-1]
and
h\in[1:H]
. Then, there exists a constant
C_{M,J}=C_{M,J}(\bm{q},\beta,C_{Z},R_{Z},n)
such that for any training step
\tau\in[0:T]
and layer number
r^{\prime}\in[1:L]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,w)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{r=0}^{r^{\prime}-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\xi_{r,h}J(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{M,J}\left(\frac{1}{L}+\frac{1}{L^{\frac{n}{2n+1}}H^{1/2}}\right),

(84)

\mathbb{P}^{0}
-almost surely.

Remark 37
.

By the subadditivity of the supremum, Lemma
36
extends to the case where
J
is a linear combination of functions that satisfy Assumption
8
with possibly different
\bm{q}
.

Proof.

Take
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
m\leq\sqrt{L}
.
Partition the layers into
\lfloor r^{\prime}/m\rfloor
contiguous blocks, and let
\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{t}
denote the
(n-1)^{th}
order Taylor approximation to
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
centered at
m\lfloor Lt/m\rfloor/L
so that

\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}}:=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\partial_{t}^{j}Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}\big|_{t=\frac{lm}{L}}\frac{(r-lm)^{j}}{L^{j}j!},\qquad\text{for}\;\;r\in[lm:(l+1)m-1].

(85)

Define
\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{t}
similarly.
Then, approximating
Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}
using
\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}}
, the expected supremum of
J
is bounded by two parts corresponding to

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{r^{\prime}-1}\frac{\xi_{r,h}}{LH}J^{\psi}_{r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\rfloor}\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\mathbbm{1}_{r<r^{\prime}}\frac{\xi_{r,h}}{LH}\left(J^{\psi}_{r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})-J(\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\rfloor}\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\mathbbm{1}_{r<r^{\prime}}\frac{\xi_{r,h}}{LH}J(\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}),

(86)

where for ease of notation, similarly to equation (
80
), we have introduced
\psi=(x,\zeta,u,w)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}
and

J^{\psi}_{r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})=J(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}).

The indicator function has been introduced to account for the case when
r^{\prime}
is not divisible by
m
.
Then, substituting in the definition of
\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Psi^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}}
, the second term on the right-hand side of equation (
86
) satisfies

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}

\displaystyle\xi_{r,h}J(\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\bigg|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\xi_{r,h}J\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\partial_{t}^{j}Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{lm}{L}}\frac{(r-lm)^{j}}{L^{j}j!},\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\partial_{t}^{j}\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{\frac{lm}{L}}\frac{(r-lm)^{j}}{L^{j}j!},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}\right)\right|.

Then, since Assumptions
5
and
8
hold, we apply Corollary
34
with
q=q_{1}+q_{2}
to get

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}

\displaystyle\xi_{r,h}J(\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\bigg|

(87)

\displaystyle\leq C_{Z}^{q}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q}=0}^{n-1}R_{Z}^{j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q}}\left\|\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\xi_{r,h}\left(\frac{r-lm}{L}\right)^{j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q}}\widetilde{J}\left(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}.

Notice that the right-hand side of equation (
87
) is independent of
x,\zeta,u,w
. Therefore, the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of the supremum over
\mathscr{D}_{2}
of the left-hand side of equation (
86
) is
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s. controlled by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[

\displaystyle\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,y)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\rfloor}\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mathbbm{1}_{r<r^{\prime}}\xi_{r,h}J(\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\frac{C_{Z}^{q}}{LH}\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\rfloor}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q}=0}^{n-1}R_{Z}^{j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q}}\left\|\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mathbbm{1}_{r<r^{\prime}}\xi_{r,h}\widetilde{J}\left(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}\right)\left(\frac{r-lm}{L}\right)^{j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q}}\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\right].

Then, by applying Jensen’s inequality followed by the independence and zero mean of the random variables
\xi_{r,h}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[

\displaystyle\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,y)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\rfloor}\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mathbbm{1}_{r<r^{\prime}}\xi_{r,h}J(\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{Z}^{q}}{LH}\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\rfloor}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q}=0}^{n-1}R_{Z}^{j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q}}\sqrt{\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mathbbm{1}_{r<r^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\xi^{2}_{r,h}\left\|\widetilde{J}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}}\right]\left(\frac{r-lm}{L}\right)^{2(j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q})}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{4}C_{Z}^{q}}{\sqrt{LH}}\left(\left\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\right\rfloor+1\right)\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q}=0}^{n-1}R_{Z}^{j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\left(\frac{i}{L}\right)^{2(j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q})}}.

The final inequality uses the boundedness assumption on
\widetilde{J}
, see Assumption
8
. Note that for any
c>0

\frac{1}{L}\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\left(\frac{i}{L}\right)^{c}\leq\int_{0}^{\frac{m}{L}}x^{c}dx=\frac{1}{c+1}\left(\frac{m}{L}\right)^{c+1}.

(88)

Therefore, by applying bound (
88
) and simplifying the resulting geometric series, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,y)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\frac{1}{LH}

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\rfloor}\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mathbbm{1}_{r<r^{\prime}}\xi_{r,h}J(\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{4}C_{Z}^{q}}{\sqrt{LH}}\left(\left\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\right\rfloor+1\right)\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q}=0}^{n-1}\left(\frac{R_{Z}m}{L}\right)^{j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q}}\sqrt{\frac{m}{L}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2C_{4}C_{Z}^{q}}{\sqrt{mH}}\left(\frac{1-\left(\frac{R_{Z}m}{L}\right)^{n}}{1-\frac{R_{Z}m}{L}}\right)^{q}.

(89)

The final inequality uses the fact
\lfloor r^{\prime}/m\rfloor+1\leq 2L/m
. For
0\leq r<L
with
l=\lfloor r/m\rfloor
, by Taylor’s Theorem and Assumption
5
on
\|\partial_{t}^{j}Z_{t}^{x,\zeta}\|
, for any
(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}
there exists some
s\in(lm/L,r/L)
such that

\displaystyle\|Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}-\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}}\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\partial_{t}^{n}Z_{t}^{x,\zeta}|_{t=s}\frac{(r-lm)^{n}}{n!L^{n}}\right\|\leq C_{Z}\left(R_{Z}\frac{r-lm}{L}\right)^{n},

(90)

\displaystyle\|\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}}\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{Z}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(R_{Z}\frac{r-lm}{L}\right)^{i}\leq C_{Z}\frac{1-\left(\frac{R_{Z}}{\sqrt{L}}\right)^{n}}{1-\frac{R_{Z}}{\sqrt{L}}},

(91)

since
(1-x^{n})/(1-x)
is increasing in
x>0
for any
n\geq 1
and
R_{Z}m/L\leq R_{Z}/\sqrt{L}
. Since
\Psi
is given by (
72
), identical bounds hold for
\Psi
with
C_{Z}
replaced by
\sqrt{2}C_{Z}
. Since Assumption
8
holds and
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t},\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{t},\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{t}
,and
\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{t}
are bounded
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.s., Corollary
35
applies, giving a constant
\Lambda_{J}=\Lambda_{J}(R_{Z},C_{Z},n,\bm{q})
such that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\sum_{h=1}^{H}\xi_{r,h}

\displaystyle\left(J(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})-J(\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{J}\left(\|Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}-\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}}\|+\|\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}}-\Psi^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}}\|\right)\Bigg\|\sum_{h=1}^{H}\xi_{r,h}\widetilde{J}\left(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}\right)\Bigg\|_{\mathrm{HS}}.

Therefore, by the Lipschitz continuity of
J
, given above, and the Taylor approximation error (
90
), the first term on the right-hand side of equation (
86
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,w)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\rfloor}\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mathbbm{1}_{r<r^{\prime}}\xi_{r,h}\left(J^{x,\zeta,u,w}_{r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})-J(\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\Lambda_{J}}{LH}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\rfloor}\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\mathbbm{1}_{r<r^{\prime}}C_{Z}\left(R_{Z}\frac{r-lm}{L}\right)^{n}\Bigg\|\sum_{h=1}^{H}\xi_{r,h}\widetilde{J}\left(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}\right)\Bigg\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\right].

Since
\{\xi_{0,1},\ldots\xi_{L-1,H}\}
are independent
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
random variables, Jensen’s inequality together with Assumption
8
imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,w)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\rfloor}\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\mathbbm{1}_{r<r^{\prime}}\xi_{r,h}\left(J^{x,\zeta,u,w}_{r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})-J(\hat{Z}^{x,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\hat{\Psi}^{w,\zeta,(n-1)}_{\frac{r}{L}},\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3C_{Z}\Lambda_{J}}{LH}\sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\rfloor}\sum_{r=lm}^{(l+1)m-1}\mathbbm{1}_{r<r^{\prime}}\left(R_{Z}\frac{r-lm}{L}\right)^{n}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sum_{h=1}^{H}\xi_{r,h}^{2}\left\|\widetilde{J}\left(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau}\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\Lambda_{J}C_{4}C_{Z}R_{Z}^{n}}{(n+1)\sqrt{H}}\left(\left\lfloor\frac{r^{\prime}}{m}\right\rfloor+1\right)\left(\frac{m}{L}\right)^{n+1}.

(92)

The last step follows from (
88
). Then, by combining equations (
89
) and (
92
),
there exists a constant
C_{M,J}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,w)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{L-1}\xi_{r,h}J^{x,\zeta,u,w}_{r}(\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\bigg]\leq C_{M,J}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{L}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{mH}}+\frac{1}{n\sqrt{H}}\left(\frac{m}{L}\right)^{n}\right).

(93)

Notice that the expression

\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}+\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{m}{L}\right)^{n}

is maximised at
m=L^{\frac{n}{n+1/2}}2^{-\frac{1}{n+1/2}}
, over
m\leq\sqrt{L}
. At this value of
m
, after absorbing any constants into
C^{\prime}_{M,J}
, we have

\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\sup_{(x,\zeta,u,w)\in\mathscr{D}_{2}}\frac{1}{LH}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\sum_{r=0}^{L-1}\xi_{r,h}J(Z^{x,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{\frac{r}{L}},u,\theta^{h}_{r,\tau})\bigg]\leq C^{\prime}_{M,\Gamma}\left(\frac{1}{L}+\frac{1}{H^{1/2}L^{\frac{n}{2n+1}}}\right).

∎

Lemma 38
.

Suppose that Assumptions
6
,
7
and
8
hold for
F
and its associated
J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}
for some
\bm{q}\in\{0,1,2\}^{3}
, and that the map
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
satisfies Assumptions
4
and
5
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, with
\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{t}
defined in (
72
). Then, for
M^{x,\zeta}_{l,\tau}
defined in (
76
),
there exists a constant
C_{M}^{*}=C_{M}^{*}(B_{F},R_{Z},C_{Z},\bm{q},\beta,n,\Lambda_{F})
such that for any
l\in[0:L]
, we have

\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{r\in[0:l]}\sup_{(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}}\|M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}\|^{2}\right]\leq C^{*}_{M}\left(L^{-2}+L^{-\frac{2n}{2n+1}}H^{-1}\right),\qquad\mathbb{P}^{0}-a.s.

Proof.

Recall the filtration
\mathcal{G}_{r}
defined in equation (
77
).
Then,
M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}
is
\mathcal{G}_{r}
-measurable, as seen in the proof of Lemma
31
, and it is bounded by
2B_{F}
by Assumption
7
. Since,
\theta^{h}_{r,0}
is independent of
\mathcal{G}_{r^{\prime},\tau}
for any
r^{\prime}\leq r
, the martingale differences satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[

\displaystyle M^{x,\zeta}_{r+1,\tau}-M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}\big|\mathcal{G}_{r,\tau}\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\int_{\frac{r}{L}}^{\frac{r+1}{L}}\mathbb{E}\left[f(Z^{\xi,\zeta}_{t},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t},\Psi_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta^{h}_{r,0}))-F(Z^{\xi,\zeta}_{t},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t},\nu_{\frac{r}{L},\tau})\middle|\mathcal{G}_{r}\right]dt

\displaystyle=0,

after following the same argument as seen in the proof of Lemma
33
to compute the expectation. Therefore,
M_{r,\tau}^{x,\zeta}
is a
\mathcal{G}_{r}-
martingale for every
(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}
. Let us consider

M^{*}_{r,\tau}:=\sup_{(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}}\|M_{r,\tau}^{x,\zeta}\|,

(94)

which is bounded almost surely by
2B_{F}
according to Assumption
7
. Furthermore,
M^{*}_{r,\tau}
is
\mathcal{G}_{r}
-measurable by Lemma
31
.
As a result,
M^{*}_{r,\tau}

is a non-negative
\mathcal{G}_{r}-
sub-martingale, and so applying Doob’s
L^{2}
inequality, Chapter II Theorem 1.7 in
[
44
]
, yields

\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{r\in[0:l]}\left(M^{*}_{r,\tau}\right)^{2}\right]\leq 4\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\left(M^{*}_{l,\tau}\right)^{2}\right],\qquad\mathbb{P}^{0}-a.s.

Since Assumption
6
holds, Lemma
33
deduces an
L^{1}
upper bound on
M^{*}_{L,\tau}
in terms of the functions
J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}
. Therefore, applying Lemma
33
followed by Lemma
36
on each of the resulting terms, we see that there exists a constant
C_{M,1}
such that

\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\|M^{*}_{l,\tau}\|\right]\leq C_{M,1}\left(L^{-1}+L^{-\frac{n}{2n+1}}H^{-1/2}\right),

(95)

for every
l\in[0:L]
.
However, our claim is an
L^{2}
bound on
M^{*}_{L-1,\tau}
rather than
L^{1}
. Therefore, for a fixed
l\in[0:L-1]
, consider
\Upsilon^{x,\zeta}:(\mathbb{R}^{k\times 4d})^{(l+1)H}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{x,\zeta}(\phi_{0,1},\ldots,\phi_{l,H})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{H}\sum_{r=0}^{l}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\int_{\frac{r}{L}}^{\frac{r+1}{L}}\left(F_{3,t,\tau}^{x,\zeta}(\phi_{r,h})-\int F_{3,t}^{x,\zeta}(\theta)\;d\pi(\theta)\right)dt,

\displaystyle F_{3,t,\tau}^{x,\zeta}(\theta)

\displaystyle=F(Z^{x,\zeta}_{t},\mathfrak{r}^{\zeta}_{t},\Phi_{t,\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)),

and denote the supremum of the norm of
\Upsilon^{x,\zeta}
over
(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}
by

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\phi_{0,1},\ldots,\phi_{l,H})

\displaystyle=\sup_{(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}}\|\Upsilon^{x,\zeta}(\phi_{0,1},\ldots,\phi_{l,H})\|.

Then, Assumption
7
states that
F
is bounded by
B_{F}
, and so

\displaystyle\|F_{3,t}^{x,\zeta}(\phi_{r,h})-F_{3,t}^{x,\zeta}(\phi^{\prime}_{r,h})\|\leq 2B_{F},

for every
\phi_{r,h},\phi_{r,h}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}
,
which immediately gives

\|\Upsilon^{x,\zeta}(\phi_{0,1},\ldots,\phi_{r,h},\ldots,\phi_{l,H})-\Upsilon^{x,\zeta}(\phi_{0,1},\ldots,\phi^{\prime}_{r,h},\ldots,\phi_{l,H})\|\leq\frac{2B_{F}}{LH}.

Therefore, the triangle inequality implies that for any
(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}

\displaystyle\|\Upsilon^{x,\zeta}(\theta_{0,1},\ldots,\theta_{r,h},\ldots,\theta_{l,H})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\Upsilon^{x,\zeta}(\theta_{0,1},\ldots,\theta^{\prime}_{r,h},\ldots,\theta_{l,H})\|+\frac{2B_{F}}{LH}

\displaystyle\leq\Upsilon(\theta_{0,1},\ldots,\theta^{\prime}_{r,h},\ldots,\theta_{l,H})+\frac{2B_{F}}{LH}.

Since the above expression holds for every
(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}
, by the definition of the supremum we have that

\Upsilon(\phi_{0,1},\ldots,\phi_{r,h},\ldots,\phi_{l,H})\leq\Upsilon(\phi_{0,1},\ldots,\phi^{\prime}_{r,h},\ldots,\phi_{l,H})+\frac{2B_{F}}{LH}.

The same bound holds with
\phi_{r,h},\phi_{r,h}^{\prime}
interchanged, and so
\Upsilon
satisfies
the bounded differences property

\displaystyle|\Upsilon(\phi_{0,1},\ldots,\phi_{r,h},\ldots,\phi_{l,H})-\Upsilon(\phi_{0,1},\ldots,\phi^{\prime}_{r,h},\ldots,\phi_{l,H})|\leq\frac{2B_{F}}{LH}.

Notice that

\Upsilon(\theta^{1}_{0,0},\ldots,\theta^{H}_{l,0})=M^{*}_{l+1,\tau},

where
\theta^{1}_{0,0},\ldots,\theta^{H}_{l,0}
are the parameters of the discrete-time model at initialisation and so are
i.i.d.
.
Therefore, McDiarmid’s inequality, Lemma 1.2 in
[
36
]
, applies
and yields the tail bound

\mathbb{P}^{1}\left(\left|M^{*}_{l+1,\tau}-\mathbb{E}^{1}M_{l+1,\tau}^{*}\right|\geq t\right)\leq 2\ exp\left(-\frac{LHt^{2}}{2B_{F}^{2}}\right),

(96)

for any
t>0
. Denote the variance of
M^{*}_{l+1,\tau}
under
\mathbb{P}^{1}
by
\mathbb{V}^{1}(M^{*}_{l+1,\tau})
. This can be bounded above by integrating the tail bound over
t\in(0,\infty)
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}^{1}(M^{*}_{l+1,\tau})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{1}\left(|M^{*}_{l+1,\tau}-\mathbb{E}^{1}M^{*}_{l+1,\tau}|^{2}\geq t\right)dt\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}2e^{-\frac{LHt}{2B^{2}_{F}}}dt=\frac{4B_{F}^{2}}{LH}.

(97)

The Lemma concludes by substituting bounds (
95
) and (
97
) into

\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{r\in[0:l]}\left(M^{*}_{r,\tau}\right)^{2}\right]\leq 4\left(\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[M^{*}_{l,\tau}\right]\right)^{2}+4\mathbb{V}^{1}(M^{*}_{l,\tau}).

∎

Remark 39
.

The second term in
\mathcal{K}
is not of the form given in equation (
73
). Instead, it can be written as

f(z,\rho,\theta)=\int\frac{\exp(f_{1}(z,w,\theta))\;f_{2}(z,w,\theta)}{F_{2}(w,\rho,\theta)}d\rho^{\otimes 2}(w).

(98)

The difference is that the first argument of the denominator
F_{2}
is
w
rather than
z
, and thus is integrated over. However, this discrepancy corresponds to replacing
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
with
\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{t}
in the denominator of
F_{2}
appearing in equation (
81
). Since
\Psi_{t}^{(w_{1},w_{2}),\zeta}=(Z_{t}^{w_{1},\zeta},Z_{t}^{w_{2},\zeta})
, it inherits the same properties as
Z_{t}^{x,\zeta}
. Thus, we may replace
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
by
\Psi^{w,\zeta}_{t}
without effecting the subsequent analysis. This implies that Lemma
38
still holds if Assumption
6
is changed so that
f
satisfies (
98
) instead of (
74
).

Appendix I
Stochastic Approximation Lemma

Let
\bm{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}
and denote the empirical measure on
y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}
by
\zeta^{N}
. Then, according to (
64
), the solution
(X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})
to (
17
) corresponds to the solution to (
56
)-(
57
), with initial condition
x=y_{i}
and
\zeta=\zeta^{N}
. Thus, for all
t\in[0,1]
, we define

Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{t}:=Z^{y_{i},\zeta^{N}}_{t},\qquad\mathfrak{m}^{N,\bm{y}}_{t}:=(Z^{\cdot,\zeta^{N}}_{t})_{\#}\zeta^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{t}},

where
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
is the map defined in Appendix
G
.

Furthermore, let us introduce a piecewise constant approximation to
Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{t}
that corresponds to the discrete-time transformer (
4
). For each
i\in[1:N]
, define
\bar{Z}_{t}^{i,L,\bm{y}}:\Omega\times[0,1]\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{2d}
by

\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t}:=\sum_{r=0}^{L}\mathbbm{1}_{Lt\in[r,r+1)}z^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{r},

where each
z^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{r}
is a random variable taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and defined on the complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
that was introduced in Section
3.4
. Since
\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t}
is a finite sum of
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{B}([0,1]))
-measurable functions,
(\omega,t)\mapsto\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t}(\omega)
is
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{B}([0,1]))
-measurable. We define the empirical measure associated to
((\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}})_{i=1}^{N})_{t\in[0,1]}
by

\bar{m}^{N,L,\bm{y}}_{t}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t}}.

Furthermore, we assume that the map
\bm{y}\mapsto(\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t}(\omega))_{i=1}^{N}
is continuous for every
\omega\in\Omega
and
t\in[0,1]
.
The following Lemma combines the estimates from Appendices
F
and
H
to bound the difference between the continuous-time mean-field model and its discrete counterpart.

Lemma 40
.

Suppose that
F
with its associated
f,J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}
satisfy Assumptions
6
,
7
, and
8
. Suppose further that Assumptions
4
and
5
hold for the flow map
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
for some
n\geq 1
. Then, for

r\in[0:L-1],i\in[1:N]
and
\tau\in[1:T]
, we define

\displaystyle\Delta_{r,\tau}^{i,\bm{y}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{L}}F(Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{s},\mathfrak{m}^{N,\bm{y}}_{s},\nu_{s,\tau})-F(\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{s},\bar{m}^{N,L,\bm{y}}_{s},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{s,\tau})ds\right\|^{2},

(99)

where
\bm{\nu}_{\tau},\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}_{\tau}
are the parameters of models (
17
) and (
16
) at training step
\tau
, respectively.
Then, there exists a constant
C_{5}=C_{5}(\Lambda_{F},D_{1},D_{2},C_{Z},R_{Z},C^{*}_{M})
such that for any training step

\tau\in[1:T]
and
l\in[0:L-1]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{r\in[0:l]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}\Delta_{r,\tau}^{i,\bm{y}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{5}}{L}\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{l}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{r\in[0:r^{\prime}]}\max_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}\|Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L}}-\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L}}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle+C_{5}\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{HL^{\frac{2n}{2n+1}}}+\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}\mathscr{L}_{j}\right),

\mathbb{P}^{0}
-almost surely, where
\kappa^{0}_{i,T}
is defined in (
65
) and
\mathscr{L}_{j}
is defined by

\mathscr{L}_{\tau}=\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}\|X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}-\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}\|^{2}+\|a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}-\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}\|^{2}\right].

(100)

Proof.

Since
(t,\bm{y})\mapsto Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
and
\bm{y}\mapsto\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{\tau}
are continuous for every
\omega\in\Omega
, by assumption, and all subsequent variables depend continuously on
Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
and
\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
, it follows that all suprema over
(t,\bm{y})\in[0,1]\times(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}
are
\mathcal{F}
-measurable. This is because
[0,1]\times(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}
is separable and so the suprema can be taken over a countable dense subset.

As discussed in Section 2.1.3 in
[
8
]
Vol. II., for any
\mathcal{F}
-measurable random variable
\mathcal{Z}
, the section
\mathcal{Z}(\omega^{0},.)
is
\mathcal{F}^{1}
-measurable
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.e. in
\omega^{0}
. Therefore, there exists a set subset
\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.,2}}\subseteq\Omega^{0}
with full
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-measure such that the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
on all subsequent variables is well-defined for all
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{w.d.,2}}
.

Let us expand
\Delta^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime},\tau}
into different sources of error according to

\displaystyle\Delta^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime},\tau}

\displaystyle\leq 3\underbrace{\left\|\int_{0}^{\frac{r^{\prime}+1}{L}}F(Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{s},\mathfrak{m}^{N,\bm{y}}_{s},\nu_{s,\tau})-F(Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{s},\mathfrak{m}^{N,\bm{y}}_{s},\nu_{r_{s},\tau})ds\right\|^{2}}_{(c)^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime}}}

(101)

\displaystyle+3\underbrace{\left\|\int_{0}^{\frac{r^{\prime}+1}{L}}F(Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{s},\mathfrak{m}^{N,\bm{y}}_{s},\nu_{r_{s},\tau})-F(Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{s},\mathfrak{m}^{N,\bm{y}}_{s},\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{r_{s},\tau})ds\right\|^{2}}_{(d)^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle+3\underbrace{\left\|\int_{0}^{\frac{r^{\prime}+1}{L}}F\left(Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{s},\mathfrak{m}^{N,\bm{y}}_{s},\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{r_{s},\tau}\right)-F(\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{s},\bar{m}^{N,L,\bm{y}}_{s},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{r_{s},\tau})ds\right\|^{2}}_{(e)^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime}}}.

For the term labeled
(c)
, applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the Lipschitz continuity of
F
, Assumption
7
, gives

(c)^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime}}\leq\Lambda_{F}^{2}\frac{r^{\prime}+1}{L}\int_{0}^{\frac{r^{\prime}+1}{L}}W^{2}_{2}(\nu_{s,\tau},\nu_{r_{s},\tau})ds,\qquad\mathbb{P}-a.s.

Lemma
30
states that
W_{2}(\nu_{s,\tau},\nu_{t,\tau})
is
D_{2}
-Lipchitz continuous uniformly in training step
\tau
, and so we have that

(c)^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime}}\leq\Lambda_{F}^{2}D_{2}^{2}L^{-2},\qquad\mathbb{P}-a.s.

(102)

For the term labelled
(e)
, we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the Lipschitz continuity of
F
to get

\displaystyle(e)^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime}}\leq\frac{3\Lambda_{F}^{2}(r^{\prime}+1)}{L}\int_{0}^{\frac{r^{\prime}+1}{L}}\|Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{t}-\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t}\|^{2}+W_{\infty}^{2}\left(\mathfrak{m}^{N,\bm{y}}_{t},\bar{m}^{N,L,\bm{y}}_{t}\right)+W_{2}^{2}(\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{r_{t},\tau},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{r_{t},\tau})dt.

Since
\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,0}=\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,0}
, the Wasserstein distance between
\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}
and
\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}
can be bounded using the coupling induced by pushing forward
\bar{\nu}^{L,H}_{t,0}
by their respective flow maps. This coupling yields

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau})\leq\int\|\Phi_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)-\Phi_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}](\theta)\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}d\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{\frac{r}{L},0}(\theta).

By applying the Lipschitz continuity of the flow map
\Phi[\bm{\nu}]
in the measure argument, Lemma
28
whose hypotheses are satisfied because of Lemma
30
, we obtain

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\hat{\nu}^{N,L}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau})\leq\frac{D_{1}}{B}\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}W^{2}_{\infty}(\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{\frac{r}{L},j},\rho^{N,b}_{\frac{r}{L},j}),

(103)

where
\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}
is defined in (
65
) and
D_{1}
is the constant from Lemma
28
. Therefore,
(e)
is
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. bounded above by

\displaystyle(e)^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq 3\Lambda_{F}^{2}\left(\int_{0}^{\frac{r^{\prime}+1}{L}}2W_{\infty}^{2}\left(\mathfrak{m}^{N,\bm{y}}_{t},\bar{m}^{N,L,\bm{y}}_{t}\right)dt+\frac{D_{1}}{BL}\sum_{r=0}^{r^{\prime}}\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}W^{2}_{\infty}(\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{\frac{r}{L},j},\rho^{N,b}_{\frac{r}{L},j})\right).

(104)

Since
\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t}
is constant over the interval
[r_{t},t]
and
Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{t}
is
C_{Z}R_{Z}
-Lipschitz continuous in
t
due to Assumption
5
, Young’s inequality implies that

\|Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{t}-\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t}\|^{2}\leq 2\|Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{r_{t}}-\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{r_{t}}\|^{2}+2\frac{(C_{Z}R_{Z})^{2}}{L^{2}},\qquad\mathbb{P}-a.s.

Notice that the expectation under
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, averaged over the batch of training data, of the
\infty
-Wasserstein distance between
\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{\frac{r}{L},j}
and
\rho^{N,b}_{\frac{r}{L},j}
is less than or equal to
\mathscr{L}_{j}
defined in equation (
100
) for every
r\in[0:L-1]
.
Therefore, for
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-a.e.
\omega^{0}
and adjusting for the time discretisation error, the expectation of the right-hand side of equation (
104
) under
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is bounded according to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\max_{r^{\prime}\in[0:l]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}(e)^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{12\Lambda_{F}^{2}}{L}\sum_{r=0}^{l}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{r^{\prime}\in[0:r]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}\|Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r^{\prime}}{L}}-\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r^{\prime}}{L}}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle+3\Lambda_{F}^{2}\left(\frac{4(C_{Z}R_{Z})^{2}}{L^{2}}+D_{1}\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}\mathscr{L}_{j}\right).

(105)

Recall the definition of
M^{x,\zeta}_{r,\tau}
given by equation (
76
). Notice that, for every
i\in[1:N]
,

\bm{y}\in(B(R_{0}))^{N}
,
(d)^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime}}
is
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. bounded above by the supremum of
\|M^{x,\zeta}_{r^{\prime}+1,\tau}\|^{2}
over
(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}
, denoted
M^{*}_{r^{\prime}+1,\tau}
.
The current setting satisfies the assumptions for Lemma
38
(by design), thus Lemma
38
applies and gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{r\in[0:l]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}(d)^{i,\bm{y}}_{r^{\prime}}\right]\leq C_{M}^{*}\left(L^{-2}+H^{-1}L^{-\frac{2n}{2n+1}}\right),

(106)

where
n
is the constant in Assumption
5
. The Lemma concludes by combining the bounds (
102
), (
105
) and (
106
) according to equation (
101
).
∎

Appendix J
Proof of Theorem
7

Suppose that
(X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})_{i=1}^{N},(\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})_{i=1}^{N}
are the solutions to (
17
) and (
14
)-(
16
), given the initial condition
\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}
. The first of which exists by Lemma
26
. A standard Grönwall argument using the Lipschitz continuity of
\Gamma,\mathcal{K}
and
\partial_{\mu}\ell
yields that, after modifying it on a
\mathbb{P}
-null set if necessary,
\bm{y}\mapsto(\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})
is continuous for every
\omega\in\Omega
. This can be done as
\mathcal{F}
is complete. Furthermore,
(\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable by Lemma
26
. Therefore, the assumptions on
\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t}
detailed at the start of Appendix
I
are satisfied by
(\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})
.

Recall that the continuous time extensions (
14
)-(
16
) to the hidden state and adjoint variables round
t
up and down, respectively, so that

\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}=(\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})=(\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{r_{t},\tau},\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{r_{t}+\frac{1}{L},\tau}).

In Lemma
40
, the time component is discretised by approximating
Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{t}
with
Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{r_{t}}
. However, an identical bound would occur if we rounded time up and down in half of the components each.

By Young’s inequality, we have that

\displaystyle\|a^{i,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}-\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|a^{i,\bm{y}}_{1,\tau}-\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{1,\tau}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\left\|\int_{\frac{r}{L}}^{1}\mathcal{K}(X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\rho^{N,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\nu_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})-\mathcal{K}(\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{\rho}^{N,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau},\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau})dt\right\|^{2}.

The function
\mathcal{K}
is bounded and Lipschitz as a result of Lemma
16
, Corollary
19
and Remark
27
, thus
\mathcal{K}
satisfies Assumption
7
. Recall that equation (
64
) identifies
X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
with the solution
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t,\tau}=(X^{x,\zeta}_{t,\tau},a^{x,\zeta}_{t,\tau})
to equations (
60
)-(
61
), with
\bm{\nu}
taken as
\bm{\nu}_{\tau}
,
x=y^{i}
and
\zeta
taken as the empirical measure on
y^{1},\ldots y^{N}
. Corollary
23
demonstrates that the map
(x,\zeta)\mapsto Z^{x,\zeta}_{t,\tau}
is Lipschitz, which can be extended to include Lipschitz in time by the boundedness of
\mathcal{K}
and
\Gamma
. After modifying
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t}
on a
\mathbb{P}^{0}
, if necessary, this Lipschitz estimate holds for all
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}
. Corollary
23
also proves that
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t,\tau}
is
\mathcal{F}^{0}
-measurable for fixed
(t,x,\zeta)\in[0,1]\times\mathscr{D}
. Therefore,
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t,\tau}
satisfies
4
. Furthermore, Lemmas
16
and
26
combined imply that
\Gamma
and
\mathcal{K}
are bounded by
R_{\theta}^{2}R_{X}
and
R_{a}\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}
, respectively. Hence, for any
(x,\zeta)\in\mathscr{D}

\|\partial_{t}Z^{x,\zeta}_{t,\tau}\|\leq R_{\theta}^{2}R_{X}+R_{a}\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}.

Therefore, the flow map
Z^{x,\zeta}_{t,\tau}
satisfies Assumption
5
with
n=1
.

Let
z=(x,a)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,
w=(x,a,\tilde{x},\tilde{a})\in\mathbb{R}^{4d}
. We use subscript
j
to index the
j
-th block of
d
components so that odd subscripts correspond to the hidden states
X^{i,b}_{t,\tau}
, while even subscripts correspond to the adjoint variables
a^{i,b}_{t,\tau}
. Then,
\nabla_{x}\mathcal{H}(x,\rho,\nu,a)
satisfies
6
with

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&f^{\gamma z}_{1}(z,w,\theta)=f^{\gamma z}_{3}(z,w,\theta)=\beta\langle\theta_{Q}z_{1},\theta_{K}(w_{1}+w_{3})\rangle,\\
&f^{\gamma z}_{2}(z,w,\theta)=\beta(\theta_{Q})^{T}\theta_{K}\,(w_{1}-w_{3})\langle\theta_{V}w_{1},\theta_{O}z_{2}\rangle.\end{cases}

(107)

These functions
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}
are clearly jointly continuous in each variable and satisfy the boundedness requirement of Assumption
6
.
These choices of
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}
ensure that the corresponding
J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}
, defined in
32
, are linear in each variable or linear in
ww^{T}
. Therefore, each
J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}
satisfies Assumption
8
, where the associated
\widetilde{J}
will be bounded by a constant that only depends on
R_{\theta},\beta
due to Remark
27
. See Section
J.1
for further details.

The second term in
\mathcal{K}
takes the form

\int\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{H}(y,\rho|_{x},\nu,p)(x)d\rho(y,p)=\iint\frac{\exp(f^{\gamma\mu}_{1}((x,a),w,\theta))\,f^{\gamma\mu}_{2}((x,a),w,\theta)}{\int\exp(f^{\gamma\mu}_{3}(w,\tilde{w},\theta))d\rho^{\otimes 2}(\tilde{w})}d\rho^{\otimes 2}(w)d\nu(\theta),

where

\displaystyle\begin{cases}f_{1}^{\gamma\mu}(z,w,\theta)&=\beta\langle\theta_{Q}w_{1},\theta_{K}(z_{1}+w_{3})\rangle,\\
f_{2}^{\gamma\mu}(z,w,\theta)&=\beta(\theta_{K})^{T}\theta_{Q}w_{1}\langle\theta_{V}(z_{1}-w_{3}),\theta_{O}w_{2}\rangle+\theta_{V}^{T}\theta_{O}w_{2},\\
f_{3}^{\gamma\mu}(w,\tilde{w},\theta)&=\beta\langle\theta_{Q}w_{1},\theta_{K}(\tilde{w}_{1}+\tilde{w}_{3})\rangle.\end{cases}

(108)

These choices of
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}
only satisfy the continuity and boundedness requirement for Assumption
6
.
Nevertheless, as noted in Remark
39
, the conclusion of Lemma
33
still holds for the second term of
\mathcal{K}
.
Furthermore, there does not exist a singular
\widetilde{J}
such that
J_{1}^{\gamma\mu}
or
J^{\gamma\mu}_{2}
satisfy Assumption
8
, where these functions correspond to
f_{1}^{\gamma\mu}
and
f_{2}^{\gamma\mu}
according to definition (
32
). However, as shown in Section
J.1
,
J_{1}^{\gamma\mu},J^{\gamma\mu}_{2}
and
J_{3}^{\gamma\mu}
can be expressed as a sum of at most three terms of the form of
\widetilde{J}
in Assumption
8
. As a result, Remark
37
states that Lemma
36
still applies for the given
J^{\gamma\mu}_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
. Since these are the only instances in which these assumptions are used, the conclusion of Lemma
38
remains valid. These Assumptions
6
and
8
are only required in Lemma
40
so that Lemma
38
can be applied.

Accordingly, Lemma
40
can be applied to both terms of
\mathcal{K}
, and so, combining them by Young’s inequality, we deduce that there exists a constant
C_{6}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\max_{r\in[l:L]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}\|a^{i,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}-\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}\|^{2}\bigg]

(109)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}\|a^{i,\bm{y}}_{1,\tau}-\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{1,\tau}\|^{2}\bigg]+\frac{C_{6}}{L}\sum_{r^{\prime}=l}^{L-1}\mathscr{W}_{r^{\prime},\tau}+C_{6}\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{HL^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}\mathscr{L}_{j}\right),

where
\mathscr{L}_{j}
is defined in (
100
) and
\mathscr{W}_{l,\tau}
is defined by

\mathscr{W}_{l,\tau}=\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{r\in[l:L-1]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}\|X^{i,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}-\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}\|^{2}+\|a^{i,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r+1}{L},\tau}-\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r+1}{L},\tau}\|^{2}\right].

(110)

Here, we use the convention
\sum_{r^{\prime}=L}^{L-1}\mathscr{W}_{r^{\prime},\tau}=0
. Note that the time steps on the hidden state and adjoint variables have been rounded up and down, respectively, as discussed at the start of this section.

The argument for the difference in hidden states follows similarly. Since
X^{i,\bm{y}}_{0,\tau}=\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{0,\tau}
, for every
r\in[0:L-1]

\|X^{i,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r+1}{L},\tau}-\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r+1}{L},\tau}\|^{2}=\left\|\int_{0}^{\frac{r+1}{L}}\Gamma(X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\mu^{N,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\nu_{t,\tau})-\Gamma(\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{\mu}^{N,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{t,\tau})dt\right\|^{2}.

By Lemmas
16
,
17
and Remark
27
, the velocity field
\Gamma
is Lipschitz and bounded and so satisfies Assumption
7
. Furthermore,
\Gamma
satisfies Assumption
6
with

\displaystyle f_{1}^{\gamma}(z,w,\theta)

\displaystyle=f_{3}(z,w,\theta)=\beta\langle\theta_{Q}z_{1},\theta_{K}w_{1}\rangle,

\displaystyle f_{2}^{\gamma}(z,w,\theta)

\displaystyle=(\theta_{O})^{T}\theta_{V}w_{1},

which are all linear in
z,w
. Hence, the associated functions
J^{\gamma}_{1},J^{\gamma}_{2},J^{\gamma}_{3}
, as defined in
32
, satisfy Assumption
8
for some
\widetilde{J}(\theta)
that is bounded in terms of
\beta
,
R_{\theta}
, as a result of Remark
27
.
Therefore, Lemma
40
applies with
Z^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{Z}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
taken as
X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
and
\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
, respectively, to yield that there exists a constant
C_{7}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[1:N]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{r\in[0:l+1]}\|X^{i,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}-\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}\|^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{7}}{L}\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{l}\mathbb{E}^{1}\left[\max_{r\in[0:r^{\prime}]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{i\in[1:N]}\|X^{i,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}-\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}\|^{2}\right]+C_{7}\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{HL^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}\mathscr{L}_{j}\right).

By applying the discrete Grönwall’s Lemma, we deduce that

\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\max_{i\in[1:N]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\max_{r\in[0:L]}\|X^{i,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}-\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}\|^{2}\bigg]\leq C_{7}\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{HL^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}\mathscr{L}_{j}\right),

(111)

where we have absorbed the
\exp(C_{7})
factor from the Gröwnall Lemma back into
C_{7}
. By using the Lipschitz continuity of the terminal condition for the adjoint variable, Assumption
2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\max_{i\in[1:N]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\|a^{i,\bm{y}}_{1,\tau}-\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{1,\tau}\|^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 2\Lambda_{\ell}^{2}\;\mathbb{E}^{1}\bigg[\max_{i\in[1:N]}\sup_{\bm{y}\in(\bar{B}(R_{0}))^{N}}\|X^{i,\bm{y}}_{1,\tau}-\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{1,\tau}\|^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{7}\Lambda_{\ell}^{2}\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{HL^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}\mathscr{L}_{j}\right).

(112)

The second inequality uses equation (
111
).
Since
\mathscr{W}_{r^{\prime},\tau}
can be bounded above by the contributions from
(X,a)
separately, combining equations (
109
), (
111
) and (
J
) gives for any
l\in[1:L]

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}_{l-1,\tau}\leq\frac{C_{6}}{L}\sum_{r^{\prime}=l}^{L-1}\mathscr{W}_{r^{\prime},\tau}+(C_{6}+C_{7}(1+2\Lambda_{\ell}^{2}))\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{HL^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}\mathscr{L}_{j}\right),

where by convention
\sum_{r=L}^{L-1}\mathscr{W}_{r,\tau}=0
.
Therefore, by the discrete Gönwall inequality

\mathscr{W}_{0,\tau}\leq C_{8}\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{HL^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}\mathscr{L}_{j}\right),

(113)

for some constant
C_{8}=C_{8}(C_{6},C_{7},\Lambda_{\ell})
.
Notice that
\bar{X}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau},\bar{a}^{i,L,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}
are constant on each interval
[\frac{r}{L},\frac{r+1}{L})
. Also, the boundedness of
\Gamma
and
\mathcal{K}
, Lemma
16
, implies that

\|X^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}-X^{i,\bm{y}}_{r_{t},\tau}\|\leq\frac{R_{\theta}^{2}R_{X}}{L},\qquad\|a^{i,\bm{y}}_{t,\tau}-a^{i,\bm{y}}_{r_{t},\tau}\|\leq\frac{R_{a}\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}}{L}.

Hence, the time discretisation error between
\mathscr{L}_{j}
and
\mathscr{W}_{0,j}
is of order
\mathcal{O}(L^{-2})
, and using Young’s inequality, we get

\mathscr{L}_{\tau}\leq 2\mathscr{W}_{0,\tau}+\frac{2R_{\theta}^{4}R_{X}^{2}+2R_{a}^{2}\tilde{B}_{\mathcal{K}}^{2}}{L^{2}}.

Hence, substituting (
113
) in to the above equation and redefining
C_{8}
to absorb the
\mathcal{O}(L^{-2})
time discretisation error, we deduce that for every
\tau\in[1:T]

\mathscr{L}_{\tau}\leq C_{8}\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{HL^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\kappa^{0}_{j,\tau}\mathscr{L}_{j}\right).

(114)

Base Case:
\tau=0

The validity of Lemma
40
is restricted to
\tau\geq 1
, since it relies on Lemmas
28
and
30
, which only hold for
\tau\in[1:T]
. These Lemmas are used to control the expression

\int\|\Phi_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}[\bm{\nu}](\theta)-\Phi_{\frac{r}{L},\tau}[\bm{\bar{\nu}}^{N,L}](\theta)\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\,d\bar{\nu}^{N,L}_{\frac{r}{L},0}(\theta).

However, at
\tau=0
,
\Phi_{\frac{r}{L},0}
is the identity map, and so this expression is identically zero. As a result, the argument in the proof of Lemma
40
applies at
\tau=0
, with any term that comes from the above expression set to zero. Therefore, the same conclusion to Lemma
40
holds, except without the
\mathscr{L}_{j}
term. Applying the preceding Grönwall argument yields that (
114
) holds for
0\leq\tau\leq T
with the convention
\sum_{j=0}^{-1}\kappa_{j,0}\mathscr{L}_{j}=0
.

Notice that
\kappa^{0}_{j,T}
in (
65
) coincides with
\kappa^{\lambda}_{i,T}
from (
66
) when
\lambda=0
, so the factors
\alpha_{\tau+1,T}
reduce to one. Furthermore, since the proof of Lemma
41
does not involve division by
\lambda
, its conclusion still holds with
\kappa^{\lambda}_{i,T}
replaced by
\kappa^{0}_{i,T}
. Therefore, there exists a constant
C_{9}
that scales exponential in the total step length
\sum_{j=0}^{T}\eta_{j}
such that for every
\tau\in[1:T]

\mathscr{L}_{\tau}\leq C_{9}\left(\frac{1}{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{L^{2/3}H}\right),

(115)

holds
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-almost surely. This is precisely the conclusion to Theorem
7
.

Since the maximum of
W^{2}_{\infty}(\bar{\rho}^{N,L,b}_{\frac{r}{L},j},\rho^{N,b}_{\frac{r}{L},j})
over
